## P1 — Fix before pilot launch

- [x] **#W1-SEC-1** · P1 — MediaMirror's expired-URL refresh write is the one update in the class not scoped by `user_id`
    - **Where:** app/Services/Media/MediaMirror.php:99-101 (`mirror()`); app/Services/Media/MediaMirror.php:122-134 (`refreshedInstagramUrl()`)
    - **Affects:** `content.media_assets` rows; a mismatched (userId, assetId) pair passed to `MediaMirror::mirror()` (via `MirrorMediaAssetJob`, e.g. from a dispatch bug or an asset re-association race) would overwrite a `source_url` cross-tenant instead of failing.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `->where('user_id', $userId)` to the expired-URL refresh `UPDATE` in `mirror()`, matching the `#SEC-5` pattern already applied to every other write in this class (the video branch, the image branch, and `fail()`).
        - Have `refreshedInstagramUrl()` join through `user_id` (or verify it against the asset row) rather than correlating by `asset_id` + coord alone.
    - **Technical:** Every other write in `MediaMirror` (the video-branch UPDATE, the image-branch UPDATE, and `fail()`'s attempt-counter UPDATE) carries an explicit `#SEC-5` comment and a `->where('user_id', $userId)` predicate specifically to make a mismatched dispatch pair a no-op instead of a cross-tenant write. The expired-Instagram-URL refresh path — added most recently per its own "R3 pre-flight (2026-08-27)" comment — updates `content.media_assets` by `id` alone: `->where('id', $assetId)->update(['source_url' => $fresh])`. `MirrorMediaAssetJob::handle()` always passes `$this->userId`/`$this->assetId` from the same dispatch call, so today's exploit path requires an upstream dispatch or race bug rather than attacker-controlled input — but that is exactly the risk the class's own `#SEC-5` doctrine exists to close, and this one path breaks the invariant.
    - **Plain English:** Every other place this code updates a photo record double-checks that the photo belongs to the account making the change — like checking both the coat-check ticket number AND the name on it. One newly-added step (refreshing an expired Instagram link) only checks the ticket number. If a bug elsewhere ever hands this code the wrong pairing, it could silently overwrite a different customer's photo record instead of safely doing nothing.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($this->instagramUrls->isExpired($sourceUrl)) {
            $fresh = $this->refreshedInstagramUrl($userId, $assetId, $sourceUrl);
            if ($fresh === null) {
                return $this->fail($assetId, 'source_expired', $sourceUrl, userId: $userId);
            }
            DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.media_assets')
                ->where('id', $assetId)
                ->update(['source_url' => $fresh]);
            $sourceUrl = $fresh;
        }
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): FIXED, and the prescription was incomplete. Scoping the UPDATE by user_id is half of it; the other half is the class's own doctrine at :250-266 — a zero-row UPDATE must not be treated as success, or the job continues to stream() with a URL that was never persisted. Both are now in place, routing a miss through the existing asset_unwritable reason (fail() is itself owner-scoped, so a mismatched pair bumps nobody's counter). The READ is scoped too, through a join to content.media_assets on ma.user_id, because content.item_media has no user_id column and BOTH the primary and fallback joins correlated by asset_id alone. That ordering matters for cost as well as leakage: the read runs before the write and freshUrl() spends an embed fetch AND a billed Apify actor call, so a mismatched pair should cost nothing. The new negative test pins the read scope (Http::assertNothingSent — nothing is even fetched); a positive control covers the refresh branch end-to-end, which had no coverage at all before. The write-scope predicate is defence-in-depth with no direct test: content.media_assets.id is the PRIMARY KEY, so user_id is fixed per asset and the scoped read can never hand the write a mismatched pair. asset_unwritable is asserted in zero tests for any of the three branches — pre-existing debt across the class, not introduced here.

- [x] **#W1-SEC-2** · P1 — Menu-scan upload has no decompression/pixel-bomb guard
    - **Where:** app/Http/Requests/Platforms/ScanMenuUploadRequest.php:20-32; app/Http/Controllers/Api/Platforms/MenuController.php:146-154
    - **Affects:** Any authenticated food-business user uploading a menu photo/PDF; the OCR pipeline (Mistral) and the PHP worker processing the upload.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a header-only image dimension check (`getimagesize`) against `config('partna.image_max_pixels')` before the file is base64-encoded and sent to OCR.
        - Reuse the existing `ImagePixelBudget` helper (already used by `MediaMirror` for the same class of bug) rather than writing a new check.
    - **Technical:** `ScanMenuUploadRequest` validates content-sniffed MIME type and a byte-size cap (`max:{$maxKb}`, default 20 MB) but never checks header-declared pixel dimensions before `MenuController::scan()` reads the whole file and hands it to Mistral OCR. `MediaMirror::stream()` has an explicit comment for exactly this class of bug: "a 20000×20000 PNG of flat colour is ~90 bytes on the wire and 1.6 GB once rasterised… four orders of magnitude under the [byte] limit." This upload path has no equivalent guard, and unlike `MediaMirror` it doesn't even reach the point of local decoding — the file is base64'd and forwarded to a third-party OCR API, but the same PHP worker still reads it into memory first via `file_get_contents`.
    - **Plain English:** The upload form checks the file's size and type, but not how "big" the picture would become once unpacked. A tiny, specially crafted image file can unpack into gigabytes of data and crash the server process handling it, the same way a zip bomb crashes an unzip tool.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        'file' => [
            'required',
            'file',
            'mimetypes:image/jpeg,image/png,image/webp,application/pdf',
            "max:{$maxKb}",
        ],
        ```
        ```php
        $mime = (string) $file->getMimeType();
        $dataUri = 'data:'.$mime.';base64,'.base64_encode((string) file_get_contents($file->getPathname()));
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): FIXED, with the stated impact corrected. The finding claims a pixel bomb "can crash the server process" — nothing on this path rasterises: the bytes are base64-encoded and POSTed to Mistral OCR, and peak memory is already bounded by the existing max:20480 rule. The real cost is narrower but real: a ~33-byte PNG declaring 20000x20000 passes both existing rules and spends a BILLED OCR call on a payload that will fail or hang. Guarded in ScanMenuUploadRequest::withValidator() using the existing ImagePixelBudget::exceeds() (bytes-based, fails closed on an unreadable header). NOT getimagesize(), which is not a decode gate, and NOT safeToDecode() — its decodable() allowlist is jpeg/png/webp, so it would 422 every legitimate menu PDF; PDFs take an explicit early return. The regression assertion is Http::assertNothingSent(), i.e. the billed call never happens.

- [x] **#W1-SEC-3** · P1 — Non-admin staff can use hidden PII fields as search filters to confirm they exist, defeating the PII-hide gate
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/Staff/UserSiteManagement/StaffUserController.php index()
    - **Affects:** Professional users' email addresses and phone numbers; any non-admin staff role using the professionals search screen.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Gate the `primary_email`/`phone` ILIKE branches behind the same admin check that already gates `$showPii`, so a non-admin's search simply does not match on those columns.
        - Add a regression test asserting a non-admin `q=<email>` search returns the same result set as an unrelated query (no narrowing signal).
    - **Technical:** The search query always includes `primary_email ILIKE ?` and `phone ILIKE ?` regardless of caller role, while the response uses `$showPii = $staff && $staff->isAdmin()` to hide those columns in `StaffUserListResource` for non-admins. A non-admin can still submit `q=someone@example.com`: if exactly one row comes back, they've confirmed that email belongs to a professional even though the field itself never renders. Hiding a column in the response does not close the channel when the same value can still be used as a search predicate — this is a parameterized query (no injection risk), but a working existence oracle over data the endpoint is explicitly designed to hide from this caller.
    - **Plain English:** A staff member who isn't allowed to see customer email addresses can still type an email address into the search box. If the search narrows down to one result, they've effectively confirmed whose email that is — even though the address itself is blanked out on screen. Hiding a field on the page doesn't help if the search box still quietly accepts it.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($searchLike) {
            $query->where(function ($qq) use ($searchLike) {
                $qq->whereRaw('handle ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
                    ->orWhereRaw('display_name ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
                    ->orWhereRaw('primary_email ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
                    ->orWhereRaw('phone ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
                    ->orWhereRaw('first_name ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
                    ->orWhereRaw('last_name ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
                    ->orWhereRaw('sector ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
                    ->orWhereHas('site', function ($s) use ($searchLike) {
                        $s->whereRaw('subdomain ILIKE ?', [$searchLike]);
                    });
            });
        }
        ...
        $showPii = $staff && $staff->isAdmin();
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): FIXED. The $showPii gate is now hoisted above the query and shapes the PREDICATE as well as the response, so the primary_email/phone ILIKE legs simply do not match for a non-admin. handle, display_name, first_name, last_name, sector and the subdomain sub-query stay ungated — all are rendered to every staff tier, so none is an oracle. No Policy introduced: index() deliberately has no authorizeForUser because the target is a collection, and the house pattern for role-shaped DATA is the inline isAdmin() read (show(), StaffWorkplaceController) — this is query shaping, not an authorization abort. The regression test asserts the support actor's email search returns the SAME result set as an unrelated random string (the no-narrowing assertion the finding actually asks for; a bare "returns empty" would be weaker), with an admin positive control proving the capability is preserved for the tier that already sees the value. Verified in the APPLIED-SCHEMA lane (ILIKE has no SQLite equivalent), and the reviewer confirmed non-vacuity by reverting the gate and observing exactly the two expected failures. grep confirms no sibling endpoint has the same hide-in-response-but-search-on-it mismatch. PRODUCT NOTE: support-tier staff lose email/phone lookup entirely on this endpoint — deliberate, but flagged to the owner.

- [x] **#W1-SEC-4** · P1 — MenuAiExtractor logs raw exception messages that can carry signed hosted-media URLs
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/MenuAiExtractor.php:128-140 (`ocr()`), 187-191 (`structure()`); real hosted-URL callers app/Jobs/Platforms/GoogleMenuPhotoScanJob.php:177, app/Jobs/Platforms/WebsiteMenuPdfScanJob.php:74
    - **Affects:** Log/Nightwatch persistence of Google Places photo URLs and scanned-website document URLs, both of which can carry access-scoped query parameters.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Log `$e->getMessage()` only after stripping query strings, or log the exception class + HTTP status instead of the raw message.
        - Extend the house `PiiLogHygieneSweepTest` pattern to catch a URL-shaped substring in a log payload.
    - **Technical:** `MenuController::scan()` only ever passes a base64 `data:` URI here, so that one call site is not at risk. But `GoogleMenuPhotoScanJob::ocrImageUrl($url, ...)` and `WebsiteMenuPdfScanJob::ocrDocumentUrl($this->documentUrl, ...)` pass real hosted URLs (a Google Places photo URL, or a scraped website's PDF link) into the same `ocr()` method, and Laravel's HTTP client exception messages commonly embed the full request URL. `catch (Throwable $e) { Log::warning('menu_ai.ocr.threw', ['error' => $e->getMessage()]); }` logs that message verbatim, so a Google Places photo URL (which carries a scoped access key in its query string) can land in log aggregation.
    - **Plain English:** When the menu-photo reader hits an error, it writes the full web address of the photo into the error log — and that address can carry a temporary access key baked into it. Anyone who can read the logs could reuse that key. The fix is to log a short summary of the failure instead of the whole address.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        } catch (Throwable $e) {
            Log::warning('menu_ai.ocr.threw', ['user_id' => $userId, 'error' => $e->getMessage()]);
            return null;
        }
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): PARTIALLY STALE. The named leak is unreachable: ocr() POSTs to the constant MISTRAL_OCR_URL with the hosted URL in the request BODY, and Laravel pins http_errors=false (PendingRequest.php:268) with no ->throw() here, so the catch only ever sees ConnectionException — whose message carries the constant Mistral URI, never the photo URL. The Places key is an X-Goog-Api-Key header, not a query param. Hardened anyway (log $e::class; the full exception object still reaches Nightwatch via report(), so no diagnostic detail is lost) and fixed the REAL adjacent leak the finding walked past: WebsiteMenuPdfScanJob logged document_url VERBATIM at :76, :85, :96 and :108 — now sha1-hashed, matching the correlation handle the job already computes at :54. The value passed to ocrDocumentUrl() is untouched, so the hash is log-only and does not break identity.

- [x] **#W1-SEC-5** · P1 — DSAR export can leak a previous email owner's early-access signup history after email recycle
    - **Resolved:** Fixed 2026-08-28 as a duplicate of #W1-PRIV-1 (same method, same mechanism) — see that finding. Its prescribed interim (suppress when `created_at` predates the account by an implausible margin) was REJECTED: timestamp proximity is not ownership and would wrongly redact a genuine applicant who signs up long after joining the waitlist.
    - **Where:** app/Services/User/DataExport/DataExportPayloadBuilder.php streamEarlyAccessSignups()
    - **Affects:** Users requesting a GDPR export after acquiring an email previously used for another person's early-access signup.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add an owner key (or last-write timestamp check) to `core.early_access_signups` so a resubmission under a recycled email is distinguishable from the original signup, per the file's own "no schema-level fix yet" note.
        - Until that lands, suppress `type`/`status`/`invited_at`/`signed_up_at` on export when the row's `created_at` predates the requesting user's own account by an implausible margin.
    - **Technical:** `streamEarlyAccessSignups()` resolves the exported row solely by `where('email_lc', $emailLc)`. The class's own comment documents the mechanism precisely: `EarlyAccessService::signupFromMarketing()` upserts by `email_lc`, and once a row has progressed past `waitlist` a resubmission under a recycled email is a silent no-op that leaves the former occupant's `type`, `status`, `invited_at`, and `signed_up_at` intact. `streamEarlyAccessSignups()` then discloses those fields to the new email owner under an Article 15 request — a genuine cross-subject disclosure, explicitly flagged in-code as unresolved ("Larger leak than the streamEmailSubscriptions case; no schema-level fix yet").
    - **Plain English:** If someone's old email address gets reassigned to a new person, and that new person requests "everything you have on me," this export can hand them a previous stranger's marketing signup history — because the system only looks the record up by email address and doesn't fully overwrite the old details when a signup is retried under a recycled address. The code already flags this as a known, unfixed gap.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // Email-recycle note: EarlyAccessService::signupFromMarketing() upserts by
        // email_lc via firstOrCreate, refreshing type/workplace_or_industry/platforms
        // only while the existing row's status is still 'waitlist'. Once a row has
        // progressed to invited or signed_up, a resubmission under a recycled email
        // is a silent no-op — the row keeps the FORMER occupant's type, status,
        // invited_at, and signed_up_at, not just created_at/id. Larger leak than the
        // streamEmailSubscriptions case; no schema-level fix yet.
        ```
        ```php
        yield from $this->lazyRows(
            DB::connection('pgsql')
                ->table('core.early_access_signups')
                ->select(['id', 'email', 'type', 'workplace_or_industry', 'platforms', 'status', 'source', 'invited_at', 'signed_up_at', 'created_at', 'updated_at'])
                ->where('email_lc', $emailLc)
        );
        ```

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-6** · P2 — `public-subscribe` rate limiter stores raw email addresses in Redis keys
    - **Where:** app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php (`public-subscribe` limiter, contrast `early-access` limiter)
    - **Affects:** Newsletter subscribers' email addresses at rest in Redis, visible to `KEYS`/slowlog/monitoring/backup tooling.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Hash the email the same way the `early-access` limiter already does (`hash('sha256', $email)`) before it becomes part of the Redis key.
    - **Technical:** `early-access` hashes the email before keying (`$emailKey = hash('sha256', $email)`), but `public-subscribe` interpolates the raw address: `->by($email !== '' ? "email:{$email}" : 'no-email')`. Same file, same pattern, inconsistently applied.
    - **Plain English:** One rate limiter anonymizes the email address before using it as a lookup key; a sibling limiter right next to it writes the raw email address into the same kind of key. Anyone who can browse the cache's key names can read subscriber emails from the second one but not the first.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // early-access limiter
        $emailKey = $email !== '' ? hash('sha256', $email) : 'unknown';
        // public-subscribe limiter
        Limit::perHour((int) config('partna.throttle.public_subscribe_per_hour_email', 12))->by($email !== '' ? "email:{$email}" : 'no-email')
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-7** · P2 — `partna.http_fetch.max_redirects` config default (8) exceeds `SafeUrlFetcher`'s own documented 5-hop safe bound
    - **Where:** config/partna.php:1886; app/Services/Http/SafeUrlFetcher.php:19, 27
    - **Affects:** All SSRF-guarded outbound fetches routed through `SafeUrlFetcher` (link previews, platform connectors, design auto-grabbers).
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Set `PARTNA_HTTP_FETCH_MAX_REDIRECTS` default to `5`, matching `SafeUrlFetcher::MAX_REDIRECTS`, or clamp the config read to `min($configured, 5)` inside the constructor so config can only tighten, never widen, the bound.
    - **Technical:** `SafeUrlFetcher`'s docblock states redirects are "followed manually (max 5) and EACH hop is re-validated," and the class defines `private const MAX_REDIRECTS = 5` as its documented fallback. But the constructor reads `config('partna.http_fetch.max_redirects', self::MAX_REDIRECTS)`, and the shipped config default is `env('PARTNA_HTTP_FETCH_MAX_REDIRECTS', 8)` — silently widening the "canonical" 5-hop bound to 8 in every environment that doesn't override it. Each hop is still SSRF-re-validated, so this is not a direct bypass, but it drifts the live behavior away from the value the class's own contract advertises.
    - **Plain English:** The safe-fetch service's own documentation says it allows at most 5 redirect hops before giving up, and checks safety at every hop. The actual configuration quietly allows 8. It's not a hole by itself since every hop is still checked, but it means the real limit doesn't match what the code claims, which is exactly how limits drift wider over time without anyone noticing.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        'max_redirects' => (int) env('PARTNA_HTTP_FETCH_MAX_REDIRECTS', 8),
        ```
        ```php
        private const MAX_REDIRECTS = 5;
        ...
        $this->maxRedirects = (int) config('partna.http_fetch.max_redirects', self::MAX_REDIRECTS);
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-8** · P2 — Bot protection ships fully off by default, and the production boot guard only catches the half-flip, not "never enabled"
    - **Where:** config/partna.php:2802-2806; app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php:329-340
    - **Affects:** Public unauthenticated mutation endpoints (enquiries, leads, subscriptions, early-access, reports) in any environment that never explicitly sets `BOT_PROTECTION_MODE`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a production boot check that fails (or at minimum logs loudly) when `mode === 'off'`, not only when `mode === 'enforce'` with an absent driver.
        - Confirm production's actual configured value via `cloud environment:get production` rather than assuming the code default is overridden.
    - **Technical:** `config('partna.bot_protection.mode')` defaults to `'off'` and `driver` defaults to `'null'`. `AppServiceProvider::boot()` already has a defensive check for exactly this subsystem, but it only fires `if (mode === 'enforce' && driver absent)` — the "half-flip" case. It says nothing about the default, unconfigured case where `mode` stays `'off'` in production: that path boots silently with bot protection completely inert. The existing guard's own comment states it is "Inert on both current environments by construction — production is turnstile/shadow" — an assumption about current config, not an enforced invariant.
    - **Technical (fix scope):** this is a config/deploy-hygiene finding, not a code defect — the existing check demonstrates the team already reasons carefully about this exact subsystem's failure modes; it simply doesn't cover the "nobody ever flipped it" case.
    - **Plain English:** The anti-spam check for public forms is off by default everywhere, including production, unless someone explicitly turns it on. There's already a safety check in the code for one misconfiguration (turning it "on" without giving it a real verifier), but no check for the more basic mistake of just never turning it on at all.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        'driver' => env('BOT_PROTECTION_DRIVER', 'null'),       // null | turnstile | hcaptcha | fake
        'mode' => env('BOT_PROTECTION_MODE', 'off'),          // off | shadow | enforce
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-9** · P2 — `ApplyMenuScanRequest` leaves per-item `description`/`category` fields unbounded
    - **Where:** app/Http/Requests/Platforms/ApplyMenuScanRequest.php:29, 34
    - **Affects:** `site.menus.scan_items` JSONB storage; up to 200 items per request each carrying unbounded text fields.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `max:` rules to `items.*.description` and `items.*.category` matching realistic menu-item sizes (e.g. 1000/100 chars).
    - **Technical:** `items.*.name` is capped at 160 chars and `items.*.price` is numerically bounded, but `items.*.description` and `items.*.category` carry only `nullable|string` with no length limit, across up to 200 items per request.
    - **Plain English:** The form limits how many menu items can be submitted at once, but not how much text can go into each item's description. One upload could smuggle in far more text than any real menu description needs.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        'items.*.name' => ['required', 'string', 'max:160'],
        'items.*.description' => ['nullable', 'string'],
        'items.*.category' => ['nullable', 'string'],
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-10** · P2 — PoolResolver's public-payload joins on tenant-owned tables rely on prior item-id scoping rather than an explicit `user_id` predicate
    - **Where:** app/Site/Pools/PoolResolver.php (`reviewsSuppressedByOwner()`, `statsFor()`, the collections/storefronts join)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage pool payloads (reviews, stats badges, collection groupings) — only if a writer bug or malformed row ever links another tenant's `content.sources`/`content.collections` row to this user's item.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `where('cs.user_id', $site->user_id)` / `where('stats_src.user_id', $site->user_id)` / `where('c.user_id', $site->user_id)` to the three joins below, matching the pattern the same file already uses for `identity_candidates`.
    - **Technical:** The initial item set is owner-scoped via `content.items.where('user_id', $site->user_id)`, but downstream joins onto `content.sources`, `site.platform_connections`, and `content.collections` are filtered only by the already-scoped item IDs, not by an explicit tenant predicate of their own. Today this can only surface wrong data if a distinct upstream bug already produced a cross-tenant `source_id`/`collection_id` link — it is not independently exploitable — but the same file demonstrates the stricter pattern elsewhere, so this is a real inconsistency worth closing as defense-in-depth.
    - **Plain English:** The code checks that a visitor's item set belongs to the right site owner once, up front, then trusts every table it joins afterward to only contain that owner's data — without re-checking. That's fine as long as nothing upstream ever mislinks a record, but the same file re-checks ownership at every join in one other place, and this one doesn't.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $rows = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.source_items as si')
            ->join('content.sources as cs', 'cs.id', '=', 'si.source_id')
            ->leftJoin('site.platform_connections as pc', 'pc.id', '=', 'cs.connection_id')
            ->whereIn('si.item_id', $reviewIds)
            ->get(['si.item_id', 'pc.platform', 'pc.display_settings']);
        ```
        ```php
        $query = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.source_stats as ss')
            ->join('content.source_items as si', 'si.source_id', '=', 'ss.source_id')
            ->join('content.sources as stats_src', 'stats_src.id', '=', 'ss.source_id')
            ->leftJoin('site.platform_connections as stats_conn', 'stats_conn.id', '=', 'stats_src.connection_id')
            ->whereIn('si.item_id', array_column($selection, 'id'))
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-11** · P2 — LinkProjector treats a PCRE runtime failure on a reject pattern as "not rejected" (fail-open)
    - **Where:** app/Routing/LinkProjector.php `matches()`; reject-pattern loop in `score()`
    - **Affects:** Link-routing auto-connect decisions — a crafted or pathological pasted URL that triggers a PCRE backtrack/UTF-8 error against a detector's reject pattern would bypass that reject rule instead of being blocked by it.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - In the `reject_patterns` loop specifically, treat a `matches()` execution error (not just a `1`/`0` result) as a rejection, since a reject pattern erroring is at least as suspicious as one matching.
        - Keep the existing malformed-pattern Nightwatch report either way.
    - **Technical:** `matches()` collapses `preg_match() === false` (a runtime error, e.g. `PREG_BACKTRACK_LIMIT_ERROR`) into `return false`, indistinguishable from a normal non-match. In `score()`, `foreach ($detector['reject_patterns'] as $reject) { if ($this->matches($reject, $iri->path, ...)) { return null; } }` — a `false` return means the loop does *not* short-circuit, so a reject pattern that errors on a crafted subject is silently treated as "did not reject," and the detector proceeds to its subdomain/path matching. The class's own docblock frames the malformed-pattern report as "observability only — it cannot change a verdict," which is the correct posture for accidentally-malformed catalog patterns but also means a runtime PCRE failure on a security-relevant reject rule has no side effect beyond a log line.
    - **Plain English:** Some website addresses are deliberately excluded from being matched to a platform (a reject rule). If a pasted address is unusual enough to make the pattern-matching engine itself error out, the code currently treats that error exactly like "no match found" and lets the address through instead of blocking it, the safer choice would be.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($detector['reject_patterns'] as $i => $reject) {
            if ($this->matches($reject, $iri->path, $detectorId, "reject_patterns[{$i}]")) {
                return null;
            }
        }
        ```
        ```php
        $result = @preg_match($pattern, $subject, $m);
        if ($result === false) {
            $m = [];
            $this->reportMalformedPattern($detectorId, $field, $pattern);
            return false;
        }
        return $result === 1;
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-12** · P2 — Staff profile update / soft-delete / restore skip the fresh-AAL2 gate applied to status changes and force-destroy
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/Staff/UserSiteManagement/StaffUserController.php `update()`, `destroy()`, `restore()`
    - **Affects:** Professional account mutations (profile edits, archive, restore) under a stale or hijacked staff session that still carries a valid-but-old AAL2 claim.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add the same `requiresFreshAal2($request)` check already present in `updateStatus()`, `bulkUpdateStatus()`, `forceDestroy()`, and `releaseClaim()` to `update()`, `destroy()`, and `restore()`.
    - **Technical:** `requiresFreshAal2()` is defined once and applied consistently to status-changing and destructive/compliance actions in this controller, but `update()`, `destroy()`, and `restore()` call only `authorizeForUser($staff, 'staffManage', $professional)` — proving staff identity and role, not MFA recency. Route-level `require.aal2` middleware proves the JWT carried an `aal2` claim *at token issue*; the fresh-AAL2 gate is what proves the MFA proof is *recent*. Editing or deleting a professional's record is at least as sensitive as a status change, which already carries this gate.
    - **Plain English:** Several sensitive staff actions ask the staff member to recently re-prove their identity (like re-entering a code) before they're allowed to proceed. Editing a professional's profile, archiving their account, or restoring it can currently be done without that re-proof, even though those actions are just as sensitive.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        public function update(StaffUpdateUserRequest $request, User $professional) {
            $staff = $request->attributes->get('partna_staff');
            $this->authorizeForUser($staff, 'staffManage', $professional);
            $validated = $request->validated();
            // ... no requiresFreshAal2() call
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-13** · P2 — WebsiteLinkHarvester parses untrusted third-party HTML without `LIBXML_NONET`
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/WebsiteLinkHarvester.php:747-750
    - **Affects:** Link-harvesting from a user-connected website; hardening against a hostile page's HTML triggering parser-level network activity.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `LIBXML_NONET` to the `loadHTML()` flags: `$doc->loadHTML($html, LIBXML_NOWARNING | LIBXML_NOERROR | LIBXML_NONET)`.
    - **Technical:** The harvested HTML is fetched via `SafeUrlFetcher` (SSRF-guarded) before reaching this point, but the subsequent `DOMDocument::loadHTML()` call passes only warning-suppression flags. `LIBXML_NONET` is the standard, zero-cost hardening for parsing any HTML/XML sourced from an untrusted third party, closing off external-entity/DTD network resolution regardless of libxml's version-dependent defaults.
    - **Plain English:** When the system reads a business's website to find its links, it hands the page's raw HTML to a parser without telling that parser "don't make network requests of your own." It's a cheap, standard safety switch for handling content from a source you don't control.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $loaded = $doc->loadHTML($html, LIBXML_NOWARNING | LIBXML_NOERROR);
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-14** · P2 — GDPR export discloses another professional's internal `user_id` on cross-tenant email-subscription rows
    - **Where:** app/Services/User/DataExport/DataExportPayloadBuilder.php streamEmailSubscriptions()
    - **Affects:** Data subjects whose email appears on another professional's subscriber list; the other professional's internal ID is disclosed as a side effect.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Null out or omit `user_id` on rows selected via the cross-tenant branch (`user_id != $userId`), keeping it only on the caller's own rows.
    - **Technical:** The query's `select()` returns `user_id` for every matched row, including the explicitly-documented "cross-tenant" branch (`email_lc` matches, `user_id` belongs to someone else). That value is the other professional's internal primary key — third-party personal data not necessary to satisfy the requesting subject's own Article 15 access right. The file's own comment already flags this row shape as a "bounded leak, mostly metadata... schema-level fix is to add an owner FK; that's a follow-up," i.e. a known, still-open gap.
    - **Plain English:** When someone requests a copy of their own data, this export can include the internal ID number of a *different* business owner whose newsletter list the requester happened to be on. That ID isn't the requester's information and doesn't need to be in their download.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        ->table('notifications.email_subscriptions')
        ->select(['id', 'user_id', 'list_key', 'email', 'email_lc', 'full_name', 'status', 'subscribed_at', 'unsubscribed_at', 'consent_source', 'created_at'])
        ...
        $q2->where('email_lc', $emailLc)
            ->where(function ($q3) use ($userId) {
                $q3->whereNull('user_id')->orWhere('user_id', '!=', $userId);
            });
        ```

## P3 — Nice to have

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-15** · P3 — `MenuController::applyScan` uses `forceFill` on a column already in `Menu`'s `$fillable` allowlist
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/Platforms/MenuController.php:216-222; app/Models/Core/Site/Menu.php:70-90
    - **Affects:** Code hygiene only — `scan_items` is already `$fillable`, so this has no live mass-assignment exposure today.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Replace `$menu->forceFill([...])->save()` with `$menu->fill([...])->save()`; verified no behavior change since `scan_items` is already in `Menu::$fillable`.
    - **Technical:** `forceFill()` deliberately bypasses Eloquent's mass-assignment guard. `Menu::$fillable` already includes `scan_items`, so `fill()` would behave identically here — the `forceFill` call is unnecessary, not unsafe, but it sets a bad precedent if a future key is added to the same write without checking fillability first.
    - **Plain English:** This code uses a "skip the safety check" method to update a field that's already on the safe list, so removing the bypass changes nothing today but keeps the pattern from becoming a real risk if the code changes later.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $menu->forceFill([
            'scan_items' => [
                'items' => array_slice([...$kept, ...$items], 0, 400),
                'source' => 'upload',
                'scannedAt' => now()->toIso8601String(),
            ],
        ])->save();
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SEC-16** · P3 — `streamDesignKit` exports `site.design_kits` with no explicit column allow-list, unlike the rest of the DSAR builder
    - **Where:** app/Services/User/DataExport/DataExportPayloadBuilder.php streamDesignKit()
    - **Affects:** Future-proofing only — `site.design_kits` today holds only design tokens, nothing sensitive.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add an explicit `->select([...])` matching the current column set, so a future internal-only column added to `design_kits` doesn't automatically become exportable.
    - **Technical:** Every other row-section in this builder uses an explicit column allow-list specifically because `DB::table()` bypasses Eloquent's `$hidden`. `streamDesignKit()` is the one section with no `select()`, returning every column by default.
    - **Plain English:** Most parts of this data-export tool have a deliberate list of exactly which fields are allowed to leave the building. This one part doesn't, so if an internal-only field is ever added to the design settings table, it would automatically get included in a customer's download unless someone remembers to add a check.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        yield from $this->lazyRows(
            DB::connection('pgsql')
                ->table('site.design_kits')
                ->where('site_id', $siteId)
        );
        ```

## P1 — Fix before pilot launch

- [x] **#W1-LIFE-1** · P1 — `moderation:sla-scan` only logs SLA breaches, no Nightwatch alert path
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/Moderation/ModerationSlaScanCommand.php:35 (scheduled by routes/console.php:466-470)
    - **Affects:** Trust & safety response times — every open/triaged/under_review moderation case approaching its SLA deadline.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Replace the bare `Log::warning` with `report(new ModerationSlaBreachWarning(...))` or `$this->fail()`-equivalent so Nightwatch's exception-based alerting actually fires.
        - Keep the per-case `Log::warning` as the diagnostic breadcrumb; add one aggregated exception per scan run (not one per case) so a busy scan doesn't spam Nightwatch.
    - **Technical:** Nightwatch (per this repo's own doctrine, CLAUDE.md) alerts on exceptions and auto-detected slow jobs/routes, never on log queries. `ModerationSlaScanCommand` is the entire mechanism the platform has for catching cases about to breach SLA, and its only output on a breach is `Log::warning('moderation.sla.breach_risk', ...)` — verified directly in the command class, not just the scheduler comment. A case can sail past its SLA deadline with zero human notification. This is the exact "Log::warning used as a breadcrumb when an alert is needed" pattern the house doctrine names explicitly.
    - **Plain English:** This command is supposed to be the smoke detector for moderation cases that are about to miss their deadline. Right now it only writes a note in a file nobody reads in real time — it never actually sounds an alarm. A case can blow its deadline and no one on the team finds out until someone happens to go looking.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($atRisk as $case) {
            $minutes = now()->diffInMinutes($case->sla_due_at, false);
            Log::warning('moderation.sla.breach_risk', [
                'case_id' => $case->id,
                'severity' => $case->severity,
                'due_in_minutes' => $minutes,
            ]);
        }
        ```

- [x] **#W1-LIFE-2** · P1 — `ensureContentSource()` find-or-create races against its own unique index, surfacing as an uncaught 500
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php:379-399 (`ensureContentSource()`)
    - **Affects:** First projection of a newly connected platform; concurrent connector runs for the same connection.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Replace the SELECT-then-INSERT with `insertOrIgnore()` + re-read, matching the pattern `ensureManualSource()` already uses just below it in the same file.
        - Or wrap the insert in a typed `catch (UniqueConstraintViolationException $e)` and re-read on conflict.
    - **Technical:** Verified against `supabase/migrations/20260727140000_content_schema.sql:40`: `content.sources` carries `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX "idx_content_sources_connection" ON "content"."sources" ("connection_id")`. `ensureContentSource()` does a plain `SELECT ... WHERE connection_id = ?` then an unconditional `INSERT` with no conflict handling. Two concurrent projection runs for the same first-time connection can both observe `$existing === null` and both attempt the insert; the second hits the real unique index and throws an uncaught `UniqueConstraintViolationException` (Postgres 23505), which is not caught anywhere in this method — the whole projection run fails. This is the canonical `lockForUpdate + UNIQUE` + typed-catch gap, and the constraint that would make it throw is confirmed to exist.
    - **Plain English:** Two background jobs can both check "does this connection already have a filing folder?" at the same moment, both see no, and both try to create one. The filing cabinet only allows one folder per connection, so the second attempt is rejected with an error that crashes the whole job instead of just reusing the folder the first job made.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $existing = DB::table('content.sources')->where('connection_id', $connectionId)->value('id');
        if ($existing !== null) {
            return (string) $existing;
        }

        $id = (string) Str::uuid();
        DB::table('content.sources')->insert([
            'id' => $id,
            'user_id' => $userId,
            'kind' => 'connection',
            'connection_id' => $connectionId,
            'label' => $label,
            'priority' => 100,
            'created_at' => now(),
            'updated_at' => now(),
        ]);

        return $id;
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): FIXED, with the stated impact corrected. It is NOT an "uncaught 500" — both callers are non-HTTP: RunExecutor.php:245 wraps the call in catch (\Throwable) -> report(), downgrades the run to degraded and writes a severity=critical ingest.anomalies row (so it pages and the whole stream's projection is dropped for that run); the other caller is the ingest:project artisan command. Reachability is narrow but real: RunExecutor is serialised per source by SourceScheduler's claim, but ingest:project bypasses that claim entirely (ProjectionWriter's own comment at :226-231) and two ingest.sources sharing one connection_id are not excluded. Fixed with insertOrIgnore + re-read + a loud throw on a null re-read, matching ensureManualSource() 30 lines below. Also added the missing idx_content_sources_connection to the SQLite stand-in — without it insertOrIgnore deduplicates nothing in that lane and any assertion on it is vacuous.

- [x] **#W1-LIFE-3** · P1 — `applyIntent()`'s connection insert can still race an identical concurrent placement into an uncaught exception
    - **Where:** app/Routing/SourceReconciler.php:472-501 (`applyIntent()`)
    - **Affects:** Duplicate placements for the same `(user, surface, identifier)` racing from a scan, paste, or retried job — the whole `reconcile()` call fails instead of resolving to the already-created connection.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the `IntegrationConnection` insert in a typed `catch (UniqueConstraintViolationException $e)` inside the same transaction, falling back to a re-read of the row a concurrent writer just committed.
        - Add a regression test dispatching two identical `reconcile()` calls concurrently for the same user/surface/identifier.
    - **Technical:** The method already re-reads `IntegrationConnection` *inside* `reconcile()`'s transaction specifically to narrow the race window (see the code's own comment: "matchExisting() ran before it opened, so a concurrent writer could have landed this exact row in between") — this is a real, deliberate mitigation, not an oversight. But the re-read-then-insert sequence itself is still unprotected: two transactions can both pass the re-read as null before either commits, and the second `$connection->save()` then hits the row's unique identity constraint and throws inside the transaction, rolling back the whole reconcile and surfacing as a 500 instead of the idempotent "already connected" outcome. This is the same shape as LIFE-2 above, in a sibling file, and both are the canonical `lockForUpdate + UNIQUE` + typed-catch gap.
    - **Plain English:** Two identical "connect this account" requests arrive at almost the same instant. Both check whether the account is already connected, both see no, and both try to create the connection record. The second one collides with the first and the whole request fails with an error, instead of just recognizing "oh, this is already connected" and moving on.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $connection = IntegrationConnection::query()
            ->where('user_id', $user->id)
            ->where('surface_key', $surfaceKey)
            ->where('resource_id', $identifier)
            ->whereNull('deleted_at')
            ->first();

        if ($connection !== null) {
            return (string) $connection->id;
        }

        $connection = new IntegrationConnection([
            'user_id' => $user->id,
            'surface_key' => $surfaceKey,
            'routing_class' => $routingClass,
            'resource_id' => $identifier,
            ...
        ]);
        $connection->created_by_catalog_digest = CompiledCatalog::digest();
        $connection->save();
        ```

- [x] **#W1-LIFE-4** · P1 — `loadDesignKit()` runs an unprotected query on the public sitepage build path
    - **Where:** app/Services/PublicSite/IndividualProfilePayloadBuilder.php:484-487 (`loadDesignKit()`)
    - **Affects:** Every public sitepage build (cache miss + `WarmPublicSiteCacheJob`); a `design_kits` query blip returns a 500 to the visitor.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the `DB::connection('pgsql')->table('site.design_kits')...->first()` call in a try/catch for `QueryException`, calling `$this->resolver->markDegraded()` and returning `[]` on fault — reusing the same `markDegraded()`/`degradedCacheTtl()` machinery `pageOrder()` and `buildActions()` in this same class already use.
    - **Technical:** This class's own `pageOrder()` and `buildActions()` methods carefully wrap their DB reads and call `$this->resolver->markDegraded()` on fault, specifically so a transient DB blip serves a short-TTL degraded payload instead of a 500 (documented at length in `SitepageDataResolverService::safeQuery()`'s docblock — CCH-5/`LIFE-6`). `loadDesignKit()` sits in the exact same build path but was never wired into that machinery: its raw query has no try/catch, so a `QueryException` here propagates straight through `build()` and 500s the public sitepage — the platform's hottest read path.
    - **Plain English:** Picture a shop whose price-tag printer occasionally jams for a few seconds. Everywhere else in this code, the shop is built to say "prices temporarily unavailable, one moment" and keep serving customers. This one spot doesn't have that safety net — a jammed printer here closes the whole shop instead of just hiding the price tags for a few seconds.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $row = DB::connection('pgsql')
            ->table('site.design_kits')
            ->where('site_id', $site->id)
            ->first();
        ```

- [x] **#W1-LIFE-5** · P1 — Seven content-projection methods on the public sitepage build path bypass the resolver's own fault-tolerance machinery
    - **Where:** app/Services/PublicSite/SitepageDataResolverService.php — `getLinks()`, `getGallery()`, `getContentMedia()`, `getDesignSingletons()`, `getDocument()`, `getWorkplace()`, `getServices()`/`buildServicesData()`
    - **Affects:** All public sitepage content sections; a query fault in any one of these causes a full 500 instead of a degraded, short-TTL payload.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Introduce a `safeQuery`-style wrapper for content reads (distinct from the existing presence-probe `safeQuery`, since these return real content rather than a boolean) that catches `QueryException`, logs with `user_id`/`site_id`/probe name, calls `markDegraded()`, and returns an empty/null section.
        - Apply it to the seven methods listed above.
    - **Technical:** `SitepageDataResolverService` has extensive, well-designed fault tolerance for *presence* probes (`presentPageIds()` wraps every DB read in `safeQuery()`, with `EscalatesRepeatedFaults` escalation and a documented rationale for why a fault must not silently re-show or hide a page). But the methods that actually *build* section content — verified directly in the file: `getLinks()` runs a raw `Block::query()->...->get()->map(...)` with zero try/catch, and `getGallery()`, `getContentMedia()`, `getDesignSingletons()`, `getDocument()`, `getWorkplace()`, `buildServicesData()` are all the same shape — have no equivalent protection. A `QueryException` in any one of them propagates through `IndividualProfilePayloadBuilder::build()` and 500s the entire public sitepage on the platform's hottest read path (public sitepage resolution), exactly the failure mode the presence-probe machinery next to these methods was built to prevent.
    - **Plain English:** The website is assembled from parts — links, photos, services, contact info. If the database has a hiccup while fetching any ONE of those parts, the whole page crashes instead of loading everything else and just showing a slightly incomplete page for a few seconds. The code already has a "keep going, just note that something's degraded" safety net for other parts of the page — these seven pieces were left outside it.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $rows = Block::query()
            ->where('site_id', $site->id)
            ->where('block_group', 'links')
            ->where('is_active', true)
            ->whereNull('deleted_at')
            ->orderBy('sort_order')
            ->get()
            ->map(function (Block $block): array { ... });
        ```

## P2 — Should fix

- [x] **#W1-LIFE-6** · P2 — Early-access signup upsert retains the former occupant's PII after an email is recycled
    - **Resolved:** Closed 2026-08-28, two-part reason. (a) Its prescribed `UNIQUE` on `email_lc` ALREADY EXISTS — `early_access_signups_email_lc_unique`, baseline line 2270, and `EarlyAccessService.php:33-36` already names it as the race-safety mechanism; WONTFIX-already-satisfied. (b) Its second half (clear the prior occupant's fields on a genuine recycle) is unresolvable at the write path — nothing distinguishes a genuine recycle from the same person resubmitting — and is superseded by #W1-PRIV-1, which handles the ambiguity where the harm actually lands, at export.
    - **Where:** app/Services/User/DataExport/DataExportPayloadBuilder.php:1198-1204 (docblock above `streamEarlyAccessSignups()`)
    - **Affects:** Data subjects requesting a GDPR export under a recycled email address; accuracy of `core.early_access_signups` records.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a `UNIQUE` constraint on `core.early_access_signups.email_lc` and have `EarlyAccessService::signupFromMarketing()` handle the conflict with a typed `catch (UniqueConstraintViolationException $e)`.
        - When an existing row has progressed past `waitlist`, quarantine or clear the prior occupant's `type`/`invited_at`/`signed_up_at` on a genuine email-recycle resubmission rather than silently no-op'ing.
    - **Technical:** Category 1/11 (`lockForUpdate + UNIQUE`). The code's own docblock — read directly, not paraphrased — states `signupFromMarketing()` upserts by `email_lc` via `firstOrCreate`, refreshing fields only while status is `waitlist`; once a row reaches `invited`/`signed_up`, a resubmission under a recycled email is a silent no-op that leaves the FORMER occupant's `type`, `status`, `invited_at`, `signed_up_at` in place. A DSAR export for the new owner of that email then surfaces another person's signup state. This is a real (if currently rare, pre-launch) cross-account privacy leak, not merely a UX inconsistency — hence P2 rather than P3, but downgraded from DeepSeek's P1 since it requires the specific, currently-uncommon precondition of email reuse combined with prior signup progression.
    - **Plain English:** The waitlist signup sheet only updates a person's entry until they get invited — after that, if someone else later signs up with the same (recycled) email address, the system quietly keeps the FIRST person's invite status on the record instead of the new person's. If the new person requests their data export, they could see the previous person's signup details.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        /**
         * Email-recycle note: EarlyAccessService::signupFromMarketing() upserts by
         * email_lc via firstOrCreate, refreshing type/workplace_or_industry/platforms
         * only while the existing row's status is still 'waitlist'. Once a row has
         * progressed to invited or signed_up, a resubmission under a recycled email
         * is a silent no-op — the row keeps the FORMER occupant's type, status,
         * invited_at, and signed_up_at, not just created_at/id.
         */
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-7** · P2 — Email subscription upsert overwrites the previous occupant's data on email recycle
    - **Where:** app/Services/User/DataExport/DataExportPayloadBuilder.php:1167-1171 (docblock above `streamEmailSubscriptions()`)
    - **Affects:** Data subjects whose `notifications.email_subscriptions` record can be silently overwritten by a later signup under the same (recycled) email.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a `UNIQUE` constraint on `(list_key, email_lc)` and switch the writer to an explicit `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` column list.
        - Consider an owner FK so a subscriber row can be attributed to a specific account rather than only an email.
    - **Technical:** Category 1/11, same root pattern as LIFE-6. The code's own docblock confirms writers upsert by `(list_key, email_lc)` and overwrite row content, preserving only `id`/`created_at` from the prior occupant. No unique constraint backs this key today, so the "upsert" is a bare `updateOrCreate`-style overwrite rather than a guarded conflict resolution. Bounded impact (subscription metadata, not the full signup lifecycle LIFE-6 covers), hence P2 not P1.
    - **Plain English:** A newsletter signup record is reused, not given a fresh card, when the same email address is later used by someone else. The new person's subscription preferences silently replace the old person's, and the "created" date on the record is left over from whoever signed up first — a data-integrity and minor privacy gap that a proper database constraint would catch instead of allowing silently.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // Note on email-recycle: writers upsert by (list_key, email_lc), so when
        // an email is recycled (rare today) the row CONTENT is overwritten with
        // the new user's data; only created_at and id are preserved from the
        // prior occupant.
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-8** · P2 — `buildActions()` swallows `QueryException` without marking the resolver degraded
    - **Where:** app/Services/PublicSite/IndividualProfilePayloadBuilder.php:282-287 (`buildActions()`)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage actions list; a transient DB fault serves an empty actions section for the full primary cache TTL instead of the short degraded TTL.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Call `$this->resolver->markDegraded()` inside the `catch (QueryException $e)` block so the payload uses `degradedCacheTtl()` instead of the full `cacheTtl()`.
    - **Technical:** Verified: the catch block only logs and sets `$candidates = []`; it never calls `markDegraded()`, unlike the sibling `pageOrder()` method a few lines above in the same file, which does call it on an equivalent fault. A one-off DB blip on the action-candidates query therefore hides the user's actions list for the full ~60s primary TTL instead of the ~10s degraded TTL the machinery exists to provide.
    - **Plain English:** If the system that decides which buttons to show on a page has a brief hiccup, the page gets cached with NO buttons for a full minute instead of a few seconds — and nothing marks the page as "this was served with a problem," so it doesn't get refreshed any faster than a normal successful build would.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        try {
            $candidates = $this->candidates->forSite($pro, $site, $sections, $pools);
        } catch (QueryException $e) {
            Log::warning('sitepage.actions_candidates_failed', ['site_id' => $site->id, 'error' => $e->getMessage()]);
            $candidates = [];
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-9** · P2 — Terminal Google Business enrich failure + lock timeout can leave a connection stuck in `pending` with no reconcile path
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/GoogleBusinessEnrichJob.php:484-490 (`persist()`, terminal branch)
    - **Affects:** Users whose Google Business connect job both fails terminally AND hits lock contention on its own cleanup write; their dashboard polls `apify_status = 'pending'` indefinitely.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a scheduled consistency check that finds `apify_status = 'pending'` connection rows older than N minutes and marks them `unavailable`, so a double-failure (job failure + lock timeout on the terminal write) self-heals instead of requiring the user to notice and manually reconnect.
    - **Technical:** The code already reasons through this exact scenario in its own docblock and deliberately chooses not to release/retry on a terminal lock timeout (to avoid resurrecting an already-failed job and re-billing Apify) — this is a considered trade-off, not an oversight. But the residual gap it accepts is real: if `failed()` → `mark(..., terminal: true)` → `persist()` hits a lock timeout, the connection row is left at `apify_status = 'pending'` with no code path that will ever revisit it. This requires the job to fail terminally AND the cleanup write's lock to also time out — a rare double-failure — so this is downgraded from DeepSeek's P1 to P2, but there is currently no reconcile job that would catch a row stuck this way (category 4's "long-lived in-flight state without a stuck-for-N-hours alert path").
    - **Plain English:** If a background job that's connecting someone's Google Business listing fails AND, on its way to record that failure, can't get a brief lock on the account's data, the connection is left showing "still connecting…" forever with nothing watching for that. The user has to notice on their own and try reconnecting — there's no automatic safety net that would catch and fix a stuck one.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($terminal) {
            // Terminal: no further retry of THIS job will happen (see the
            // docblock above) — releasing here would resurrect an
            // already-failed job and re-bill Apify. Just give up; a
            // user-initiated reconnect is the only recovery path.
            return false;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-10** · P2 — `writePlatformSyncStatus()` records only a binary ok/unavailable status, discarding the vendor's actual failure reason
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/MenuFetchJob.php:340-348 (`writePlatformSyncStatus()`)
    - **Affects:** Menu refresh observability for Uber Eats/DoorDash scrapes; support/debugging when a vendor changes their API or blocks scraping.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a nullable `last_error`/`last_error_code` column to `site.menu_platform_links` and write the actual scrape failure reason (timeout, bot-block status, empty payload) verbatim when a platform scrape returns null.
    - **Technical:** Category 6 (`verbatim vendor error capture`). Confirmed directly: `writePlatformSyncStatus()` writes only `synced_at` and `status: ($menus[$platform] ?? null) !== null ? 'ok' : 'unavailable'` — no discriminating reason at all, not even a status code. At the scale of many connected menus, a vendor actor change would produce a wall of identical `unavailable` rows with no way to distinguish a bot-block from a timeout from a broken selector without re-running the scraper interactively.
    - **Plain English:** When a menu fails to update from Uber Eats or DoorDash, the only record kept is "didn't work" — not WHY it didn't work (timed out? blocked? empty response?). If the same problem hits many restaurants at once, there's no way to tell from the data alone whether it's one big vendor outage or many unrelated small failures.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($storeLinks as $platform => $link) {
            MenuPlatformLink::updateOrCreate(
                ['menu_id' => $menu->id, 'platform' => $platform],
                ['synced_at' => $now, 'status' => ($menus[$platform] ?? null) !== null ? 'ok' : 'unavailable'],
            );
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-11** · P2 — A forced "Refresh menu" click can be silently dropped by the job's own uniqueness key
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/MenuFetchJob.php:84-94 (`uniqueId()`)
    - **Affects:** Users who click the manual "Refresh menu" button while an unforced auto-refresh is already queued for their account.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Include `$force` in `uniqueId()`, or give a forced dispatch its own uniqueness slot so it cannot collide with a queued unforced run.
    - **Technical:** Category 2. Verified: the constructor takes `bool $force = false`, but `uniqueId()` returns only `$this->userId`. Laravel's `ShouldBeUnique` middleware silently drops any dispatch whose `uniqueId()` collides with an already-queued job — it does not throw or notify. If an auto-triggered `MenuFetchJob(force: false)` is already queued for a user (dispatched, per this job's own docblock, "on every online-ordering change"), a manual `MenuFetchJob(force: true)` dispatched within the same `uniqueFor` (1800s) window is dropped with no error surfaced to the user — the "Refresh menu" button silently does nothing.
    - **Plain English:** If the system already has a routine background menu-check scheduled for someone, and that person clicks "Refresh menu" themselves, their click gets silently ignored because the system thinks it's a duplicate of the already-scheduled check — even though the person specifically asked for an immediate refresh. No error, just nothing happens.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        public function __construct(
            public readonly string $userId,
            public readonly bool $force = false,
        ) {
            $this->onQueue(config('partna.queues.scraping', 'scraping'));
        }

        public function uniqueId(): string
        {
            return $this->userId;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-12** · P2 — `SweepPreviousWebsiteCardsJob` retires link cards without busting the public sitepage cache
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/SweepPreviousWebsiteCardsJob.php:84-89 + app/Services/Content/ManualPoolWriter.php:100-108 (`markRemoved()`)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepages served from Cloudflare edge after the sweep removes scrape-seeded link cards that duplicate the owner's own (now-known) previous website.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Route this write through `App\Site\Documents\SiteCacheLanes::bust()` (the shared three-lane invalidation seam this codebase's own conventions require for every owner-visible pool mutation), instead of calling `ManualPoolWriter::markRemoved()` directly.
    - **Technical:** Category 8. Confirmed: `markRemoved()` is a bare `DB::table('content.items')->update(...)` query-builder write with no Eloquent event and no cache call anywhere in it, and `SweepPreviousWebsiteCardsJob::handle()` calls only `$writer->markRemoved(...)` in its loop — no `touchSite()`, no `SiteCacheLanes::bust()`. Per this repo's own documented invariant (CLAUDE.md: "Cache invalidation is a THREE-lane contract on any owner-initiated pool mutation... Lane 2 is the one people forget"), a swept card can remain visible on the public sitepage until the payload's TTL naturally expires.
    - **Plain English:** This job removes duplicate link cards from a user's page (cards that scraped their OLD website before the system knew it was their own site). But it never tells the public-facing website to refresh, so visitors can keep seeing the removed card for a while after it's supposedly gone.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $writer = app(ManualMenuWriter::class);
        $swept = 0;
        foreach ($rows as $row) {
            if ($this->hostOf((string) $row->url) === $host) {
                $writer->markRemoved((string) $row->id);
                $swept++;
            }
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-13** · P2 — Instagram single-post Apify actor call has no version/build pin
    - **Where:** app/Services/Media/InstagramMediaUrl.php:163-169 (`fromSinglePostScrape()`)
    - **Affects:** Instagram media-mirror refresh reliability; an unannounced Apify actor update can silently break every refresh.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add an env-backed actor build/version parameter and pass it explicitly in the Apify call.
    - **Technical:** Category 6 (`Vendor API version pinning`). Confirmed verbatim: the call targets `apify~instagram-scraper/run-sync-get-dataset-items` with no build or version qualifier. Apify actors are mutable and can be republished by their owner at any time; an output-shape change here degrades every mirrored Instagram video/image to poster-only with no version-change signal to distinguish it from a transient failure.
    - **Plain English:** This code relies on a third-party tool that can change its behavior at any time without warning, and the code never says "give me exactly the version I tested" — it just asks for "whatever's current." If that tool changes, everything using it can quietly break with no record of what changed or when.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $resp = Http::timeout(90)
            ->post('https://api.apify.com/v2/acts/apify~instagram-scraper/run-sync-get-dataset-items?token='.$token.'&timeout=80', [
                'directUrls' => ["https://www.instagram.com/p/{$shortCode}/"],
                'resultsType' => 'posts',
                'resultsLimit' => 1,
            ]);
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-14** · P2 — Instagram actor refresh failures are logged at `info`, invisible to any sustained-outage signal
    - **Where:** app/Services/Media/InstagramMediaUrl.php:163-174 (`fromSinglePostScrape()`)
    - **Affects:** Instagram media freshness; a sustained refresh-actor outage degrades silently to poster-only with no operational visibility.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Log actor-call failures at `warning`, and route sustained failures through the same `EscalatesRepeatedFaults` pattern `MediaMirror::fail()` (in the same subsystem) already uses for `store_failed`/`gave_up`.
    - **Technical:** Category 6/10. Confirmed: both the thrown-exception branch (`Log::info('media_mirror.refresh_actor_failed', ...)`) and the non-2xx-response branch return `null` with no logging at all for the latter. `MediaMirror`, the direct caller of this class, already has a mature escalation pattern (`self::escalateIfSustained(...)`) for exactly this kind of "quiet degradation vs. real outage" distinction — this refresh leg was not wired into it, so a token expiry or actor-schema change here is invisible until a human notices stale Instagram media.
    - **Plain English:** If the tool this code uses to refresh expired Instagram photos/videos starts failing, the failure is written to a log file at the quietest level — the same level used for routine informational notes — instead of something that would actually get someone's attention. A sustained outage here just makes content quietly degrade with no alarm.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        } catch (\Throwable $e) {
            Log::info('media_mirror.refresh_actor_failed', ['short_code' => $shortCode, 'error' => $e->getMessage()]);

            return null;
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-15** · P2 — `WorkplaceObserver::mirrorIdentityFields()` performs an unlocked read-modify-write on the user row
    - **Where:** app/Observers/Core/WorkplaceObserver.php:98-140 (`mirrorIdentityFields()`)
    - **Affects:** Users with `workplace_brand_is_site_identity` (Business accounts) — public contact/bio/location fields on `core.users` can lose an update when two workplace saves race.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the read-compare-write in `DB::transaction()` with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the user row before comparing/mutating fields.
    - **Technical:** Category 2. Confirmed: `$user = $workplace->site?->user`, then per-field compare-and-set, then a single `$user->save()` — no row lock anywhere in the sequence. Two concurrent Workplace saves that each mirror different fields (plausible during an import or two rapid dashboard edits) can both read the same starting state and the second `save()` silently drops the first request's field-level changes.
    - **Plain English:** If a business owner edits two different fields on their profile in quick succession (or an import runs at the same time as a manual edit), the system can read the "before" state twice and the second save can overwrite the first save's change — even though they touched different fields — because nothing locks the record while it's being read and written.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($user->{$to} !== $workplace->{$from}) {
            $user->{$to} = $workplace->{$from};
            $dirty = true;
        }
        }
        if ($dirty) {
            $user->save();
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-16** · P2 — `WorkplaceObserver`'s after-commit side effects (mirror/sweep/scan) have no retry or reconcile path on dispatch failure
    - **Where:** app/Observers/Core/WorkplaceObserver.php:133-139, 163-169, 181-187 (catch blocks in `mirrorIdentityFields()`, `dispatchCardSweep()`, `dispatchContentScan()`)
    - **Affects:** Users editing a workplace during a brief Redis/queue outage — the workplace save commits successfully, but the mirror/sweep/scan side effects it depends on are silently lost.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a scheduled reconcile job that finds recently-changed workplaces missing an expected downstream effect (e.g. a `previous_website` set with no corresponding sweep having run) and re-dispatches it.
    - **Technical:** Category 4 (transactional-outbox / reconcile gap). Confirmed: `$afterCommit = true` on this observer means the workplace write is already durable when these dispatches run; each catch block only `report($e)` + `Log::warning`, with no retry and no record of the missed intent. A queue-dispatch failure here (e.g. a brief Redis blip) leaves the DB claiming the save succeeded while its dependent side effects (identity mirror, previous-website card sweep, content scan) simply never happen, with nothing that will ever revisit it short of another unrelated save touching the same fields.
    - **Technical note on tier:** downgraded from DeepSeek's implied severity because each of the three dispatches independently fails closed (a lost mirror leaves stale-but-correct-shaped data; a lost sweep is caught by LIFE-12's cache staleness at worst) — this is a genuine gap but not a crash or security issue.
    - **Plain English:** Saving a workplace kicks off several background tasks. If the queue that carries those tasks briefly hiccups, the save still succeeds and a note gets written to a log file — but the background tasks are never tried again. The database looks like everything worked, but some of the follow-up work silently never happened.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        } catch (\Throwable $e) {
            report($e);
            Log::warning('WorkplaceObserver identity-mirror failed', [
                'site_id' => $workplace->site_id,
                'message' => $e->getMessage(),
            ]);
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-17** · P2 — `SourceReconciler::applyIntent()`'s exclusive-primary flag is a read-then-write with no lock
    - **Where:** app/Routing/SourceReconciler.php:503-515 (`applyIntent()`, exclusive-class primary assignment)
    - **Affects:** Auto-connect flows in exclusive routing classes (booking, reservations, ordering) — two concurrent first-connects in the same class can both end up flagged `is_primary`.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Protect the `is_primary` check-and-set with a row lock (`lockForUpdate`) on the connections in that routing class, or add a partial unique index on `(user_id, routing_class) WHERE is_primary AND deleted_at IS NULL`.
    - **Technical:** Category 2. Confirmed: after inserting the new connection, the code does `IntegrationConnection::query()->where(...)->where('is_primary', true)->exists()` and conditionally sets `is_primary = true` on the new row — a plain SELECT with no `FOR UPDATE`, inside the surrounding transaction but not serialized against a concurrent transaction doing the same check. Two simultaneous first-connects within the same exclusive class can both observe no existing primary and both claim it, resulting in two "primary" CTAs.
    - **Plain English:** When someone connects their first booking platform, the system checks "is there already a primary booking button?" If two connect attempts happen at almost the same moment, both can see "no primary yet" and both claim to be the primary one — leaving two platforms both marked as the main booking button.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($this->isExclusiveAuto($routingClass)) {
            $hasPrimary = IntegrationConnection::query()
                ->where('user_id', $user->id)
                ->where('routing_class', $routingClass)
                ->where('is_primary', true)
                ->whereNull('deleted_at')
                ->exists();

            if (! $hasPrimary) {
                $connection->forceFill(['is_primary' => true])->save();
            }
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-18** · P2 — Fresha employee-menu fetch runs a synchronous, multi-second vendor call inside the request cycle
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/FreshaScraper.php:331-408 (`fetchEmployeeServices()`)
    - **Affects:** The team-picker "save selection" endpoint; user-facing latency and PHP-FPM worker occupancy when Fresha's GraphQL endpoint is slow.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Move the employee-menu fetch to a queued job with a cached/polled result, or reduce the synchronous ceiling well below the current combined budget.
    - **Technical:** Category 6 (synchronous vendor calls in the request cycle). Confirmed: the method issues `Http::withHeaders(...)->timeout($timeout)->post(self::GRAPHQL_URL, $payload)` synchronously, with the code's own comment stating the prior worst case was "the 20s budget PLUS this leg's flat 12s." At scale, a slow Fresha response here ties up a request-handling worker for up to that combined window; the fix already applied (R8: surfacing failures to Nightwatch instead of silently degrading) addresses visibility, not the underlying synchronous-call cost.
    - **Plain English:** When a business owner picks which team member's services to show, this code makes a live, synchronous call out to Fresha's servers and waits for it — up to several seconds — before the page can respond. If Fresha is slow, the person clicking "save" is stuck waiting, and the server resource handling their request is tied up the whole time.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $timeout = $remaining === null
            ? self::GRAPHQL_TIMEOUT_SECONDS
            : (int) max(1, ceil(min(self::GRAPHQL_TIMEOUT_SECONDS, $remaining)));

        $response = Http::withHeaders([...])->timeout($timeout)->post(self::GRAPHQL_URL, $payload);
        ```

    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/GoogleBusinessAutoSync.php:825-833 (`seedSocials()`)

        - Wrap the `has()`/`write()` span for each social in the same platform-seed lock (`withPlatformSeedLock` or a dedicated `withBookingXorLock`-style wrapper) the booking, reservation, and ordering seed paths in this same class already use.
    - **Technical:** Category 2. Confirmed by direct comparison within the same file: `seedBooking`/`seedReservation` explicitly run inside `withBookingXorLock`/`withReservationsXorLock`, and ordering uses `withPlatformSeedLock` — `seedSocials()` alone does a bare `if ($this->has($userId, $platform)) {...} $this->write(...)` with no lock. Two concurrent auto-syncs (e.g. a scheduled refresh racing a manual reconnect) can both observe no existing Facebook/TikTok/X/LinkedIn connection and both write, producing a duplicate or a lost update.
    - **Plain English:** Every other type of platform connection in this file (booking links, reservation links, ordering links) is protected so two simultaneous sync attempts can't step on each other. Social media links (Facebook, TikTok, etc.) were left out of that protection.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($this->has($userId, $platform)) {
            $findings[] = $this->conflictFinding($platform, $platform, 'social', $label, $url, [
                'remove' => [$platform], 'write' => $write,
            ]);

            continue;
        }
        $this->write($userId, $platform, $platform, $payload);
        ```
