- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-21** · P2 — `MenuScanApplier::apply()` skips cache invalidation if a dish write fails mid-batch
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/MenuScanApplier.php:144-343 (`apply()`, invalidation at 337-343)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage menu freshness — some scanned dishes can commit to the database while the public cache is never told to refresh.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the per-item write loop and the trailing cache invalidation in a `try/finally` so invalidation runs even when an individual item write throws partway through the batch.
    - **Technical:** Category 5/8. Confirmed: the method's own comment states there is deliberately "NO surrounding transaction" (each `writeManualItem()` call commits independently), and the two cache-invalidation calls (`touchSite()` + `writer->invalidate()`) sit only after the entire per-item loop completes. If item N of a multi-item scan throws, items 1..N-1 are already committed to the database but neither invalidation call runs, leaving the public sitepage serving a stale menu until an unrelated cache-touching action occurs.
    - **Plain English:** When a scanned menu is applied dish-by-dish, each dish is saved independently. If dish 4 of 10 fails partway through, dishes 1–3 are already saved to the database — but the public page's cache is never told to refresh, so visitors keep seeing the old menu even though part of the new one is already in the database.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($updated > 0 || $added > 0) {
            $this->invalidator->touchSite(fn () => $user->site, 'menu-scan-apply', ['user_id' => $userId]);

            $siteId = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('site.sites')->where('user_id', $userId)->value('id');
            if ($siteId !== null) {
                $this->writer->invalidate([(string) $siteId]);
            }
        }
        ```

Notably, `MenuFetchJob::handle()` (in the same domain) already uses `Menu::updateOrCreate(['user_id' => $this->userId], [...])` for the same table — this method is the odd one out.
    - **Plain English:** Two menu scans can start for the same brand-new user at almost the same time. Both check "does this person already have a menu record?", both see no, and both rush to create one — a race that a sibling method elsewhere in this codebase already avoids by using a proper upsert.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php

        if ($menu !== null) {
            $menu->forceFill(['last_fetched_at' => now()])->save();

        return Menu::create([

            ...
        ]);
        ```

    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/MenuApifyScraper.php:277-303 (`fetchHttpStores()`)

Confirmed directly: `attemptScrape()` (the Apify actor path, a few hundred lines below in the same file) wraps its HTTP call in try/catch and returns a structured `['menu' => null, 'retryable' => ..., ...]` result on failure; `fetchHttpStores()` calls `$driver->fetchMenu($url)` with no equivalent guard. An uncaught exception here (a `ConnectionException` from `MenuHttpDriver`) propagates out of the whole scrape, failing the job and preventing any OTHER connected platform in the same run from being processed.
    - **Plain English:** The direct connection to Square's menu system has no safety net that the older Apify-based connections have. If Square's site has a network hiccup, the entire menu-refresh job crashes — taking down the refresh for every other connected platform too, not just Square.

        ```php
            Cache::forget($blockedKey);

        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-24** · P2 — `SquareMenuDriver::fetchProducts()` returns a partial catalog indistinguishably from a complete one on mid-pagination failure
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/SquareMenuDriver.php:219-247 (`fetchProducts()`)
    - **Affects:** Menu freshness for Square Online stores; a store's own multi-page catalog can be silently truncated in the projected menu.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Return a typed/flagged result distinguishing "complete" from "partial" so callers (`MenuFetchJob`'s absent-dish retirement logic) can avoid retiring dishes that live only on unfetched pages.
    - **Technical:** Category 5. Confirmed: the code's own comment explicitly documents the trade-off ("A transient miss on page 1 fails the whole fetch... a miss mid-pagination keeps what arrived rather than losing the store over one page") — a deliberate, reasoned choice, not an oversight. But the consequence is real: the caller has no way to tell a genuinely complete scrape from a page-2-onward failure, so `MenuFetchJob`'s dish-retirement pass (which already has bespoke handling to spare dishes exclusive to a *fully*-failed platform) cannot apply the same protection to a *partially*-failed one — a dish that only appeared on an unfetched page can be incorrectly retired as "no longer listed."
    - **Plain English:** When fetching a Square store's full product list across several pages, if page 2 of 3 fails to load, the system just keeps what it got from pages 1 and 3 and treats that as the complete list — with no note that anything's missing. That means dishes that were only ever listed on the missing page can get mistakenly marked as removed from the menu.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($resp === null) {
            // A transient miss on page 1 fails the whole fetch (the caller
            // ghosts the platform and retirement is deferred); a miss
            // mid-pagination keeps what arrived rather than losing the
            // store over one page.
            return $products === [] ? null : $products;
        }
        ```

Nightwatch alerts on exceptions and log-level `warning`+ signals, not `info`.

## P3 — Nice to have

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-27** · P3 — GoogleBusinessEnrichJob's paid-Apify idempotency markers live only in cache, by explicit accepted-risk design
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/GoogleBusinessEnrichJob.php:60-73, 173-197 (`APIFY_INFLIGHT_TTL`/`APIFY_RESULT_TTL`, `handle()`)
    - **Affects:** Apify scraping budget; a duplicate paid actor run is possible if the cache store is flushed/evicted between attempts, or under rare double-worker contention.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h) — documentation/monitoring only; a durable fix is out of scope per the code's own note.
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a metric/log on `apify_orphaned_run` occurrences so the accepted residual risk is at least observable in aggregate; no schema change is proposed here since the code already explains why one isn't straightforward (`apify_status`'s CHECK constraint has no slot for an in-progress state).
    - **Technical:** Category 1/2. The code explicitly documents and accepts this risk in its own comments: "Residual assumption (accepted, not a defect): these markers live ONLY in cache. If the cache store is flushed or LRU-evicts them between attempts, the markers vanish and the next retry re-bills Apify... They can't be backstopped in the DB instead — apify_status's CHECK constraint... only allows 'pending'|'ok'|'unavailable'; a 'processing' value is illegal." The `Cache::get()`-then-`Cache::put()` sequence that sets the inflight marker is also not atomic, but is substantially protected by the job's own `ShouldBeUnique` lock (`uniqueFor = 900`). Given this is already a deliberate, reasoned, documented trade-off with a bounded cost (a duplicate paid scrape, not data loss or a crash), this is downgraded well below DeepSeek's P1/P2 split into a single P3 observability item.
    - **Plain English:** This job pays a third party to scrape a business listing, and it keeps a note in a fast-but-temporary memory cache saying "already paid for this." If that memory cache gets wiped between attempts (rare, but possible), the job might pay twice. The developers already knew about this and decided the alternative fixes weren't worth it right now — this just suggests keeping a count of how often it actually happens.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // Residual assumption (accepted, not a defect): these markers live ONLY in cache.
        // If the cache store is flushed or LRU-evicts them between attempts, the markers
        // vanish and the next retry re-bills Apify as if it were a first attempt.
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-LIFE-30** · P3 — `SourceReconciler::recordCapBlock()` calls a lock-dependent helper outside its required transaction
    - **Where:** app/Routing/SourceReconciler.php:301-324, 419 (`recordCapBlock()` → `upsertIntent()` → `advanceLiveIntent()`)
    - **Affects:** Callers of `recordCapBlock()` (`LinkRouter.php:453,529`) — a documented race-free invariant is not actually met.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:** Wrap `recordCapBlock()`'s body in `DB::transaction()`, matching `reconcile()`'s own structure.
    - **Technical:** Category 2. Confirmed by cross-file check: `advanceLiveIntent()`'s docblock states "Race-free ONLY inside a transaction that holds that lock... never call this outside one," and `reconcile()` (the intended caller) does wrap its equivalent call in `DB::transaction()` at line 165 — but `recordCapBlock()`, called directly from `LinkRouter.php`, calls `upsertIntent()` → `advanceLiveIntent()` with no surrounding transaction at all. The code's own docblock at the call site acknowledges the current unique-live-row predicate happens to make the outcome id-stable regardless, which is why this stays P3 rather than higher — it is a structural doctrine violation, self-admittedly not currently exploitable, but fragile to future changes.
    - **Plain English:** There's a helper function labeled "only safe to call from inside a locked section of code." One of its callers uses it without that lock. It happens to still work correctly today by the shape of the data involved, but the label is being ignored, which is exactly the kind of thing that quietly breaks later when someone changes something nearby.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        public function recordCapBlock(
            User $user, string $surfaceKey, string $routingClass, string $identifier,
            string $url, string $incumbentConnectionId, string $origin = 'google_business',
        ): void {
            ...
            $this->upsertIntent(
                $user, $placement, RoutingContext::forUser($user, $origin), $iri,
                $routingClass, $identifier, Verdict::Hold, 'cap_reached', $incumbentConnectionId,
            );
        }
        ```

Confirmed: this branch already carries `reason`, `slug`, `employee_id`, and `status`, plus a distinct reason string per outcome (`exception`/`http_error`/`no_categories`/`budget_exhausted`) — genuinely good distinct-log-per-outcome discipline. The one gap is the response body itself, which would help distinguish a rotated hash/version from an unrelated HTTP error. Downgraded from DeepSeek's P2 given the substantial diagnostic detail already present.

Confirmed: `catch (Throwable $e) { // Silent — keep-alive failures aren't actionable. }` — this is an explicit, reasoned design choice (the schedule itself is the retry loop, firing every minute; a single missed ping is self-healing), so this is genuinely low severity rather than a real gap, but a bare empty catch is worth a minimal breadcrumb for the rare case where it degrades to a sustained run of misses.
    - **Plain English:** A background task that keeps the server "warm" fails completely silently if something goes wrong with it. Since it retries every single minute automatically, one missed attempt doesn't matter much — but there's currently no way to tell if it's been failing for an hour straight either.

        ```php
            try {
                $url = rtrim((string) config('app.url'), '/').'/up';
                Http::timeout(3)->retry(1, 200)->get($url);
            } catch (Throwable $e) {
                // Silent — keep-alive failures aren't actionable.
            }
        })
        ```

    - **Affects:** Menu ordering-offer attribution and reporting.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:** Add `CHECK (platform IN ('square', 'uber-eats', 'doordash') OR platform IS NULL)` in a follow-up migration.
    - **Technical:** Category 11. Confirmed: the column is documented in the migration's own comment as a "menu-registry slug ('square' | 'uber-eats' | 'doordash')" but added as bare `text NULL` with no constraint. Low severity: this column is written only by the trusted internal `MenuProjectionMapper`, not from user input, so the exposure is a code-bug-introduced typo rather than an injection/tampering risk.
    - **Plain English:** A database column that's supposed to only ever hold one of three specific platform names has no rule enforcing that — the database will accept any text at all, even though only three values are ever supposed to appear there.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```sql
        ALTER TABLE "content"."offers"
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "platform" text NULL,
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "item_url" text NULL,
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "external_ref" text NULL;
        ```

## Suggested Bundled Sessions

- **Bundle 1 — Public sitepage build resilience:** LIFE-4, LIFE-5, LIFE-8
    - **Why grouped:** All three are gaps in the same `IndividualProfilePayloadBuilder`/`SitepageDataResolverService` degraded-cache machinery, same file pair, same fix pattern (wire into existing `markDegraded()`/`safeQuery()`).
    - **Model:** Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 2 — Menu scrape idempotency & cache:** LIFE-11, LIFE-21, LIFE-22, LIFE-23
    - **Why grouped:** All touch the `MenuFetchJob`/`MenuScanApplier`/`MenuApifyScraper` menu-refresh subsystem's race-safety and failure-handling; same domain, related test fixtures.
    - **Model:** Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 3 — Vendor-failure log-level fixes (Square/Fresha/Instagram):** LIFE-25, LIFE-26, LIFE-31, LIFE-32, LIFE-14
    - **Why grouped:** Same root cause (vendor exception logged at `info`/no log instead of `warning`+escalation) across multiple platform-scraper files; each is a small, independent one-line-per-site change.
    - **Model:** Plan: Sonnet · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 4 — Missing user_id in log context:** LIFE-28, LIFE-29, LIFE-35, LIFE-36
    - **Why grouped:** Identical mechanical fix (thread an already-available `user_id`/`menu_id` into a log call) across four unrelated files; safe to batch.
    - **Model:** Plan: Sonnet · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 5 — DataExport email-recycle hardening:** LIFE-6, LIFE-7
    - **Why grouped:** Same root cause (upsert keyed on email with no unique constraint) across two adjacent sections of the same `DataExportPayloadBuilder` domain (early access + email subscriptions); a shared migration session makes sense.
    - **Model:** Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet. Escalate implement → Opus if the unique-constraint migration and conflict-handling code need to land together.

## Standalone — do NOT bundle

- **LIFE-1 — `moderation:sla-scan` no alert path:** P1, moderation/trust-and-safety-adjacent; run alone with sign-off.
- **LIFE-2 — `ensureContentSource()` race:** P1, touches a schema unique-index interaction on a shared ingest write path.
- **LIFE-3 — `applyIntent()` connection insert race:** P1, same reasoning as LIFE-2, plus routing-identity correctness.
- **LIFE-9 — GoogleBusinessEnrichJob stuck-pending:** touches paid vendor billing logic (Apify) and lock/retry semantics; standalone per the "money" rule.
- **LIFE-13 — Instagram Apify actor version pin:** touches a paid vendor integration (Apify billing) — standalone per the "money" rule.
- **LIFE-15 — WorkplaceObserver identity-mirror race:** touches `core.users` PII fields directly — standalone per the "auth/PII-adjacent" caution, even though not strictly authorization.
- **LIFE-17 — SourceReconciler exclusive-primary race:** touches routing/connection identity logic shared with LIFE-2/LIFE-3's file; run after those land, not bundled with them, to avoid one session touching the same file's transaction boundaries twice concurrently.
- **LIFE-38 — `offers.platform` CHECK constraint:** DB migration — standalone per the "any migration/schema change" rule, even though L/XL-sized effort doesn't apply here.

- app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Analytics/DevInsightsController.php
- app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php

## Progress

- P2 Medium: 0 of 2 complete

---

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W1-CACHE-1** · P2 — `pageScores()` scans the full lifetime of `analytics.section_views`/`analytics.link_clicks` with no time bound
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Analytics/DevInsightsController.php:130-146
    - **Affects:** Any professional opening `GET /api/professional/dev-insights`; Postgres read load on the append-only `analytics` schema for sites with a long or high-traffic history.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add an `occurred_at >=` lower bound (e.g. the same 30-day window `pageDailySeries()` already uses), or source the two counts from a rollup.
        - Fold the `COUNT(*)` and `SUM(duration_ms)` reads on `analytics.section_views` into one `selectRaw` pass instead of two separate full scans.
        - If usage of this endpoint grows, front it with `CacheLockService::rememberLocked` + jittered TTL, push-invalidated on analytics writes.
    - **Technical:** `pageScores()` issues three `DB::table(...)->where('site_id', $siteId)->selectRaw(...)->groupBy(...)` queries against `analytics.section_views` (twice) and `analytics.link_clicks` (once), none of which bound `occurred_at`. Every call therefore scans that site's entire event history rather than a window proportional to the request. This is category (5) hot-path heavy work: cost grows with cumulative traffic to the professional's sitepage, not with anything about the request itself, and the two `section_views` scans are redundant work that could be a single pass. The endpoint's own docblock labels it "DEV / testing... Not production-critical," which caps the blast radius (self-triggered by the site owner, not by every visitor) — that is why this sits at P2 rather than P1, not P0/P1: a site that goes viral accumulates the very rows this query scans, so the owner opening this diagnostics page during or after a traffic spike is the realistic trigger, not a routine load.
    - **Plain English:** This diagnostics page recounts every visit and click your site has ever received, every single time it's opened — like an accountant re-adding every receipt since the shop opened instead of just this month's. If your page has a big viral moment, the pile of receipts to recount keeps growing, and this internal report gets slower and more expensive to open, right when the owner is most likely to check it.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $impressions = $this->foldToPage(
            DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.section_views')
                ->where('site_id', $siteId)->whereNotNull('section_key')
                ->selectRaw('section_key, COUNT(*) as n')->groupBy('section_key')->pluck('n', 'section_key')
        );
        // Dwell (page grain only): SUM of the section-dwell annotations, in ms —
        $dwellMs = $this->foldToPage(
            DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.section_views')
                ->where('site_id', $siteId)->whereNotNull('section_key')
                ->selectRaw('section_key, COALESCE(SUM(duration_ms), 0) as n')->groupBy('section_key')->pluck('n', 'section_key')
        );
        $clicks = $this->foldToPage(
            DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.link_clicks')
                ->where('site_id', $siteId)->whereNotNull('section_key')
                ->selectRaw('section_key, COUNT(*) as n')->groupBy('section_key')->pluck('n', 'section_key')
        );
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-CACHE-2** · P2 — `itemScores()` scans the full lifetime of `analytics.item_views`/`analytics.link_clicks` with no time bound
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Analytics/DevInsightsController.php:197-220
    - **Affects:** Any professional opening `GET /api/professional/dev-insights`; `analytics.item_views`/`analytics.link_clicks` read load for accounts with many items/links and a long event history.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add the same `occurred_at >=` window used by `itemDailySeries()`, or read from a precomputed rollup instead of raw events.
        - Reuse the folded daily projection so this and `itemDailySeries()` don't each pay a separate full-history-shaped scan.
    - **Technical:** Same root cause and same fix shape as CACHE-1, in the item-grain sibling method: `analytics.item_views` is grouped by `item_type, item_id` and `analytics.link_clicks` by `product_id, section_key`, both filtered on `site_id` only with no `occurred_at` bound, then folded in a PHP loop. Cost scales with the site's all-time item-view/click volume rather than the size of the request. Kept at the same tier as CACHE-1 per the "same root cause, same tier" rule — same controller, same missing time bound, same dev-only mitigating context.
    - **Plain English:** Same issue as the page-level report, but for individual products, links and services: it never stops re-tallying every historical view and click, even though what the owner actually wants is a current snapshot.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach (DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.item_views')
            ->where('site_id', $siteId)
            ->selectRaw('item_type, item_id, MAX(item_title) as title, COUNT(*) as n')
            ->groupBy('item_type', 'item_id')->get() as $r) {
            $key = "{$r->item_type}:{$r->item_id}";
            $impressions[$key] = (int) $r->n;
            $titles[$key] = $r->title;
        }

        foreach (DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.link_clicks')
            ->where('site_id', $siteId)->whereNotNull('product_id')->whereNotNull('section_key')
            ->selectRaw('product_id, section_key, MAX(product_title) as title, COUNT(*) as n')
            ->groupBy('product_id', 'section_key')->get() as $r) {
        ```

## Progress

---

## P1 — Fix before pilot launch

- [x] **#W1-SCALE-1** · P1 — Facet-origin backfill migration runs four unbounded full-table UPDATEs with no lock/statement timeout
    - **Where:** supabase/migrations/20260826120000_facet_source_item_origin.sql:54-112
    - **Affects:** Deploy safety for `content.item_media`, `content.offers`, `content.item_tags`, `content.item_variants` whenever this migration reaches an environment where the `content` schema is live and populated (dev today; production once the deferred schema reconciliation runs — CLAUDE.md: prod is currently missing the `content` schema entirely).
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the four `UPDATE` statements in `SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s'; SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '10s';` matching the sibling migrations in this same date range (e.g. `20260823100000_unified_actions.sql`, `20260827072000_event_item_family.sql`).
        - If a table's row count makes a 10s window too tight at apply time, move the backfill to a post-deploy artisan command that batches by `chunkById`, leaving the migration to add only the nullable columns.
    - **Technical:** The four `UPDATE ... FROM content.source_items ... WHERE NOT EXISTS (...)` statements each join and scan a full content table with no `SET LOCAL lock_timeout`/`statement_timeout`, unlike every sibling migration touching these same tables in the surrounding date range (`20260823100000`, `20260827072000`). The columns themselves are safe (nullable, no default, no rewrite), but the backfill is real, unbounded work with no batching. This is exactly the operational-safety gap CLAUDE.md's migration conventions exist to prevent — a long-running migration blocks deploy, and an unbounded lock wait can stall behind an unrelated long read.
    - **Plain English:** This update touches every photo, price, tag and size-option row for every user's content in one uninterrupted sweep, with no safety timer to stop it if something else is using the table. On a small test database that's instant; once real content builds up, this step could hang the deployment.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```sql
        UPDATE "content"."item_media" m
        SET "source_item_id" = si."id"
        FROM "content"."source_items" si
        WHERE si."item_id" = m."item_id"
          AND si."source_id" = m."source_id"
          AND si."removed_at" IS NULL
          AND m."source_item_id" IS NULL
          AND NOT EXISTS (
              SELECT 1 FROM "content"."source_items" si2
              WHERE si2."item_id" = m."item_id"
                AND si2."source_id" = m."source_id"
                AND si2."removed_at" IS NULL
                AND si2."id" <> si."id"
          );
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): WONTFIX on the migration file; the lint gap it exposed is fixed forward. 20260826120000 is already applied to dev (ledger row verified) and its backfill COMPLETED — 15,307/15,514 item_media, 5,629/5,725 offers, 1,713/1,812 item_tags, 590/590 item_variants. At those row counts the four UPDATEs are milliseconds, not a stall, and on production they will match ZERO rows: prod has no content schema, so reconciliation creates these tables empty in the same run before this file reaches them. Editing an applied migration only changes fresh-apply behaviour, and the prescribed BEGIN + SET LOCAL statement_timeout wrapper would contradict CONVENTIONS.md §5 (never backfill inside a migration transaction) while capping the backfill at a hard 10s abort — the opposite of safer at the scale the finding worries about. The REAL defect is that guard Check 5's HOT_TABLES never gained the populated content.* tables when the content-pool programme landed, which is why this file passed CI. Fixed by adding CONTENT_HOT_TABLES (7 tables) on its own cutoff, and by correcting Check 5's printed remedy: for a bare-statement backfill it now advises and ACCEPTS a session-level SET lock_timeout, because SET LOCAL outside a transaction is a silent no-op — the old text steered authors toward either a §5 violation or a bound that bounds nothing.

- [x] **#W1-SCALE-2** · P1 — `PoolResolver` re-reads every `site.section_items` row ever created for a section on the public sitepage hot path
    - **Where:** app/Site/Pools/PoolResolver.php:117-131 (`hasSelection()`), 236-245 (`plan()`)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage resolution on every cache-miss/purge rebuild for any site with an active curation history (excluding items from a pool is a normal, expected lifecycle action, not an edge case).
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a pruning path for `state = 'excluded'` rows (e.g. cap retained exclusions, or convert stale exclusions older than N days to a hard delete on a scheduled sweep), OR
        - Scope the read to `whereIn('state', ['pinned', 'excluded'])` at minimum, and quantify how large `excluded` grows on a real multi-year site before pilot launch.
    - **Technical:** The method's own comment (tagged `SCALE-13`, an earlier partial fix) already documents the live gap: the column list was narrowed to `['section_id', 'item_id', 'state', 'sort_key']`, but "excluded rows have no pruning path, so a full-row select grows unbounded with a site's lifetime for no benefit." `hasSelection()` and `plan()` are both on `PoolResolver`, which `PoolWire` calls to render the public sitepage payload on every cache-miss and purge rebuild — the platform's explicitly hottest path per this lens. A site whose owner has spent a year curating pools (excluding dozens of items across each of the seven pools) pays for that entire history on every rebuild, forever, with no upper bound.
    - **Plain English:** Every time a visitor's page needs rebuilding because the cache expired, the system re-reads the complete history of every item an owner has ever hidden from every section of their page — not just what's currently showing. That history only ever grows, so the busiest, most important page load on the platform gets slower every time an owner tidies up their page.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // SCALE-13: `id`/`created_at` are read nowhere downstream, and excluded
        // rows have no pruning path, so a full-row select grows unbounded with
        // a site's lifetime for no benefit.
        $curation = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('site.section_items')
            ->where('section_id', $section->id)
            ->get(['section_id', 'item_id', 'state', 'sort_key']);
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): WONTFIX — quantified and rejected. (1) The prescribed whereIn(state, [pinned, excluded]) is a NO-OP: the CHECK constraint (supabase/migrations/20260727150000:84) permits exactly those two values. (2) The read is already indexed by idx_section_items_section (section_id, state) at :90 and column-narrowed by SCALE-13. (3) Exclusions are not unbounded — section_items_item_id_fk is ON DELETE CASCADE onto content.items (20260729150007), so an exclusion cannot outlive its item. (4) Measured on dev 2026-08-28: excluded = 3 rows TOTAL across 2 sections, vs pinned = 1662 across 126 sections (max 169 in one). The volume is pins, which are load-bearing selection + ordering and can be neither pruned nor filtered. (5) The prescribed prune is a CORRECTNESS REGRESSION: plan() computes selection = (pinned u ruleCandidates) - excluded, so deleting an excluded row makes the item eligible again and re-shows an owner-hidden item on the public page. (6) The public build never runs plan()'s query at all — PoolWire batches preloadSections()/preloadCuration() into two shared reads and injects $curation. Revisit only if a real site is observed with thousands of rows in one section.

- [x] **#W1-SCALE-3** · P1 — Public pageview ingest has no bot filter or dedup, so a viral page's bot traffic writes unbounded events to the analytics queue
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/PublicSite/AnalyticsController.php:45-74 (`pageview()`)
    - **Affects:** The `analytics` queue, Redis, and Postgres write capacity for every site during a traffic spike; the only analytics endpoint of the eight on this controller with no `isBotUserAgent()` gate.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Apply the same `isBotUserAgent()` gate `click`, `sectionSeen`, `itemSeen`, `actionSeen`, `actionTap` and `ping` already use, or introduce a pageview-specific sampling/rate policy if the bot-inclusive count is deliberately wanted for a different metric.
        - If the "record every pageview including bots" behavior is intentional for a specific metrics reason, add a per-site or per-IP burst cap before `$this->ingestor->ingest($event)` so a crawler storm on one viral page cannot starve the shared queue for every other site.
    - **Technical:** Every other beacon on this controller (`click`, `sectionSeen`, `itemSeen`, `actionSeen`, `actionTap`, `ping`) calls `isBotUserAgent()` and short-circuits before minting an event or touching Redis dedup; `pageview()` is the sole exception, by its own comment, "preserved." Each pageview — bot or not — still calls `$this->ingestor->ingest($event)`, pushing a full event onto the shared `analytics` queue. A crawler sweep or a viral spike with heavy bot/scraper traffic (a very common real-world occurrence for a page that goes viral) turns into an unbounded multiplier on the write-heaviest queue in the system, with no dedup and no per-tenant ceiling, while every sibling endpoint already has this exact protection.
    - **Plain English:** Every page visit sends a note into the same in-tray as every other site's visits, whether a real person or an automated web-crawler bot is the visitor. Every OTHER type of visit note (clicks, section views, etc.) already filters bots out before writing anything; page-visit notes deliberately don't. When one person's page goes viral, that's exactly when bot traffic explodes too, flooding everyone's shared in-tray with notes nobody asked for.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // NOTE: pageview intentionally has NO bot filter and NO dedup (preserved). A bot
        // UA still records a pageview today; changing that is a separate metrics decision.
        $event = $this->buildEvent(
            type: AnalyticsEvent::TYPE_PAGEVIEW,
            request: $request,
            site: $site,
            data: $data,
            referrer: $data['referrer'] ?? $request->headers->get('referer'),
        );

        $this->ingestor->ingest($event);
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): FIXED, but NOT as prescribed — the prescription would have reversed a documented owner decision. The code fact is live (pageview() is the one public method with no isBotUserAgent() gate), but three independent artefacts say that is DELIBERATE: two in-code notes at AnalyticsController.php:59-68, DetectsClientInfo.php:87-90 returning null rather than "bot" SPECIFICALLY to preserve pageview's row shape and naming this method as the reason, and PageviewDeviceTypeTest, which PINS that a curl/ and a facebookexternalhit UA still write a 201 + a row labelled device_type=bot. The design is LABEL, DON'T DROP: bots are recorded and separated at read time. Adding the filter would have broken two tests and silently changed the headline metric every site owner sees; a visitor-keyed dedup was rejected too, since suppressing a genuine refresh is the same metric change by another route. "Unbounded" is also overstated — throttle:analytics is 120/min per visitor IP plus a 3000/min per-true-IP backstop, and QueuedIngestor already dispatches on the 3.0s-bounded redis_request connection. What was GENUINELY missing: every one of those controls is per-IP, so a distributed crawler across many source IPs passes all of them and one tenant can consume shared analytics queue capacity. Fixed with a per-SITE burst ceiling (default 2000/min, owner-approved), applied before the pure buildEvent() so bot labelling is untouched, dropping ONLY the queue write. Over the cap the response is byte-identical 201 + visit_id, so the cap cannot be fingerprinted and no client breaks; any cache fault ingests normally (analytics fails open by design) while escalateIfSustained still pages on a sustained one. Accepted and stated: a fixed window lets up to 2x the cap through at a bucket boundary, and above the ceiling a real visitor is dropped alongside a bot.

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-4** · P2 — `event_item_family` migration runs `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` on two live analytics tables inside the same `statement_timeout = '10s'` window, scoped only to "small on the dev ref"
    - **Where:** supabase/migrations/20260827072000_event_item_family.sql:17-47
    - **Affects:** Deploy safety for `analytics.content_popularity_scores` and `analytics.item_views` once those tables carry real production write volume.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Before this migration is applied to any environment with meaningful row counts (including during the deferred prod schema reconciliation), re-verify table size and either raise the local `statement_timeout` for that one apply or split the `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` calls into a follow-up migration run in a maintenance window.
        - No code change needed today — this is a process guard for future re-application, not a live defect.
    - **Technical:** The migration's own header already states the assumption explicitly — "NOT VALID + immediate VALIDATE is safe here: both tables are small on the dev ref" — so the team has already reasoned through this exact tradeoff for the current dev-ref data size. The residual risk is that the same "NOT VALID + immediate VALIDATE in one file" pattern gets reused against a genuinely large table without re-checking the assumption, or that this exact file gets replayed once `analytics.item_views` carries real production volume during the deferred prod reconciliation (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-17-prod-schema-reconciliation.md`). Downgraded from the draft's implicit P1 because the current risk is zero (prod has no `content`/`analytics` write volume yet, `core.users = 0`) — this is a pattern-hygiene note, not an active defect.
    - **Plain English:** This deployment step double-checks two rapidly-growing visit-tracking tables all at once, promising to finish in ten seconds. The team already knew this was only safe because the test database is small, and wrote that down. The reminder here is: don't reuse this exact recipe once those tables hold real visitor data, or the ten-second promise will be broken and the deploy will fail.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```sql
        -- NOT VALID + immediate VALIDATE is safe here: both tables are small on the
        -- dev ref and every existing row already satisfies the WIDER list.
        BEGIN;
        SET LOCAL lock_timeout      = '2s';
        SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '10s';
        ...
        ALTER TABLE analytics.content_popularity_scores
            VALIDATE CONSTRAINT content_popularity_scores_content_type_check;

        ALTER TABLE analytics.item_views
            VALIDATE CONSTRAINT item_views_item_type_check;
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-5** · P2 — `ActionScorer::aggregate()` groups by a cast-expression day bucket with `COUNT(DISTINCT ...)` over a viral site's full scoring window
    - **Where:** app/Services/Analytics/ActionScorer.php:216-241
    - **Affects:** The 15-minute `analytics:compute-popularity` scoring cadence for any site with elevated `analytics.action_events` volume (a viral spike is exactly the scenario this lens targets).
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a functional index on `analytics.action_events ((occurred_at::date))` combined with `site_id`, or restructure the query to avoid grouping on a derived expression.
        - Consider a pre-aggregated per-day rollup fed by the ingest writer instead of recomputing `COUNT(DISTINCT COALESCE(session_id, visitor_id, id))` from raw rows every 15 minutes.
    - **Technical:** The query is already scoped by `site_id` and `occurred_at >= ScoringWindow::since()` — both covered by the existing `action_events_site_occurred_idx (site_id, occurred_at)` btree index (`supabase/migrations/20260726000000_baseline_pilot.sql:2603`) — so the filtering itself is index-backed. The residual cost is the `GROUP BY action_id, event, (occurred_at::date)::text` plus `COUNT(DISTINCT ...)`, which Postgres cannot satisfy from that index alone and must sort/hash after the scan. For a single site mid-viral-spike, the row count inside the scoring window can grow large enough that this per-run cost becomes material, and it recurs every 15 minutes for every active site.
    - **Plain English:** Every 15 minutes, the system recalculates how popular each button on a page is by counting unique visitors per day. The counting itself is already narrowed to the right site and the right time window, but the final "group by day, count unique people" step still has to sort through everything by hand rather than using a shortcut. For a page that suddenly goes viral, that hand-sorting step gets noticeably heavier every single time it runs.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // SCALE-3: bound the scan to ScoringWindow (see that class) — same
        // index-covered predicate as ComputeContentPopularityScores'
        // action_events read.
        DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.action_events')
            ->where('site_id', $site->id)
            ->where('occurred_at', '>=', ScoringWindow::since())
            ->selectRaw("action_id, event, {$day} as day, COUNT(DISTINCT COALESCE(session_id, visitor_id, id)) as sessions")
            ->groupByRaw("action_id, event, {$day}")
            ->get()
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-6** · P2 — `ProjectionWriter::upsertSourceItem()` issues a SELECT-then-write-then-reread per record inside the projection loop
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php:579-637
    - **Affects:** Write throughput of `ingest:dispatch`'s `RunSourceJob`s for any connector stream (Instagram, Google Business, menu scrapes) proportional to its record count.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Batch the SELECT and the `insertOrIgnore` across a chunk of records (the surrounding `lazy(500)` page is a natural batch boundary) rather than per-record, then re-read only the ids that reported a conflict.
        - Preserve the documented race-safety requirement: two concurrent writers (a scheduled run and a `PoolItemCreateController`-derived coord) can legitimately race on the same `(source_id, coord)` — any batched rewrite must keep the `insertOrIgnore` + re-read fallback for genuine conflicts, not replace it with a plain `upsert()`.
    - **Technical:** `upsertSourceItem()` runs inside the per-record `DB::transaction()` in `projectStream()`'s main loop (line 261), issuing a `SELECT` first, then either an `UPDATE` or an `insertOrIgnore` + a second `SELECT` to resolve the landed id — 2–4 round trips per record. The method's own docblock explains this shape exists because the SELECT and the write are not atomic and a double-clicked "Add" can race on the same coord, so any fix must preserve that safety property rather than naively batching. For a connector stream with hundreds of records (a menu scrape, an Instagram media backfill), this is real per-record write amplification that recurs on every scheduled `ingest:dispatch` tick for every due source.
    - **Plain English:** For every single item a connected platform sends over, the system checks if it already has a record, then either updates it or creates one and checks again to confirm it landed — one at a time, never in a batch. For a menu with 200 dishes or an Instagram backfill with hundreds of posts, that's hundreds of individual round trips to the database instead of a handful.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $existing = DB::table('content.source_items')
            ->where('source_id', $contentSourceId)
            ->where('coord', $coord)
            ->first(['id']);

        if ($existing !== null) {
            DB::table('content.source_items')->where('id', $existing->id)->update([...]);
            return (string) $existing->id;
        }
        $id = (string) Str::uuid();
        DB::table('content.source_items')->insertOrIgnore([...]);
        $landed = DB::table('content.source_items')
            ->where('source_id', $contentSourceId)
            ->where('coord', $coord)
            ->value('id');
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-7** · P2 — `MenuFetchJob::reassertSlugs()` runs one SELECT per dish after every scrape
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/MenuFetchJob.php:1009-1043
    - **Affects:** `scraping` queue worker time on every `MenuFetchJob` run for a large menu.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Batch-load current slugs for every `$namesById` id in one `whereIn` read, then only call `ensureCurrent()` for dishes whose slug actually differs.
        - Follow the pattern already applied to `ProjectionWriter::refreshItemCaches()` for the same problem (see that method's `#SCALE-9`/`#API-7` fix).
    - **Technical:** The method's own comment documents the cost directly: "one SELECT per dish here — this loop is unchanged," and explicitly notes the equivalence claim it used to make against `ProjectionWriter::refreshItemCaches()` "no longer holds" now that that method batches its slug reads per chunk — making this loop strictly more expensive per item than its sibling. A 200-dish menu refresh issues up to 200 sequential SELECTs on every scrape that moves a dish off a `-N` suffix, on the `scraping` queue.
    - **Plain English:** After a menu refresh, the system double-checks each dish's web address one at a time instead of checking the whole list at once. A restaurant with 200 dishes means 200 separate look-ups every time the menu re-syncs.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($namesById as $itemId => $name) {
            $slugs->ensureCurrent($userId, $itemId, $name);
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-8** · P2 — `MenuFetchJob::retire()` marks each removed dish with its own UPDATE
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/MenuFetchJob.php:1002-1007; app/Services/Content/ManualPoolWriter.php:100-108
    - **Affects:** `scraping` queue worker time when a vendor drops many dishes from a menu at once.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a batch variant to `ManualPoolWriter::markRemoved()` that accepts a list of item ids and issues one `UPDATE ... WHERE id IN (...)`.
        - `freeSlug()`'s per-item kind check can stay per-row inside that batch if the kind lookup is also batched via one `whereIn` SELECT first.
    - **Technical:** `retire()` loops `$itemIds` calling `$writer->markRemoved($itemId)`, and `ManualPoolWriter::markRemoved()` (verified: this is where `markRemoved` actually lives — `ManualMenuWriter` subclasses it) issues one `UPDATE content.items ... WHERE id = ?` plus a `freeSlug()` call (its own SELECT) per id. A store that drops a large chunk of its catalog in one scrape (a menu rotation, a seasonal cutover) retires dozens of dishes one row at a time.
    - **Plain English:** When a restaurant removes a batch of dishes from their online menu, the system crosses each one off the list separately instead of crossing off the whole batch in one motion.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($itemIds as $itemId) {
            $writer->markRemoved($itemId);
        }
        ```
        ```php
        public function markRemoved(string $itemId, mixed $removedAt = null): void
        {
            DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.items')
                ->where('id', $itemId)->whereNull('removed_at')
                ->update(['removed_at' => $removedAt ?? now(), 'updated_at' => now()]);
            $this->freeSlug($itemId);
        }
        ```

    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/MenuFetchJob.php:1089-1123; app/Services/Content/ManualPoolWriter.php:53-59, 74-90
    - **Affects:** First-scrape latency and write volume for a fresh, large menu.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Batch-insert new pin rows into `site.section_items` in one multi-row insert with sort keys computed in memory beforehand, reusing the same `$next` counter logic.
    - **Technical:** The loop calls `$writer->pin($site, $itemId, $next)` per new item id; `pin()` (`ManualPoolWriter::pin()`) does a `curationRow()` SELECT-or-new, then a `save()` — one INSERT per dish. A brand-new menu with hundreds of dishes performs hundreds of sequential inserts on first scrape, holding the `scraping` queue worker for longer than needed.
    - **Plain English:** When a new menu is scraped for the first time, every dish gets its position pinned on the owner's page one at a time instead of all at once.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($itemIds as $itemId) {
            if ($existing->has($itemId)) {
                continue;
            }
            $writer->pin($site, $itemId, $next);
            $next += 1.0;
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-10** · P2 — `CommerceProbeJob` fetches user-supplied URLs with no per-host rate limit or politeness middleware
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/CommerceProbeJob.php:111-273; app/Services/Platforms/GenericShopScraper.php:116
    - **Affects:** Outbound vendor politeness for any host that receives multiple probes in a short window (a shared storefront platform, several users linking the same shop).
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a per-host `RateLimited` middleware to `CommerceProbeJob`, keyed on `parse_url($this->url, PHP_URL_HOST)`.
        - Alternatively, centralize a per-host delay inside `SafeUrlFetcher` (which `GenericShopScraper` already routes through) so every scraper benefits, not just this job.
    - **Technical:** `probe()` calls `$generic->readProductPage($this->url)`, which is correctly routed through `SafeUrlFetcher` (SSRF-safe per the platform's Category B doctrine), but `CommerceProbeJob` carries no `middleware()` override at all — unlike `MenuFetchJob` and `GoogleBusinessEnrichJob`, which both apply `RateLimited('platform-connect')`. Many distinct URLs on the same host (several users linking the same third-party shop, or a burst of scans against one popular storefront platform) can hit that host concurrently with zero coordination.
    - **Plain English:** When the system checks out a link someone pasted to see if it's a shop, it has no built-in pacing for how often it knocks on the same website's door. If several users happen to link the same store around the same time, that store could see a burst of requests that looks like abuse.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $read = $generic->readProductPage($this->url);
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-11** · P2 — `LinkPreviewController::show()` fetches the same product URL twice when no product markup is found
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/Content/LinkPreviewController.php:67-102
    - **Affects:** Outbound fetch volume and preview latency for every user pasting a non-product-markup URL in the Sell pool "add link" flow.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Reuse the HTML already fetched by `readProductPage()` to build the fallback link card instead of issuing a second `snapshotOrMinimal()` fetch, or have `readProductPage()` return a prebuilt card snapshot alongside its product/store verdict.
    - **Technical:** When `intent === 'product'`, the handler calls `$this->shop->readProductPage($url)` under a `FetchBudget`; when that returns no product, it falls through to `$this->scraper->snapshotOrMinimal($url)` — a second independent HTTP fetch of the identical URL, not wrapped in the same budget guard. A bot-blocked or JS-only product page (a common real case, per the surrounding comments about DoorDash/Uber Eats WAF-blocking) doubles the outbound request rate and adds a second full network round trip to the user-facing preview latency.
    - **Plain English:** When someone pastes a product link that turns out not to have clear product details, the system visits that website twice in the same request instead of reusing what it already downloaded the first time — doubling how often outside websites get pinged and slowing down what the user sees.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $read = $this->budget->open($seconds, fn () => $this->shop->readProductPage($url));
        ...
        // No product markup: fall through to the link card, product null.
        ...
        $card = $this->scraper->snapshotOrMinimal($url);
        ```

A professional with a long-lived, high-traffic site pays a full historical aggregation on every dashboard open.

## P3 — Nice to have

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-14** · P3 — Migration adding three nullable columns to `content.offers` omits `SET LOCAL lock_timeout`/`statement_timeout`
    - **Where:** supabase/migrations/20260826200000_offers_per_item_platform_links.sql:34-41
    - **Affects:** Deploy safety for `content.offers`; consistency with sibling migrations in the same date range.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add the same `SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s'; SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '10s';` pair every sibling migration in this batch already uses.
    - **Technical:** `ADD COLUMN` on three nullable columns with no default is metadata-only and fast, but the file is the one outlier in this migration batch that omits the lock/statement timeout guard every neighbor applies, so an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock request here could wait indefinitely behind an unrelated long-running read on `content.offers`.
    - **Plain English:** This deploy step doesn't have the safety timer its sibling steps all have. The change itself is quick, but without the timer it could wait forever if something else is briefly using that table.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```sql
        BEGIN;

        ALTER TABLE "content"."offers"
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "platform" text NULL,
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "item_url" text NULL,
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "external_ref" text NULL;

        COMMIT;
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-15** · P3 — `DROP COLUMN` migration for `content.items.eligible_cache` carries no lock/statement timeout
    - **Where:** supabase/migrations/20260827071000_drop_items_eligible_cache.sql:10
    - **Affects:** Deploy safety for `content.items`, the platform's largest content table.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the statement in `BEGIN; SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s'; SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '10s'; ...; COMMIT;`.
    - **Technical:** `DROP COLUMN` is metadata-only (no rewrite) but still requires an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock. The file is a single bare statement with no transaction wrapper at all, so it cannot set a local timeout as written. The operation itself is quick and low-risk; this is a hardening gap, not an active defect.
    - **Plain English:** Removing this unused column is normally instant, but the deploy step has no safety timer to bail out early if the table happens to be busy at that exact moment.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```sql
        ALTER TABLE "content"."items" DROP COLUMN IF EXISTS "eligible_cache";
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-16** · P3 — `ProjectionWriter::upsertCollections()` re-seeds uncurated collection order with one UPDATE per collection
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php:2633-2658
    - **Affects:** One-time re-seed cost per `(user, kind)` the first time a machine-derived collection set is ordered.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Replace the per-collection `UPDATE` with one bulk statement (a `CASE` expression, or reuse the chunked-upsert pattern already used earlier in the same method).
    - **Technical:** The loop only fires once per `(user, kind)` — guarded by `if ($positions->count() < 2 || $positions->max() > 0) continue;`, meaning it never re-runs once any position is non-zero. For a menu with a few dozen categories this is a bounded, one-time cost, not a recurring hot-path concern, but it's still an easy batch conversion.
    - **Plain English:** The very first time a machine-created set of menu categories gets ordered, the system saves each category's position separately instead of all at once. This only happens once per menu, but it's still an easy fix.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($seeded as $w) {
            DB::table('content.collections')
                ->where('user_id', $userId)->where('kind', $kind)->where('external_ref', $w['external_ref'])
                ->update(['position' => $w['position']]);
        }
        ```

The one residual per-item cost is the conditional `UPDATE` fired only for rows whose headline or facets actually changed — a real but small subset of any batch in the common case. Kept as a P3 given how much of this method is already batched.


    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/SettleConnectedSourceIntentsCommand.php:59-70
    - **Affects:** Runtime of the daily `routing:settle-connected` sweep (06:10 UTC) when a chunk of 500 live intents spans many distinct users.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**

    - **Technical:** The command is already correctly `chunkById(500)`-bounded and this per-user query shape mirrors `SuggestionsController::index()`'s own inbox-read pattern by design (per its own comment). In the worst case (500 intents each from a distinct user) this is 500 queries in one chunk — real, but this is an off-peak, low-frequency daily maintenance sweep, not a user-facing path.
    - **Plain English:** This daily cleanup job already processes intents in safe batches, but within each batch it still asks the database once per person instead of asking once for the whole batch.

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-19** · P3 — `ComputeContentPopularityScores::siteIdsWithRecentEvents()` merges site ids from four analytics tables in PHP
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/ComputeContentPopularityScores.php:318-330
    - **Affects:** The 15-minute `analytics:compute-popularity` scheduler tick's platform-wide active-site discovery step.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Replace the four sequential `pluck('site_id')` + PHP-side `array` merge with a single SQL `UNION` (or `UNION DISTINCT`) across the four tables.
    - **Technical:** Unlike the per-site aggregation elsewhere in this file (which is correctly bounded to `ScoringWindow::since()` and one site), this method scans across the whole platform for any site with an event in the last `RECENT_EVENTS_WINDOW_MINUTES` (60 min), merging distinct site ids from four tables into a PHP array. This scales with total platform activity rather than one site's data, which fits this lens's "thousands of users" framing better than a per-user finding — though the underlying reads are each indexed on `occurred_at` and the actual active-site count in a 60-minute window is realistically bounded.
    - **Plain English:** Every 15 minutes, the system asks four different visit-tracking tables "which sites had any activity in the last hour" and stitches the answers together by hand in memory, instead of asking the database to do that stitching in one go.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach (['analytics.section_views', 'analytics.link_clicks', 'analytics.item_views', 'analytics.action_events'] as $table) {
            foreach (
                DB::connection('pgsql')->table($table)
                    ->where('occurred_at', '>=', $since->toISOString())
                    ->distinct()
                    ->pluck('site_id') as $siteId
            ) {
                $ids[(string) $siteId] = true;
            }
        }
        ```

    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/PublicSite/AnalyticsController.php:600-635
    - **Affects:** Log volume and per-request latency on the `rum` beacon during a page's traffic spike.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Sample RUM logs (e.g. 5–10%) before calling `Log::info`, or route them through a non-blocking/batched log channel.
    - **Technical:** `rum()` already filters bot user agents before logging, but every remaining (real-user) request writes a synchronous structured `Log::info` call. The payload is small and bounded (a handful of numeric timing fields plus a hashed handle), so this is lower severity than an unbounded payload log, but it is still an uncapped write-per-pageload on a viral page.
    - **Plain English:** Every real visitor's page-load speed gets written to a log file the instant it happens, with no sampling. A page that suddenly gets a huge number of visitors means a huge number of log writes, all at once.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        Log::info('rum', [
            'handle' => hash('sha256', strtolower($handle)),
            'ttfb_ms' => isset($payload['ttfb']) ? (int) $payload['ttfb'] : null,
            ...
        ]);
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-21** · P3 — Staff professional search uses leading-wildcard `ILIKE` across seven columns with no verified trigram index
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/Staff/UserSiteManagement/StaffUserController.php:71-83
    - **Affects:** Staff dashboard search latency as `core.users` grows past ~10K rows.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `pg_trgm` GIN indexes on the searched columns if search latency becomes noticeable, or move to Postgres full-text search.
    - **Technical:** A `%term%` `ILIKE` pattern across `handle`, `display_name`, `primary_email`, `phone`, `first_name`, `last_name`, `sector`, plus a `whereHas` on `site.subdomain`, cannot use a plain B-tree index — Postgres sequential-scans `core.users`. This is a staff-only, low-QPS internal tool, not a user-facing path, so the urgency is low even though the query shape is genuinely unindexed.
    - **Plain English:** When staff search for a professional, the system has to check every user record one by one because the search starts with "anything, then the term" — no shortcut index can help. It's not felt by real users, only by staff running searches.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $qq->whereRaw('handle ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
            ->orWhereRaw('display_name ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
            ->orWhereRaw('primary_email ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
            ->orWhereRaw('phone ILIKE ?', [$searchLike])
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-22** · P3 — `SuggestionsController::index()` selects the full `payload` column for every grouped connection though nothing in the handler reads it
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/Routing/SuggestionsController.php:84-90
    - **Affects:** Per-request memory/transfer for a user's suggestions inbox render with many integration connections.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Drop `payload` from the `->get([...])` column list unless a downstream consumer needs it; verified no `->payload` access exists in this file's `index()`/`needsSwapResolution()`/`decideSwapIncumbent()` path.
    - **Technical:** The grouped `IntegrationConnection` read explicitly selects `payload`, but a repo-wide check of this file shows no `->payload` access on the `$connectionsBySurface` rows — `payload` is used elsewhere in the file only via a separately-fetched `$gb` connection (`googleListingSuggestion()`). `IntegrationConnection.payload` can carry a full scraped third-party payload, so this is real, if minor, over-fetching on a page render bounded to 100 intents.
    - **Plain English:** The suggestions inbox asks the database for the full contents of every connected account's record when it only ever uses the small identifying fields — a bit like requesting someone's entire file folder to read just the label on the front.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        IntegrationConnection::query()
            ->where('user_id', $user->id)
            ->whereIn('surface_key', $intents->pluck('surface_key')->unique()->all())
            ->whereNull('deleted_at')
            ->orderBy('created_at')
            ->get(['id', 'surface_key', 'resource_id', 'canonical_key', 'payload'])
            ->groupBy('surface_key');
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-23** · P3 — `BackfillStorefrontFaviconsCommand` applies `--limit` after hydrating the full candidate set into memory
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/BackfillStorefrontFaviconsCommand.php:43-54
    - **Affects:** Operator memory when running this one-off backfill against a large `content.storefronts` table.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Apply the limit in SQL (`->when($limit > 0, fn ($q) => $q->limit($limit))`) instead of `->take()` on an already-materialized collection.
    - **Technical:** `$candidates = ...->get(); if ($limit > 0) { $candidates = $candidates->take($limit); }` — the `--limit` flag exists specifically as a safety valve but doesn't bound the query, only the already-hydrated PHP collection. This is an operator-run, non-scheduled maintenance command with its own `--dry-run`/`--limit` guardrails, so the practical severity is low, but the fix is trivial.
    - **Technical:** —
    - **Plain English:** This one-off maintenance tool has a "stop after N" switch, but it only flips that switch after already loading everything into memory — the safety valve doesn't actually limit the work being loaded.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $candidates = DB::table('content.storefronts as s')
            ->join('content.collections as c', 'c.id', '=', 's.collection_id')
            ->whereNull('s.favicon_url')
            ->whereNull('s.logo_url')
            ->where('s.is_individual', false)
            ->select('s.collection_id', 'c.user_id')
            ->orderBy('s.collection_id')
            ->get();

        if ($limit > 0) {
            $candidates = $candidates->take($limit);
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-24** · P3 — `BackfillStorefrontFaviconsCommand` reads and writes one storefront at a time
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/BackfillStorefrontFaviconsCommand.php:59-89
    - **Affects:** Runtime of this operator-run backfill for a large candidate set.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - If this backfill needs to run recurringly rather than once, move it to a queued job with chunked reads; as a one-off tool with `--dry-run`/`--limit`, this is low priority.
    - **Technical:** The `foreach ($candidates as $row)` loop calls `$shop->storeByCollection(...)` and `$writer->upsertStore(...)` per candidate — a per-row service read/write. Same file and same root cause as SCALE-23; grouped for that reason.
    - **Plain English:** The backfill tool processes each store one at a time — look one up, fix it, move to the next — rather than working through them in batches.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($candidates as $row) {
            $store = $shop->storeByCollection((string) $row->collection_id);
            ...
            $writer->upsertStore($store->with(['faviconUrl' => $favicon]), (string) $row->user_id);
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-25** · P3 — `BackfillStorefrontFaviconsCommand` fetches each candidate's favicon with no per-host delay
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/BackfillStorefrontFaviconsCommand.php:70-78
    - **Affects:** Third-party host politeness and backfill reliability when several candidates share a host.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a small per-host delay around `$favicons->fetch($source)`, or confirm the underlying scraper already rate-limits per host.
    - **Technical:** Same file/root cause as SCALE-23/24 — the outbound fetch loop has no visible per-host throttle. Grouped as part of the same one-off, operator-controlled command with its own `--dry-run` safety net.
    - **Plain English:** The tool visits each store's website to grab its icon as fast as it can, with no pause between visits — which can look like abuse to a website if several candidates happen to share a host.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $favicon = $favicons->fetch($source);
        if ($favicon === null) {
            $missed++;
            continue;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-26** · P3 — `GoogleBusinessAutoSync::seedOrdering()` re-fetches the same user via `User::find()` inside each store-loop iteration
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/GoogleBusinessAutoSync.php:606-637
    - **Affects:** Minor extra `core.users` PK lookups during Google Business ordering seed, bounded by `MAX_ORDERING = 10`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Resolve `$user` once before the `foreach ($stores as $storeKey => $group)` loop and reuse it, or thread the already-loaded `User` from `seed()` into `seedOrdering()`.
    - **Technical:** The caller (`seed()`) already loads `$user = User::find($userId)` at line 104; `seedOrdering()` re-fetches it via a fresh `User::find($userId)` PK lookup inside every store iteration, capped at `MAX_ORDERING = 10` per run. Low severity given the hard cap and the fact it's a PK-indexed lookup.
    - **Plain English:** While matching up ordering links for a business, the code re-asks for the same customer's file on every store it checks, even though it already has that file open — capped at ten repeats per run.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $user = User::find($userId);
        $routed = $user === null
            ? null
            : $this->linkRouter->routeOrdering($user, $repUrl);
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-SCALE-27** · P3 — `SquareMenuDriver::fetchProducts()` paginates up to 20 pages with no delay between successful requests
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/SquareMenuDriver.php:219-249
    - **Affects:** Outbound request pacing against `cdn5.editmysite.com` for a single large Square store's menu refresh.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a small delay between successful page fetches, or register this host under the same per-host politeness mechanism other platform drivers use.
    - **Technical:** `fetchProducts()` loops up to `MAX_PAGES = 20`, each iteration calling `productsPage()`, which only sleeps between *failed* attempts (`usleep(self::RETRY_DELAY_MS * 1000)`), never between successful sequential pages. A large single-store menu can issue up to 20 back-to-back requests with no pause. The whole job is already bounded by the global `platform-connect` per-minute rate limiter at the `MenuFetchJob` level, which caps how many such bursts can happen concurrently across users — this finding is about the burst shape within one store's own pagination, not cross-user concurrency.
    - **Plain English:** If one restaurant's Square menu spans 20 pages, the scraper requests all 20 back-to-back with no pause between them — like knocking on the same door 20 times in a row without waiting for an answer.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        for ($page = 1; $page <= self::MAX_PAGES; $page++) {
            $resp = $this->productsPage($userId, $siteId, $page);
            if ($resp === null) {
                return $products === [] ? null : $products;
            }
            $batch = (array) $resp->json('data');
            $products = array_merge($products, $batch);
            ...
        }
        ```
