## P1 — Fix before pilot launch

- [x] **#W1-CFG-1** · P1 — Feature flag `partna.connect.auto_booking.enabled` defaults to `true`, enabling unvetted feature in fresh environments
    - **Where:** config/partna.php:2161
    - **Affects:** Unclaimed Fresha sites; auto-booking connection behavior in fresh deployments without explicit opt-in.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Change the default to `false`: `'enabled' => (bool) env('PARTNA_AUTO_BOOKING_ENABLED', false)`.
        - Ensure `.env.example` documents the flag with a commented-out stub and explanation that it opts in to auto-booking discovery.
        - Add a test asserting the default is `false` so a regression is caught.
    - **Technical:** Feature flags must default to safe-off (disabled) so a new environment doesn't accidentally enable incomplete or capability-gated features. The current default of `true` means fresh deployments auto-connect Fresha booking menus from Instagram links without opting in — a behavior not intended for unvetted features. This violates the Partna pattern that capability-gated logic must never default to enabled.
    - **Plain English:** A new environment accidentally auto-connects restaurant booking menus to every user's Instagram handle without anyone asking for it. It's like shipping a building with all the lights on even though nobody decided to use them yet. The switch should be off by default.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        'auto_booking' => [
            'enabled' => (bool) env('PARTNA_AUTO_BOOKING_ENABLED', true),
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): WONTFIX — premise false. The flag is a KILL SWITCH for a shipped feature, not a rollout gate for an unvetted one (46dfd867a, 2026-08-11). `partna.connect.deferred` is this repo's rollout-gate key and DOES default off (config/partna.php:2159); auto_booking is deliberately separate — see the comment at config/partna.php:2162-2168. Default TRUE is pinned on purpose by tests/Unit/Config/AutoBookingConfigTest.php:8, and all three consumers (SourceReconciler.php:225, LinkRouter.php:319, GoogleBusinessAutoSync.php:346) pass `true` as the config() fallback. Blast radius is already bounded by global_daily_cap=500/day and the isUnclaimed() gate. Flipping the default would disable a live pre-account feature in every fresh env.

- [x] **#W1-CFG-2** · P1 — Notification prune command hardcodes `--days=30`, potentially bypassing per-category retention config
    - **Where:** routes/console.php:60
    - **Affects:** Notification retention; long-lived categories like `policy_update` (365d) and `profile_task` (180d) may be pruned too early if the command honors the explicit override.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Remove the `['--days' => 30]` override and let the command read category-specific retention from config.
        - Or pass `config('partna.notification_retention_days.default', 30)` explicitly if a global fallback is intended.
        - Add a test proving per-category retention windows are respected.
    - **Technical:** The scheduler passes `--days=30` explicitly, while `config/partna.php` defines category-specific retention windows (policy_update=365, profile_task=180, default=30). If the command's `--days` option overrides its internal config lookup, all categories are pruned at 30 days regardless of their intended window. Even if the option is ignored, a hardcoded magic number duplicates config and drifts over time.
    - **Plain English:** The cleanup instructions say "throw out all notifications after 30 days," but the rulebook says some notifications stay for a year. The calendar has a sticky note that contradicts the rulebook. Remove the sticky note and let the rulebook decide per category.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        Schedule::command('partna:prune-notifications', ['--days' => 30])
            ->dailyAt('03:25')
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): STALE — per-category retention IS honoured, via ends_at, not via --days. NotificationPublisher.php:98-99,122 stamps ends_at = now + notification_retention_days[<category>] at PUBLISH time; PruneNotifications.php:78-79 deletes on `ends_at < cutoff`. So --days=30 is a grace period AFTER expiry (a 365-day policy_update goes at day 395), not a retention window — nothing is pruned early. Closed by adding a clarifying comment at routes/console.php and in the command docblock, plus a regression test in PruneNotificationsCriticalTest pinning that a +365d notification survives a --days=30 run.

---

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W1-CFG-3** · P2 — Horizon supervisors use hardcoded queue names instead of reading `config('partna.queues.*')`
    - **Where:** config/horizon.php:255 (supervisor-1 queue array) vs config/partna.php:2088-2125 (queue env mapping)
    - **Affects:** Operators using `PARTNA_QUEUE_*` env vars to reroute queue traffic; jobs would silently queue on an unmapped name.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Refactor Horizon supervisor queue arrays to reference `config('partna.queues.*')` instead of literal strings.
        - Ensure the queue assignment is consistent between dispatcher (`onQueue()` calls in jobs) and consumer (Horizon supervisor definitions).
        - Add a test that verifies changing a queue env var updates the supervisor's queue list.
    - **Technical:** `config/partna.php` declares queue names as env-driven config (e.g., `'cloudflare' => env('PARTNA_QUEUE_CLOUDFLARE', 'cloudflare')`), but `config/horizon.php` hardcodes string arrays. If an operator sets `PARTNA_QUEUE_CLOUDFLARE=cloudflare-v2`, dispatched jobs go to `cloudflare-v2` while Horizon keeps consuming `cloudflare`; the new queue has no consumer, so jobs back up silently. This split config-access pattern violates the single-source-of-truth principle and creates an invisible routing failure mode.
    - **Plain English:** Like having a routing label that can change on outgoing packages, but delivery trucks are hard-wired to pick up only the old labels. Someone changes the label, and packages sit in a bin no truck visits.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // config/horizon.php
        'queue' => [\App\Jobs\Moderation\ModerationQueue::HIGH, 'default', 'cloudflare', 'cache-warm', 'images', 'media-mirror', 'streaming', 'platform_refresh', 'platform_connect', 'analytics', 'cloudflare_bulk'],
        ```
        ```php
        // config/partna.php
        'cloudflare' => env('PARTNA_QUEUE_CLOUDFLARE', 'cloudflare'),
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-CFG-4** · P2 — `public_domain` has mismatched fallbacks between config and route bootstrap
    - **Where:** config/partna.php:98-104 (config default: `localhost`) and routes/api/publicSite.php (route fallback: `partna.au`)
    - **Affects:** Public subdomain resolution in environments where `PARTNA_PUBLIC_DOMAIN`, `SIDEST_PUBLIC_DOMAIN`, and `APP_URL` are missing; routes can resolve to the wrong domain.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Unify the fallback: either both use `partna.au` (production safe default) or add an explicit error when the config value is empty/`localhost`.
        - Ensure `AppServiceProvider` validates that `config('partna.public_domain')` is production-safe in prod environments (not `localhost`).
    - **Technical:** `config('partna.public_domain')` falls back through `PARTNA_PUBLIC_DOMAIN`, then `SIDEST_PUBLIC_DOMAIN`, then `parse_url(APP_URL) ?? 'localhost'`. In production with missing env but unset `APP_URL`, this yields `localhost`, which is non-empty so the config is never null — but it's wrong. Meanwhile `routes/api/publicSite.php` has its own fallback to `partna.au` that is unreachable because the config value is rarely null. Two different safety nets contradictorily fail.
    - **Plain English:** Safety net 1 says "if the address is missing, use localhost." Safety net 2 says "if it's blank, use partna.au." Net 1 catches the error first and uses the wrong fallback, so net 2 never helps.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // config/partna.php
        'public_domain' => env(
            'PARTNA_PUBLIC_DOMAIN',
            env(
                'SIDEST_PUBLIC_DOMAIN',
                parse_url((string) env('APP_URL', 'http://localhost'), PHP_URL_HOST) ?: 'localhost'
            )
        ),
        ```
        ```php
        // routes/api/publicSite.php
        $publicDomain = config('partna.public_domain') ?: 'partna.au';
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-CFG-5** · P2 — Analytics `page_titles` config comment says 15 canonical pages but array has 12 and `services` is labeled `book`
    - **Where:** config/partna.php:3030-3041
    - **Affects:** Analytics page-label consistency; missing canonical page IDs silently fall back to humanized names instead of curated labels.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Update the comment to say "12 canonical page-ids" (matching `App\Enums\SitepageId` count).
        - Replace `'book' => 'Book'` with `'services' => 'Services'` (the enum case is `Services`, not a booking page).
        - Verify every case in `SitepageId` has a corresponding entry in `page_titles`.
    - **Technical:** The comment explicitly claims "15 canonical page-ids (App\Enums\SitepageId)", but the enum has exactly 12 cases, and the config array has 12 entries. Additionally, the config uses the key `'book'` while the enum defines `Services = 'services'`. When `AnalyticsQueryService::pageTitle()` encounters an unknown page-id, it falls back to humanizing the key; any missing entry falls through to this implicit handling and displays an inconsistent generated label instead of the curated one.
    - **Plain English:** The nametag chart says it covers 15 rooms, but only 12 are defined. For missing rooms, the system makes up a name instead of using the one you picked. Also, one room is mislabeled.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // Comment: "Keyed by the 15 canonical page-ids (App\Enums\SitepageId)..."
        // Enum SitepageId has 12 cases (Home, Listen, Watch, Shop, Menu, Services, Events, Gallery, Reviews, Documents, Contact, Links)
        'page_titles' => [
            'home' => 'Home', 'listen' => 'Listen', 'watch' => 'Watch', 'shop' => 'Shop',
            'menu' => 'Menu', 'book' => 'Book',  // ← should be 'services' => 'Services'
            'events' => 'Events', 'gallery' => 'Gallery', 'reviews' => 'Reviews',
            'documents' => 'Documents', 'contact' => 'Contact', 'links' => 'Links',
        ],
        ```

Canonical fix is to add config keys with fallback defaults, consistent with how `SafeUrlFetcher` and other services read limits.

## Suggested Bundled Sessions

Group findings that share a file, subsystem, or root-cause pattern into coherent fix sessions (each bundle = one `execute audit` working session).

- **Bundle 1 — Feature flags safe defaults (P1):** #CFG-1
    - **Why grouped:** Single file change, blocking gate (P1).
    - **Model:** Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 2 — Queue config consistency (P2):** #CFG-3
    - **Why grouped:** Cross-file refactor (Horizon + config + jobs), shared root cause.
    - **Model:** Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 3 — Config fallbacks & comments (P2-P3):** #CFG-4, #CFG-5

# Migration Safety Audit — 2026-08-28

**Branch:** development
**Lens:** Migration safety: lock-on-deploy risk, backfill ordering, online DDL hygiene, reversibility
**Pipeline:** scan-tier draft by `deepseek-v4-pro`, adjudicated by `claude-opus-4.6`
**Source files audited:**
- supabase/migrations/20260826120000_facet_source_item_origin.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260826120001_facet_origin_idx_item_media.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260826140000_source_intents_identifier_label.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260826200000_offers_per_item_platform_links.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260827070000_analytics_scoring_watermarks.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260827071000_drop_items_eligible_cache.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260827072000_event_item_family.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260827080000_drop_design_kit_vestige_columns.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260827090000_add_typography_uppercase.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260827120000_sites_architecture_default_scroll.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260828010000_pre_account_builds_published_by_claim.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260828030000_f_review_staff_name.sql
- supabase/migrations/CONVENTIONS.md
- scripts/guard-no-unsafe-migrations.php

## Progress

- P0 Blockers: 0 of 0 complete
- P1 High: 1 of 1 complete
- P2 Medium: 0 of 0 complete
- P3 Low: 0 of 0 complete

---

## P1 — Fix before pilot launch

- [x] **#W1-MIG-1** · P1 — Inline `ADD COLUMN ... REFERENCES` on four `content.*` tables adds a validated FK, bypassing both the repo's `NOT VALID`/`VALIDATE` convention and the automated lint that enforces it
    - **Where:** supabase/migrations/20260826120000_facet_source_item_origin.sql:34-48
    - **Affects:** `content.item_media`, `content.offers`, `content.item_tags`, `content.item_variants` — all four are being written continuously by the active platform-harvesting pipeline (the wave-2 batch of 34 new brands + Deezer + TikTok/Facebook connectors landed in the days immediately preceding this migration, per recent commits `fb0da3592`, `4d630138a`). Concurrent writers to these tables are blocked for the duration of each `ALTER TABLE`'s FK-validation scan.
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Split each column addition: `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "source_item_id" uuid NULL;` with no inline `REFERENCES`.
        - Add the FK in a companion migration as a named constraint: `ADD CONSTRAINT ... FOREIGN KEY ("source_item_id") REFERENCES "content"."source_items" ("id") ON DELETE CASCADE NOT VALID;`.
        - `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT ...` in its own transaction/file per `CONVENTIONS.md` §4, guarded with `SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s'; SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '10s';`.
        - Separately: `scripts/guard-no-unsafe-migrations.php` Check 2 only matches the literal `ADD CONSTRAINT ... FOREIGN KEY` shape (`/ADD\s+CONSTRAINT\s+\S+\s+FOREIGN\s+KEY.../`) — it does not match an inline `REFERENCES` clause on `ADD COLUMN` at all, so this exact pattern currently passes CI silently. Extend Check 2's regex to also flag `ADD COLUMN ... REFERENCES` without a trailing `NOT VALID`/companion split, or the same evasion will recur (a second live instance already exists at `20260812090000_content_media_assets_site_media_id.sql:15-16`, unflagged for the same reason).
    - **Technical:** `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN col uuid REFERENCES other(id)` adds the FK as part of the same command, which validates it immediately under `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` — a lock that conflicts with the `ROW EXCLUSIVE` lock every `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE` takes, so concurrent writers queue behind it. `CONVENTIONS.md` §4 states the FK rule unconditionally ("always `NOT VALID` first") with no carve-out for a column added in the same statement — unlike the `CREATE INDEX` check (Check 1), which explicitly exempts columns added in the same file, the guard's FK check (Check 2) has no such exemption and simply never sees this statement shape. Because the column is new and every existing row is NULL, the validation predicate (`col IS NOT NULL`) is trivially false for every row, but Postgres still must sequentially read the whole heap to evaluate that predicate on each row while holding the lock — an I/O-bound cost that scales with table size, not a true no-op. The four `UPDATE` backfills that immediately follow (lines 54-112) run in the same unbounded, un-`BEGIN`'d multi-statement file, so the ALTER-induced locks and the backfill's row locks are held back-to-back inside one implicit transaction with no `lock_timeout`/`statement_timeout` bound — a long scan fails to abort quickly and instead queues behind live harvesting writes for however long it takes.
    - **Plain English:** This migration adds four new columns and, in the same breath, tells the database to double-check every existing row against another table — while holding a lock that blocks anyone else from writing to those tables. Because the columns are brand new and start out empty, that check should be fast, but the database still has to read through the entire table to confirm that, and it holds the "no one else can write right now" lock the whole time it's reading. On the four tables involved here, background jobs are actively importing new content from dozens of platforms as this deploys, so those imports would stall for however long the check takes. The safer, two-step version used everywhere else in this codebase adds the rule first (instant), then checks the existing rows separately under a much gentler lock that doesn't block other writers.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```sql
        ALTER TABLE "content"."item_media"
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "source_item_id" uuid NULL
            REFERENCES "content"."source_items" ("id") ON DELETE CASCADE;

        ALTER TABLE "content"."offers"
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "source_item_id" uuid NULL
            REFERENCES "content"."source_items" ("id") ON DELETE CASCADE;

        ALTER TABLE "content"."item_tags"
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "source_item_id" uuid NULL
            REFERENCES "content"."source_items" ("id") ON DELETE CASCADE;

        ALTER TABLE "content"."item_variants"
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "source_item_id" uuid NULL
            REFERENCES "content"."source_items" ("id") ON DELETE CASCADE;
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): LINT-ONLY; the DB half is WONTFIX. The lint gap is real — Check 2 anchors on ADD CONSTRAINT, so an FK hanging off ADD COLUMN was structurally invisible, and exactly two files in the tree carry the pattern. But the prescribed remedy (split the already-applied migration and re-add NOT VALID -> VALIDATE) is rejected: the CLI tracks by version, not content hash, so the edit would never run on dev and would only change fresh-apply behaviour; and DROP CONSTRAINT + re-add takes STRICTLY MORE locking than doing nothing while opening an FK-absent write window on live harvesting tables. Measured on dev: the four content.* tables are 590-15,514 rows and all four FKs are already convalidated, so the validation scan cost milliseconds. Production has no content schema at all, so these files will apply there against empty tables and land correctly validated. The finding's lock analysis is also off — ADD COLUMN takes ACCESS EXCLUSIVE regardless; the FK's genuine incremental cost is the RI_Initial_Check scan inside that existing window plus SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE on the REFERENCED table, which the finding never mentions. Fixed forward with guard Check 10 (cutoff 20260828999999, grandfathering the two applied files), a CONVENTIONS.md §4 paragraph mirroring §2's inline-CHECK precedent, and four behavioural tests including the two anti-false-positive cases that keep the legitimate one-statement split passing.

## Suggested Bundled Sessions

None — the single finding is standalone (see below).

## Standalone — do NOT bundle

- **#MIG-1 — Inline `ADD COLUMN ... REFERENCES` bypasses the `NOT VALID`/`VALIDATE` split and the lint that enforces it** · standalone: touches a DB migration/schema change (four `content.*` tables) and modifies a shared CI guard script (`scripts/guard-no-unsafe-migrations.php`) — needs its own plan, a companion-migration pass, and independent verification that the guard regex change doesn't false-positive on the legitimate two-file split pattern used elsewhere in the tree.

# API Contract & Resource Leakage Audit — 2026-08-28

**Branch:** development
**Lens:** API Contract & Resource Leakage: raw model fields bleeding through, over-fetching, inconsistent pagination
**Pipeline:** scan-tier draft by `deepseek-v4-pro`, adjudicated by `claude-opus-4.6`
**Source files audited:**
- app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Content/ContentController.php
- app/Http/Controllers/Api/Staff/UserSiteManagement/StaffUserController.php
- app/Http/Controllers/Api/Platforms/GoogleBusinessController.php
- app/Http/Controllers/Api/Content/LinkPreviewController.php
- app/Http/Controllers/Api/Routing/SuggestionsController.php
- app/Http/Resources/PublicSite/IndividualProfileResource.php
- app/Http/Resources/Platforms/PublicIntegrationConnectionResource.php
- app/Http/Resources/Platforms/YoutubeConnectionResource.php
- app/Http/Resources/Platforms/LinkConnectionResource.php
- app/Http/Resources/Platforms/IntegrationsMetaResource.php
- app/Site/Actions/ActionCandidates.php
- app/Site/Actions/ConnectionProfileUrl.php
- app/Site/Pools/PoolResolver.php
- app/Services/PublicSite/SitepageDataResolverService.php
- app/Services/PublicSite/IndividualProfilePayloadBuilder.php
- tests/Feature/Api/PublicSite/IndividualProfileControllerTest.php
- tests/Feature/Resources/IndividualProfileResourceTest.php

## Progress

- P0 Blockers: 0 of 0 complete
- P1 High: 0 of 0 complete
- P2 Medium: 0 of 1 complete
- P3 Low: 0 of 8 complete

---

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W1-API-1** · P2 — Public sitepage build loads full section `Block` rows, including a contact block's private notification settings, on the highest-traffic public path
    - **Where:** app/Services/PublicSite/SitepageDataResolverService.php (`loadSections()`)
    - **Affects:** Every unauthenticated public sitepage request. The contact block's `settings` JSONB can hold `notification_email` / `notification_channels` — the owner's real inbox address and alert preferences — which `buildContact()` explicitly documents as never meant to reach the wire.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Project only the columns the public build path actually reads (`block_type`, `is_active`, `is_enabled`, `settings`, `sort_order`) via an explicit `select()`, or split a public-only projection that strips `notification_email`/`notification_channels` from the `settings` array before it's held in the shared `$sections` collection.
        - Since `$sections` is also handed to `ActionCandidates` and other collaborators, prefer narrowing at the JSONB-key level (Postgres `settings - 'notification_email' - 'notification_channels'`) over a blanket column drop, so nothing downstream silently loses a key it does need.
        - Add a regression test asserting the hydrated public `$sections` collection never carries `notification_email`/`notification_channels` in memory, not just that the final wire payload omits them.
    - **Technical:** `loadSections()` runs `Block::query()->where('site_id', ...)->where('block_group', 'sections')->...->get()->keyBy('block_type')` with no column projection, so every column — including the full `settings` JSONB — is pulled into PHP for every section block on every public profile request. `buildContact()`'s own comment confirms `notification_email`/`notification_channels` are "intentionally NOT surfaced," which means today's serialization is correct, but the raw values still transit the highest-traffic, CDN-fronted request path in server memory for no functional reason. That widens the blast radius of any future logging statement, exception context, or serialization bug touching `$sections` — exactly the kind of accidental-PII-in-a-stack-trace failure mode the platform's public-payload PII rule exists to prevent.
    - **Plain English:** To build the public page, the system opens the owner's entire settings folder — including the page with their private forwarding email and alert preferences — even though it only needs the public price-list page from that same folder. Nothing on the visible page shows the private page today, but carrying it around unnecessarily on every visit means one future mistake (a stray debug log, an error report) could accidentally print it. Asking for just the public page closes that door.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        return Block::query()
            ->where('site_id', $site->id)
            ->where('block_group', 'sections')
            ->whereNull('deleted_at')
            ->orderBy('sort_order')
            ->get()
            ->keyBy('block_type');
        ```
        ```php
        // Private owner settings (notification_email, notification_channels) are
        // intentionally NOT surfaced — they never belong in the public payload.
        ```

## P3 — Nice to have

- [ ] **#W1-API-2** · P3 — `ActionCandidates` selects four `IntegrationConnection` columns it never reads
    - **Where:** app/Site/Actions/ActionCandidates.php (`forSite()`)
    - **Affects:** Sitepage action-candidate building for every profile with active integrations; adds unread bytes to the query on a path that already fans out per pool item.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Trim the `get([...])` column list to `['platform', 'routing_class', 'payload', 'created_at']` — confirmed as the only fields the loop and `ConnectionProfileUrl::for()` read.
        - If a future caller needs `id`/`user_id`/`surface_key`/`resource_id`, add them back explicitly rather than defaulting to "everything."
    - **Technical:** The query selects `['id', 'user_id', 'platform', 'surface_key', 'routing_class', 'resource_id', 'payload', 'created_at']`; verified against the method body and `ConnectionProfileUrl::for()` (which reads only `platform` and `payload`), the loop only ever touches `platform`, `routing_class`, `payload`, and `created_at`. `id`, `user_id`, `surface_key`, and `resource_id` are fetched and discarded on every call.
    - **Plain English:** The system asks for four extra fields it never actually looks at every time it figures out what buttons to show on a profile page. It's harmless today, but it's wasted database work on a page that gets requested constantly.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $connections = IntegrationConnection::query()
            ->where('user_id', $pro->id)
            ->where('is_active', true)
            ->orderBy('created_at')
            ->get(['id', 'user_id', 'platform', 'surface_key', 'routing_class', 'resource_id', 'payload', 'created_at']);
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-API-3** · P3 — `PoolResolver` fetches every `content.items` column but only reads four of them
    - **Where:** app/Site/Pools/PoolResolver.php (`itemPayloads()`)
    - **Affects:** Every public pool payload build (watch/listen/media/services/shop/etc.) — this query runs on the hot sitepage path.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `->select(['id', 'kind', 'headline_cache', 'first_seen_at'])` (or the full confirmed-used set, if a later pass finds more) to the `content.items` query.
        - Keep `user_id` and `removed_at` in the `where`/`whereNull` clauses — they don't need to be selected to filter on.
    - **Technical:** `DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.items')->whereIn('id', $ids)->where('user_id', $site->user_id)->whereNull('removed_at')->get()->keyBy('id')` has no projection. Grepping every `$item->`/`$i->` access against this collection downstream confirms only `id`, `kind`, `headline_cache`, and `first_seen_at` are read from the row itself (other fields referenced nearby, e.g. `track_number`/`album`, come from a separate `$music` map, not this row).
    - **Plain English:** The system asks the database for an item's entire record — every stored field — when it only ever uses four small pieces of it. It throws the rest away every time. Asking for just those four fields makes the public page faster to build.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $items = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.items')
            ->whereIn('id', $ids)
            ->where('user_id', $site->user_id)
            ->whereNull('removed_at')
            ->get()
            ->keyBy('id');
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-API-4** · P3 — Public profile resolution fetches the full `payload` JSONB for every active integration connection just to check completeness
    - **Where:** app/Services/PublicSite/SitepageDataResolverService.php (`presentPageIds()`)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage build for every profile with at least one active integration connection.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Fetch `['id', 'user_id', 'platform']` first; only re-fetch `payload` for the connection when its platform actually registers a `completenessGate` (`PlatformDescriptor::hasCompletenessPredicate()`).
        - Since `hasCompletenessPredicate()` already exists for exactly this purpose, gate the extra column read on it rather than always paying for every platform.
    - **Technical:** The `IntegrationConnection` query selects `['id', 'user_id', 'platform', 'payload']` for every active connection, but `isComplete()` only actually inspects the connection when a platform has registered a `completenessGate` closure (confirmed in `PlatformDescriptor::isComplete()`/`hasCompletenessPredicate()`) — most platforms have none and return `true` unconditionally without touching `payload`. The full JSONB is still pulled into memory for all of them. Verified: this data is not itself classic PII (no OAuth tokens/secrets found in `IntegrationConnection` payloads), so this is a performance/hygiene finding, not a leak.
    - **Plain English:** Before showing a page, the system grabs each connected account's full stored file just to check a box that says "is this ready to show?" — but for almost every platform, that check doesn't even need to open the file. Skipping the unnecessary open makes page loads faster.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $connections = $this->safeQuery(
            fn () => IntegrationConnection::query()
                ->where('user_id', $userId)
                ->where('is_active', true)
                ->get(['id', 'user_id', 'platform', 'payload'])
                ->all(),
            [],
            'active_integration_connections',
            $site,
        );
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-API-5** · P3 — Public pool build fetches dashboard-only `sources` account metadata that the visitor never sees
    - **Where:** app/Site/Pools/PoolResolver.php (`itemPayloads()` / `DASHBOARD_ONLY_ITEM_KEYS`)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage visitors indirectly — no leak, but wasted queries and third-party account names/sync timestamps briefly held in memory on the public request path.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Extend the existing `$withDuplicateCandidates`-style opt-out pattern: add a `$withDashboardSources` flag to `hydrateItems()`/`itemPayloads()`, defaulted `false`, and thread it through `PoolWire::forSite()`'s call (`withDuplicateCandidates: false`) alongside the existing flag.
        - When false, skip the `ingest.sources` query and `$sourcesByItem` assembly entirely; `$sourcePlatforms` (used for public link-button fallback) is a separate read and must stay.
    - **Technical:** `DASHBOARD_ONLY_ITEM_KEYS = ['selected', 'overrides', 'sources', 'duplicateCandidates']` marks `sources` as dashboard-only, and `PoolWire.php` does strip it from the public payload after hydration (confirmed at the `DASHBOARD_ONLY_ITEM_KEYS` foreach). But unlike `duplicateCandidates`, which is properly gated behind the `$withDuplicateCandidates` flag (`if ($withDuplicateCandidates)` at line 984, opted out for the public path since `PoolWire::forSite()` calls `hydrateItems(..., withDuplicateCandidates: false)`), the `ingest.sources` query and `$sourcesByItem` build run unconditionally regardless of that flag. The public build pays for a query and an assembly pass whose only output is deleted before the response is sent.
    - **Plain English:** The public page build runs an extra errand — looking up which third-party accounts feed each item and when they last synced — then throws that list away right before sending the page. The visitor never sees it, but the server does the work anyway on every single public page load. There's already a working "skip this for public builds" switch for a similar case; this needs the same switch.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        public const DASHBOARD_ONLY_ITEM_KEYS = ['selected', 'overrides', 'sources', 'duplicateCandidates'];
        ```
        ```php
        $ingestByConnection = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('ingest.sources')
            ->whereIn('connection_id', $connectionIds)
            ->orderByDesc('last_run_at')
            ->get(['connection_id', 'last_run_at', 'auto_sync'])
            ->unique('connection_id')
            ->keyBy('connection_id')
            ->all();
        ```
        ```php
        'sources' => $sourcesByItem[$itemId] ?? [],
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-API-6** · P3 — Content library controller bypasses Resource-collection wrapping with manual `->toArray($request)`
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Content/ContentController.php (`library()`, `storeUpload()`)
    - **Affects:** Authenticated Content dashboard owner using the upload library and image-upload endpoints.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - In `library()`, return `ContentLibraryUploadResource::collection($uploads)` instead of `->map(fn ($m) => (new ContentLibraryUploadResource($m))->toArray($request))`.
        - In `storeUpload()`, add `item_id` as a conditional field inside `ContentLibraryUploadResource` (e.g. via `additional()` or a constructor param) rather than array-merging it onto the manually-resolved array in the controller.
    - **Technical:** Both call sites invoke `(new ContentLibraryUploadResource($m))->toArray($request)` directly instead of returning Resource/Resource-collection instances, so Laravel's Resource-collection machinery (and any future collection-level `additional()` metadata) never runs. `storeUpload()` compounds this by merging `+ ['item_id' => ...]` onto the plain array outside the Resource class, meaning the response shape can drift from what `ContentLibraryUploadResource` alone declares. Low practical impact today since both are wrapped a level up by the controller's own `success()` envelope and neither needs collection-level meta yet, but it's the same anti-pattern CI would ordinarily push back on elsewhere.
    - **Plain English:** Instead of plating each dish in the kitchen the same way every time, this endpoint half-plates each one, carries it into the hallway, and adds a garnish there. It works, but it means two different endpoints can end up describing the same kind of item slightly differently just because the finishing touch happens in different places.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        ->map(fn (SiteMedia $m) => (new ContentLibraryUploadResource($m))->toArray($request))
        ->values()
        ->all();
        ```
        ```php
        return $this->success(
            (new ContentLibraryUploadResource($media))->toArray($request)
                + ['item_id' => $bridged['id'] ?? null],
            201,
        );
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-API-7** · P3 — Staff professionals index bypasses Resource-collection wrapping with manual `->toArray($request)`
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/Staff/UserSiteManagement/StaffUserController.php (`index()`)
    - **Affects:** Staff clients consuming `GET /api/staff/professionals` (internal staff dashboard, `staff`-gated).
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Return `StaffUserListResource::collection($page->getCollection())` and pass `$showPii` through the Resource's constructor argument list (Resource collections accept `additional()`/constructor args per item via a custom collection class, or simplest: keep the per-item `new StaffUserListResource($p, $showPii)` map but let it return Resource instances rather than resolved arrays — `$this->success()` will serialize them the same way).
    - **Technical:** `$page->getCollection()->map(fn (User $p) => (new StaffUserListResource($p, $showPii))->toArray($request))` manually resolves each Resource into a plain array before the controller reassigns it into a custom `$payload['professionals']` envelope. This is a same-pattern issue as #API-6, on a different (internal, `staff`-gated) surface — grouped here for tier consistency, not because of any authorization concern (the route is already staff-gated and out of scope per audit policy).
    - **Plain English:** Same issue as the content-library one above, just on the staff side: the list of professionals is unpacked from its normal packaging before being handed off, instead of being handed off in the standard box.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $professionals = $page->getCollection()->map(
            fn (User $p) => (new StaffUserListResource($p, $showPii))->toArray($request)
        );

        $payload = $this->paginatedResponse($page, 'professionals');
        $payload['professionals'] = $professionals;
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-API-8** · P3 — Routing suggestions inbox is hard-capped at 100 rows with no pagination or "more available" signal
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/Routing/SuggestionsController.php (`index()`)
    - **Affects:** Dashboard users with more than 100 open routing suggestions — an unusual but possible state for an account with many recognised-but-unconfirmed platform links.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Replace `->limit(100)->get()` with `->paginate($perPage)` (or a cursor paginator), matching the pattern already used elsewhere (`StaffUserController::index()`).
        - Emit pagination metadata (`meta.current_page`/`meta.last_page` or a cursor) in the `success()` envelope so a client can tell there's more to fetch.
    - **Technical:** `index()` runs `DB::table('routing.source_intents')->where(...)->orderByDesc('first_seen_at')->limit(100)->get()` with no paginator and no metadata; anything past the 100th row is silently invisible with no indication more exist. This is a real but low-likelihood edge case — most accounts will have a handful of open suggestions — so it's hardening rather than a documented lifecycle event.
    - **Plain English:** The suggestions inbox stops loading after the first 100 items and gives no hint that there might be more. For the rare account with a very active integration history, some suggestions would simply never be reachable on screen.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $intents = DB::table('routing.source_intents')
            ->where('user_id', $user->id)
            ->whereIn('state', ['proposed', 'blocked'])
            ->orderByDesc('first_seen_at')
            ->limit(100)
            ->get();
        ```

- [ ] **#W1-API-9** · P3 — Accept-suggestion response only carries a `status` field on the async/probed-store path
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/Routing/SuggestionsController.php (`accept()`, `acceptPayloadFinding()`, `acceptGoogleListing()`)
    - **Affects:** Dashboard clients handling the "accept suggestion" action across its three code paths.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Always emit `status` (`'ready'` for the synchronous paths, `'pending'` for the probed-store path) on every accept response, rather than only including the key when it's `'pending'`.
    - **Technical:** The probed-store branch (`in_array($intent->surface_key, self::PROBED_STORE_SURFACES, ...)`) returns `{connectionId: null, surfaceKey, displayName, status: 'pending'}` at HTTP 202, while the normal `apply()` path, `acceptPayloadFinding()`, and `acceptGoogleListing()` all return `{connectionId, surfaceKey, displayName}` with no `status` key at all. A client has to infer "done" from the mere absence of the field rather than reading a consistent state field, which is avoidable drift within the same logical action on the same controller.
    - **Plain English:** Sometimes the confirmation message includes a "still working on it" note, and sometimes it just doesn't say anything at all when it's actually already done. A consistent status field on every response would make it obvious either way.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        return $this->success([
            'connectionId' => null,
            'surfaceKey' => $intent->surface_key,
            'displayName' => $surface['display_name'],
            'status' => 'pending',
        ], 202);
        ```
        ```php
        return $this->success([
            'connectionId' => $connection->id,
            'surfaceKey' => $intent->surface_key,
            'displayName' => $surface['display_name'],
        ]);
        ```

## P0 — Must fix before any real user touches the system

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-1** · P0 — Self-serve unclaimed pre-account builds can be claimed by any authenticated stranger who knows the subdomain
    - **Where:** app/Services/PreAccount/ClaimSiteService.php:90-95
    - **Affects:** Every self-serve (non-outreach) pre-account build with no `contact_email` set — any Supabase-authenticated user who learns or guesses a live unclaimed subdomain can bind their own auth identity to that provisional professional's account, taking over the site, its scraped content, and all future dashboard access.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Require a per-build claim secret or claim token for self-serve builds too, not only outreach builds — e.g. mint a `ClaimTokenIssuer` token at build-creation time and require it (or an equivalent ownership signal such as a verified email captured during the build flow) on `claim()`.
        - Close the gap at the CLAIM step per the project's own stated remediation direction — do not reintroduce a read-side "Dark Until Claimed" gate (reverted 2026-08-25 on owner decision because it defeats the pre-claim demo).
        - Add a regression test asserting a self-serve unclaimed build with empty `contact_email` cannot be claimed by an arbitrary second Supabase identity.
    - **Technical:** `claim()` gates claim eligibility with `if ($build->isOutreach() && $contactEmail === '' && ! $tokenOk) { throw ... CLAIM_NOT_INVITED }` — this throw fires ONLY for outreach builds. A self-serve build (the default `built_via` for the site-first signup flow) with no `contact_email` set has no ownership check at all: any caller who can produce a verified Supabase JWT and knows the target subdomain reaches the `$professional->auth_user_id = $uid` write with nothing standing between them and account takeover. This is not a new discovery — it is the documented, currently-live reopening of `#SEC-3` described in this repo's own `CLAUDE.md`: a "Dark Until Claimed" gate that closed this exact hole was shipped (`ee1c22784`, 2026-08-24) and then **reverted 2026-08-25 on owner decision** because it defeated the pre-claim demo, with the note that the premise "is therefore live again, and must be closed at the CLAIM step (ownership proof), not the read step." No subsequent commit in the current history closes it at the claim step.
    - **Plain English:** When someone builds a demo website for a business before that business has signed up, the site sits at a guessable web address waiting to be claimed by its rightful owner. Right now, for most of these demo sites, anyone who can log in — not just the actual business — can walk up and claim it first, simply by knowing the address. It's like leaving a house's title deed on the porch and giving it to whoever picks it up first, rather than checking ID. The team already identified and even shipped a fix for this once, then had to revert it for a different product reason — the underlying gap was never re-closed a different way.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $tokenOk = $this->tokens->matches($build, $claimToken);

        $contactEmail = trim((string) $build->contact_email);
        if ($build->isOutreach() && $contactEmail === '' && ! $tokenOk) {
            throw new RuntimeException('CLAIM_NOT_INVITED');
        }
        ```

## P1 — Fix before pilot launch

- [x] **#W2-SEC-2** · P1 — Manual content-library video upload has no byte-sniff, unlike the sibling image path
    - **Where:** app/Http/Requests/Api/User/Content/UploadContentImageRequest.php:32-38, 44-64
    - **Affects:** The content-library video upload endpoint (`POST /content/uploads` with a video file) — any authenticated user can upload a file whose real content does not match its declared `.mp4/.mov/.webm` extension/MIME.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a video counterpart to `assertImageMimeBytes` (e.g. `assertVideoMimeBytes`) in the shared `SniffsFileMimeType` concern, checking the real bytes via `finfo` against an allowlist (`video/mp4`, `video/quicktime`, `video/webm`) before the file reaches `MediaUploadService`.
        - Call it from `withValidator()`'s `$hasVideo` branch, mirroring the existing `$hasImage` branch exactly.
    - **Technical:** `rules()` validates the video field with `'mimes:mp4,mov,webm'`, which Laravel derives from the client-declared filename/extension, not the actual file bytes. `withValidator()` calls `$this->assertImageMimeBytes(...)` only inside the `if ($hasImage)` branch — there is no equivalent call for `$hasVideo`. This is an inconsistency within the same request class: the image path already has the byte-sniff defense the house pattern (`SniffsFileMimeType`) requires, and the video path — added in the same 2026-08-27 change that turned this into a one-of image/video request — was not given the same treatment.
    - **Plain English:** When someone uploads a photo to their content library, the system opens the file and checks it's really a photo before storing it. When someone uploads a video, the system only checks the file's name ends in ".mp4" — it never looks inside. A mislabeled or malicious file with a video-sounding name would sail through where a mislabeled image would not.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        'video' => [
            'sometimes',
            'nullable',
            'file',
            'mimes:mp4,mov,webm',
            "max:{$videoMaxKb}",
        ],
        ```
        ```php
        if ($hasImage) {
            $this->assertImageMimeBytes($this->file('image'), $v, 'image');
        }
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): STALE — premise wrong on both counts, reclassified. Laravel's `mimes:`/`mimetypes:` rules ARE content-sniffed, not extension-derived: ValidatesAttributes::validateMimes() ends in in_array($value->guessExtension(), $parameters), Symfony File::guessExtension() resolves through getMimeType() (finfo), and UploadedFile overrides only getClientMimeType() — verified in vendor source. Separately the video is container-validated pre-DB and pre-storage by ffprobe via VideoVariantService::probeAndValidate() (MediaUploadService.php:96-104), which is strictly stronger than a finfo mime sniff. The prescribed assertVideoMimeBytes was therefore NOT added: it would add no coverage and would risk false 422s on legitimate containers finfo reports as video/x-matroska or application/octet-stream. The ONE real defect on this path was different and is fixed: ContentController::storeUpload did not catch InvalidVideoFileException / VideoDispatchFailedException, so a rejected container 500d instead of 422/503-ing. Now mirrors UserUploadController.php:85-89.

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-3** · P2 — Bot-protection defaults ship off/null with no boot-time guard outside the `mode=enforce` case
    - **Where:** config/partna.php:2843-2846; app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php:330-342
    - **Affects:** All public unauthenticated mutation endpoints (enquiry, lead, early-access, report-signal) that rely on the bot-protection layer as their anti-automation defense.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Extend the existing `AppServiceProvider::boot()` guard so production also fails loudly when `bot_protection.mode` is `off`/absent while the intended posture is `shadow`/`enforce` (e.g. via a separate required env flag, not just silence).
        - Document/verify the deployed production value of `BOT_PROTECTION_MODE`/`BOT_PROTECTION_DRIVER` explicitly, rather than relying on institutional knowledge that "production is turnstile/shadow."
    - **Technical:** `AppServiceProvider::boot()` already contains a fail-loud guard for the *inconsistent* configuration (`mode=enforce` with an absent driver) — but that guard is silent whenever `mode` is `off` or `shadow`, which is also the documented config default (`BOT_PROTECTION_DRIVER=null`, `BOT_PROTECTION_MODE=off`). A production deploy that simply omits `BOT_PROTECTION_MODE`/`BOT_PROTECTION_DRIVER` boots clean with bot protection fully inert and no operator signal — the existing guard only catches the half-flipped case, not the fully-defaulted one.
    - **Plain English:** There's a security camera meant to watch the public sign-up forms for bots. The system does check for one broken configuration (camera "on" with no film in it) and refuses to start in that case — but it does not check for the camera simply being switched off, which is also the out-of-the-box setting. A deploy that forgets to explicitly turn the camera on ships silently unprotected.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        'driver' => env('BOT_PROTECTION_DRIVER', 'null'),       // null | turnstile | hcaptcha | fake
        'mode' => env('BOT_PROTECTION_MODE', 'off'),          // off | shadow | enforce
        ```
        ```php
        if (config('partna.bot_protection.mode') === 'enforce' && $botDriverAbsent) {
            $botMisconfig = 'BOT_PROTECTION_MODE=enforce requires a real BOT_PROTECTION_DRIVER (got "null") — nothing would be verified.';

            if (app()->isProduction()) {
                throw new \RuntimeException($botMisconfig);
            }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-4** · P2 — Public newsletter-subscribe rate limiter keys on the raw plaintext email
    - **Where:** app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php:777-793
    - **Affects:** Redis cache-key storage for the `public-subscribe` limiter — every subscribe attempt persists the visitor's raw email address as a cache key.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Hash the lowercased email with `hash('sha256', $email)` before using it in the `by()` key, matching the sibling `early-access` limiter's already-shipped pattern.
    - **Technical:** The `public-subscribe` limiter's per-hour bucket keys directly on `"email:{$email}"` where `$email = strtolower((string) $request->input('email', ''))` — the raw address, not a hash. The `early-access` limiter three definitions above it in the same file already hashes: `$emailKey = $email !== '' ? hash('sha256', $email) : 'unknown';`. This is an inconsistency within the same class, not a hypothetical: the fix pattern already exists and is proven in production for a near-identical limiter.
    - **Plain English:** To stop spam, the system remembers who has recently tried to subscribe. For most sign-up forms it remembers a scrambled fingerprint of the email address. For this one form, it writes the actual email address in plain text into that memory instead — a small, avoidable exposure inside internal infrastructure.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $email = strtolower((string) $request->input('email', ''));
        $key = $request->header('CF-Connecting-IP') ?? $request->ip();

        return [
            Limit::perMinute((int) config('partna.throttle.public_subscribe_per_minute', 5))->by((string) $key)->response(function () {
                return response()->json(['message' => 'Too many subscription attempts. Please wait before trying again.'], 429);
            }),
            Limit::perHour((int) config('partna.throttle.public_subscribe_per_hour_email', 12))->by($email !== '' ? "email:{$email}" : 'no-email')->response(function () {
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-5** · P2 — Analytics-click IP backstop is segmented by client-supplied `block_id`, weakening its role as a flood backstop
    - **Where:** app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php:629-644
    - **Affects:** Public click-analytics ingest (`/analytics/click`) — the true-IP backstop bucket that is supposed to bound one client's total click-event volume regardless of what they click.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Drop `block_id` from the true-IP backstop key (keep it only on the fine-grained per-visitor key), mirroring the plain `analytics` limiter above it, whose backstop is keyed on `'ip:'.$trueIp` alone.
    - **Technical:** Both buckets in `analytics-click` include `:click:'.$blockId`, where `$blockId = $request->input('block_id', 'unknown')` is entirely client-supplied. The sibling `analytics` limiter (pageview/section-seen path, same file, ~20 lines above) keeps its true-IP backstop unsegmented (`'ip:'.$trueIp`) specifically to bound total volume from one client. Segmenting the click backstop by an attacker-controlled value means a caller rotating `block_id` values can create a fresh 120/min bucket per value, defeating the backstop's purpose as a global per-IP ceiling.
    - **Plain English:** There are two counters watching for click-spam: a fine one per visitor+link, and a coarse backup counter per network address meant to catch anyone gaming the fine one. The backup counter is supposed to count all of one visitor's clicks together — but it also splits by a code the visitor's own request supplies, so someone can make up a new code each time and never trip the backup counter at all.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $blockId = $request->input('block_id', 'unknown');
        $trueIp = (string) ($request->header('CF-Connecting-IP') ?? $request->ip());
        $visitorIp = (string) ($request->header('x-visitor-ip') ?? $trueIp);

        return [
            Limit::perMinute((int) config('partna.throttle.analytics_click_per_minute', 5))
                ->by('v:'.$visitorIp.':click:'.$blockId),
            Limit::perMinute((int) config('partna.throttle.analytics_click_ip_backstop_per_minute', 120))
                ->by('ip:'.$trueIp.':click:'.$blockId),
        ];
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-6** · P2 — `UpdateSiteRequest.settings` accepts unknown nested keys with no closed allowlist
    - **Where:** app/Http/Requests/Api/User/Site/UpdateSiteRequest.php:29
    - **Affects:** Authenticated site-update path (`PATCH /api/professional/site`) — a client can submit arbitrary extra `settings.*` keys alongside the validated ones.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Close the `settings` container to a known key allowlist (e.g. `Rule::allowedKeys` or an explicit array-shape validator) so unrecognized top-level `settings.*` keys are rejected rather than silently persisted via `$request->validated()`.
    - **Technical:** `'settings' => ['sometimes', 'array']` only asserts the value is an array; every specific `settings.*` sub-rule (privacy, booking_mode, etc.) validates known keys, but Laravel's `validated()` returns the WHOLE `settings` array including any keys that carry no rule at all. A client can attach an arbitrary `settings.anything` payload (including large strings) that reaches `site.sites.settings` (JSONB) unconstrained.
    - **Plain English:** The settings form checks a specific list of known fields, but nothing stops someone from tacking on extra, made-up fields the form never asked for — those get saved anyway. It's a form with named boxes to fill in, but no wall around the page, so someone can scribble extra boxes in the margins and have them filed too.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        'settings' => ['sometimes', 'array'],
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-7** · P2 — `UpsertWorkplaceRequest.opening_hours` accepts an unbounded, unvalidated array shape
    - **Where:** app/Http/Requests/Api/User/Site/UpsertWorkplaceRequest.php:62-65
    - **Affects:** Authenticated workplace-upsert path — the structured hours field can carry arbitrarily large or malformed content before persistence.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Define a concrete shape for `opening_hours` (allowed weekday-slug keys, bounded `{open,close}` HHMM entries per day, a cap on entries per day) rather than accepting any array.
    - **Technical:** `'opening_hours' => ['nullable', 'array']` imposes no constraint on keys, nesting, or value shape, unlike the same request's other structured fields (all string-typed with explicit `max:` bounds). Same root-cause class as `#SEC-6` — an accepted-but-unvalidated JSONB container on an authenticated write path.
    - **Plain English:** The "what hours are you open" field is supposed to hold a short weekly schedule, but the system will accept literally anything shaped like a list — there's no check that it's actually seven days with sensible times, so a much larger or garbled blob could be saved instead.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // Structured per-day opening hours. Keys are weekday slugs; each maps
        // to a list of {open,close} HHMM entries. Shape owned by the workplace
        // editor + the Google hours mapper — validated loosely here.
        'opening_hours' => ['nullable', 'array'],
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-8** · P2 — Public analytics ingest's Origin check is unforgeable only for browser callers; a scripted client can still forge cross-tenant events
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/PublicSite/AnalyticsController.php:456-480
    - **Affects:** Every public site's analytics integrity against a non-browser caller — a scripted client can POST fabricated pageview/click/session events attributed to any known `site_id`.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Consider a lightweight HMAC-signed beacon token (minted server-side into the page payload, verified on ingest) for a stronger authenticity signal than Origin alone, since the code's own docblock already identifies this as the remaining gap ("A genuine server-to-server caller must be gated by a shared secret or signed request").
        - If the residual risk (analytics pollution, not data leakage) is accepted as-is, document that decision explicitly rather than leaving it implicit.
    - **Technical:** `originAllowed()` correctly defeats browser-JS-based forgery (Origin cannot be set by page script) and — per its own docblock — already closed two prior rounds of this exact class of finding (the `site_id`-only IDOR fixed under "SEC-1", and the Referer-fallback removed under "SEC-3", 2026-08-24). What remains, and is explicitly acknowledged in the code's own comments rather than hidden, is that a non-browser HTTP client can set the `Origin` header to any value it likes, so Origin alone provides no authenticity guarantee against a scripted caller who already knows a target `site_id` (itself a value the code notes is "exposed in public page payloads"). The consequence is analytics-data pollution under the victim's `user_id`/`site_id`, not data leakage or account takeover, and volume is still bounded by the `analytics`/`analytics-click` rate limiters.
    - **Technical (fix path):** the `#SEC-1`/`#SEC-3` history shows the team has iterated on this boundary twice already and explicitly named the remaining gap in-code; this finding tracks that acknowledged residual rather than a fresh discovery.
    - **Plain English:** A real visitor's browser can't lie about which website it came from, and the system checks that honestly. But a script running outside a browser can simply claim to be any website it wants, since nothing but an honor-system header stops it. Someone running such a script could write fake visit records into another professional's stats — annoying and misleading, but it can't steal or expose anyone's private data.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // When Origin is absent: rejected. Every legitimate caller is the sitepage's own
        // client-side beacon, and every ingest route is a POST, on which a browser always
        // emits Origin. Referer was accepted here until #SEC-3 (2026-08-24) — a non-browser
        // caller can set it as freely as any other header, so it authenticated nothing. A
        // genuine server-to-server caller must be gated by a shared secret or signed
        // request, never by public identifiers.
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-9** · P2 — Public analytics resolution distinguishes "invalid site_id" (422) from "unpublished site" (404), a minor existence side-channel
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/PublicSite/AnalyticsController.php:404-432
    - **Affects:** Unauthenticated callers submitting a `site_id` to the public analytics endpoints.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Return 404 uniformly for both a non-resolving `site_id` and an unpublished/private site on this public endpoint, per the platform's own 403-vs-404 doctrine ("404 in both cases; never leak existence on public endpoints").
    - **Technical:** `resolvePublishedSite()` returns 422 specifically when `site_id` was supplied but resolution failed, and 404 when resolution succeeded but the site fails the publish gate — a status-code side channel distinguishing "this UUID doesn't exist" from "this UUID exists but is private." Impact is limited because `site_id` is not itself a secret (the code's own comment notes it is exposed in public page payloads, so learning "it exists" rarely tells an attacker something they didn't already know from having the ID at all), but it is a doctrine deviation on a public endpoint.
    - **Plain English:** Ask this endpoint about a made-up ID and it says "bad request." Ask about a real ID for a site that's private, and it says "not found" instead. That tiny difference in wording lets someone tell real IDs apart from fake ones just by the response — a minor information leak given the ID isn't very secret to begin with.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if (! $site) {
            $status = ! empty($data['site_id']) ? 422 : 404;
            $error = $this->error('Site not found', $status);

            return null;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-10** · P2 — Analytics `x-visitor-ip` header is trusted from any direct caller, not only the intended Cloudflare proxy
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/PublicSite/AnalyticsController.php:523-528
    - **Affects:** IP-hash generation, dedup-identifier fallback, and geo detection on analytics events for every public site.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Only honor `x-visitor-ip` when the request is verified to have arrived via the trusted `/t/*` proxy path (e.g. a shared-secret header the proxy alone sets, or Laravel's `TrustProxies` chain); otherwise fall back to `$request->ip()` unconditionally.
    - **Technical:** `visitorIp()` accepts the client-supplied `x-visitor-ip` header from any caller with no verification that the request actually transited the intended `/t/*` proxy. A direct backend caller can spoof this header to manipulate the IP-hash-derived dedup identifier and geo fields recorded on the event. Impact is data-quality/dedup-evasion, not tenant boundary or data exposure, since the value is used purely as an identifier salt and geo hint, never for authorization.
    - **Plain English:** For technical reasons, one part of the traffic path forwards along the visitor's real address in a special header, and the analytics system trusts that header completely. But nothing checks that the header actually came from that trusted forwarding step — anyone calling the endpoint directly can write whatever address they like into it, skewing visit counts and location stats.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        private function visitorIp(Request $request): ?string
        {
            $forwarded = $request->header('x-visitor-ip');

            return is_string($forwarded) && $forwarded !== '' ? $forwarded : $request->ip();
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-11** · P2 — `MediaMirror` leaves three id-only DB accesses unscoped by `user_id` while its own success paths deliberately scope every write
    - **Where:** app/Services/Media/MediaMirror.php:99-101, 122-149, 469-484
    - **Affects:** `content.media_assets` refresh writes and cross-referenced `content.item_media`/`content.source_items` reads during Instagram media mirroring — only reachable if some future/other caller dispatches `mirror()` with a mismatched `($userId, $assetId)` pair.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `->where('user_id', $userId)` to the `content.media_assets` `source_url` refresh UPDATE in `mirror()` (currently the only unscoped write in the class, despite an adjacent comment claiming otherwise).
        - Scope the `content.item_media`/`content.source_items` lookups in `refreshedInstagramUrl()` by the asset's owning user where practical.
        - Scope the `mirror_attempts` read-back in `fail()` consistently with the update immediately above it.
    - **Technical:** The class explicitly implements tenant-scoped writes on both success paths under the label `#SEC-5` ("a mirror job dispatched with a mismatched pair should write nothing rather than land one user's bytes on another user's row") — but the early URL-refresh UPDATE inside `mirror()` predates that scoping and was missed: it updates `content.media_assets` by `id` alone. The comment beside the video-branch write claims "this is the only write in the class," which is demonstrably inconsistent with the earlier unscoped update, indicating the `#SEC-5` hardening pass did not audit that call site. `refreshedInstagramUrl()`'s joins and `fail()`'s attempts read-back are similarly id-only. All current call sites appear to always pass a matched `($userId, $assetId)` pair sourced from the same projection row, so this is not known to be exploitable today — it is a latent consistency gap in an otherwise-deliberate defense-in-depth pattern.
    - **Plain English:** This code stores photos and videos pulled in from Instagram, and it's careful almost everywhere to double-check "does this photo actually belong to this person" before touching it — except in three spots that were apparently missed when that safety check was added elsewhere in the same file. Nothing currently calls this code in a way that would trip the gap, but the inconsistency itself is worth closing so it can't become a real problem later.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.media_assets')
            ->where('id', $assetId)
            ->update(['source_url' => $fresh]);
        $sourceUrl = $fresh;
        ```
        ```php
        $row = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.item_media as im')
            ->join('content.source_items as si', 'si.id', '=', 'im.source_item_id')
            ->where('im.asset_id', $assetId)
            ->where('si.coord', 'like', 'instagram:%')
        ```
        ```php
        // #SEC-5: keyed on id AND owner. The id alone is sufficient in
        // practice — it came from ProjectionWriter — but this is the
        // only write in the class, it costs nothing, ...
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-12** · P2 — `SuggestionApplier::apply()` mutates `routing.source_intents` by ID alone, relying entirely on the caller having already tenant-scoped the intent
    - **Where:** app/Routing/SuggestionApplier.php:82-91, 201-207
    - **Affects:** Any future caller of `SuggestionApplier::apply()` that does not pre-scope its `$intent` lookup to `$user->id` — today's only caller does, so this is a latent hardening gap, not an active exploit.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `->where('user_id', $user->id)` to both `routing.source_intents` updates inside `apply()`, matching the tenant-scoping discipline `MediaMirror` (#SEC-11) and other id+owner writes in this codebase already use.
    - **Technical:** `apply(User $user, object $intent, array $surface)` never asserts `$intent->user_id === $user->id` before its two `DB::table('routing.source_intents')->where('id', $intent->id)->update(...)` calls. Verified: the sole live caller, `SuggestionsController::accept()`, resolves `$intent` via `findIntent()`, which DOES scope by `->where('user_id', $userId)` before handing the row to `apply()` — so the current call path is safe. The gap is that `apply()` itself provides no such guarantee, meaning any future caller (or a refactor that reuses this method with a differently-sourced intent) would silently inherit a tenant-boundary bug.
    - **Plain English:** This function trusts that whoever calls it has already double-checked the record belongs to the right person, but it doesn't check that itself. Today's only caller does the check correctly beforehand, so nothing is broken right now — but the function is one careless future change away from becoming a real cross-account bug, because it has no safety net of its own.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        DB::table('routing.source_intents')->where('id', $intent->id)->update([
            'state' => 'applied',
            'block_reason' => null,
            'connection_id' => $connection->id,
            'resolved_at' => now(),
            'updated_at' => now(),
        ]);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-13** · P2 — `ProjectionWriter::ensureContentSource()` / `accountRef()` resolve connection-scoped rows by ID alone despite `$userId` already being in scope
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php:379-399, 568-577
    - **Affects:** Content-source and platform-connection resolution during ingest projection — a same-shape gap to #SEC-11/#SEC-12, in the connector-ingest pipeline.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `->where('user_id', $userId)` to the `content.sources` lookup in `ensureContentSource()` and the `site.platform_connections` lookup in `accountRef()`, since `$userId` is already an available parameter in both methods.
    - **Technical:** Both lookups key purely on `connection_id`/`id`, which are UUIDs sourced from the same internally-generated ingest pipeline data, not external input — so this is not currently attacker-reachable. It is the same root-cause pattern as #SEC-11 and #SEC-12: an available tenant-scope parameter left unused on an id-only lookup, worth closing for consistency with this codebase's stated tenant-isolation discipline.
    - **Plain English:** Two lookups in the background content-import pipeline check a record by its reference number only, not also by whose account it belongs to — even though the code already knows whose account it is at that point. It's not reachable by an outside attacker today, but it's an inconsistency with how the rest of the codebase is written to be safe.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $existing = DB::table('content.sources')->where('connection_id', $connectionId)->value('id');
        ```
        ```php
        $resourceId = DB::table('site.platform_connections')->where('id', $connectionId)->value('resource_id');
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-14** · P2 — Mirrored Instagram media trusts the CDN's declared `Content-Type` header instead of byte-sniffing the fetched file
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/InstagramConnectionSeeder.php:466-469, 592-597
    - **Affects:** R2 media storage and public sitepages serving mirrored Instagram photos/reels.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a `finfo`/`getimagesize`-based byte-sniff (matching the house `SniffsFileMimeType`/`ImagePixelBudget` pattern already used by `MediaMirror`) before persisting the fetched bytes, rather than trusting the CDN-declared `Content-Type` alone.
    - **Technical:** `mirrorOne()` and `attemptMirrorVideo()` both gate storage purely on `str_starts_with($contentType, 'image/'|'video/')` read from the response header. The fetch itself IS properly SSRF-guarded (host allowlist + `SafeUrlFetcher::assertSafe()` + no-redirects + byte cap), and the source is a host-allowlisted real Instagram/Facebook CDN rather than an arbitrary attacker URL — meaningfully narrowing the threat model versus a fully open upload path. However, the underlying content still originates from third-party (Instagram-hosted) user-generated media, and the file is stored and later served publicly without ever inspecting its real bytes, unlike `MediaMirror`'s own image path, which runs `ImagePixelBudget::decodable()`/`exceeds()` before storage.
    - **Plain English:** When photos and videos are copied over from a connected Instagram account, the system checks the label the Instagram server attaches ("this is a photo") but never actually opens the file to confirm. The source is a real, allow-listed Instagram address, so this isn't wide open to random attackers — but it's still one step short of the "open the box and check" standard used elsewhere in this same codebase for similar third-party content.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $contentType = strtolower(trim(explode(';', (string) $response->header('Content-Type'))[0]));
        if (! str_starts_with($contentType, 'image/')) {
            return null;
        }
        ```
        ```php
        $contentType = strtolower(trim(explode(';', (string) $response->header('Content-Type'))[0]));
        if (! str_starts_with($contentType, 'video/')) {
            $this->logVideoMirrorDrop('bad_content_type', $url);

            return null;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-15** · P2 — Uber Eats menu driver forwards an unvalidated store URL directly into third-party Apify actor input
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/UberEatsMenuDriver.php:19-22
    - **Affects:** The Uber Eats menu-scrape flow; Apify actor spend/abuse surface (not a direct Partna-infrastructure SSRF, since Partna itself never fetches the URL).
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Validate `$storeUrl`'s host against an explicit `ubereats.com` allowlist (`parse_url` + suffix check) before it reaches `buildInput()`, so a malformed or off-platform URL cannot be forwarded to the billed third-party actor.
    - **Technical:** `buildInput()` places `$storeUrl` verbatim into `startUrls[0].url` with no scheme/host validation. This does not fetch anything from Partna's own infrastructure, so it is not a direct SSRF against Partna — but it is a missing input-shape check on a value handed to a billed third-party scraper, which is both a cost-abuse surface (an attacker-supplied non-Uber-Eats URL burns Apify budget) and a data-integrity concern (a non-Uber-Eats URL can never legitimately map to a menu import).
    - **Plain English:** Before asking an outside scraping service to go fetch a menu page, the system doesn't check the web address it's handing over is actually an Uber Eats page. It's not sending our own servers anywhere dangerous, but it could waste money sending the outside scraper to the wrong (or a malicious) address on our dime.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        public function buildInput(string $storeUrl, ?string $address): array
        {
            return ['startUrls' => [['url' => $storeUrl]], 'includeItemCustomizations' => true];
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-16** · P2 — Website link-harvester parses fetched HTML with `DOMDocument` without `LIBXML_NONET`
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/WebsiteLinkHarvester.php:805
    - **Affects:** Link-harvest paths parsing user-supplied website HTML fetched via `SafeUrlFetcher`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `LIBXML_NONET` to the `loadHTML()` flags: `LIBXML_NOWARNING | LIBXML_NOERROR | LIBXML_NONET`.
    - **Technical:** `$html` is already fetched safely via `SafeUrlFetcher::tryFetch()`, but `DOMDocument::loadHTML()` is invoked without `LIBXML_NONET`, which by default permits libxml to resolve external entities/DTDs over the network while parsing. This is a defense-in-depth gap independent of the fetch guard — the parser itself, not the initial request, is the potential secondary network egress point.
    - **Plain English:** The system carefully checks a website's address before visiting it. But once the page's content is being read, the reading tool itself is still allowed to reach out over the network on its own if the page tries to trick it into doing so. A one-line setting change tells the reader to work entirely offline.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $loaded = $doc->loadHTML($html, LIBXML_NOWARNING | LIBXML_NOERROR);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-17** · P2 — `ShortLinkExpander` logs the full user-supplied URL, including any query-string secrets, on expansion failure
    - **Where:** app/Routing/ShortLinkExpander.php:147-150
    - **Affects:** Log/Nightwatch retention for any user who pastes a short link carrying a signed or tokenized query parameter that later fails to expand.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Redact the URL's query string before logging (log host + path only, or use an existing redaction helper if one is available for this subsystem), consistent with this codebase's stated log-hygiene discipline.
    - **Technical:** The failure handler in `resolveFinal()` logs `['url' => $url, 'error' => $e->getMessage()]` verbatim. `$url` is user-controlled input (a pasted short link) and may carry sensitive query parameters (access tokens, signed-link params) that a third-party short-link service embeds. This fires only on the failure path, and only for links that fail to expand, bounding exposure — but it is a real log-hygiene gap against the codebase's PII/secret-in-logs discipline.
    - **Plain English:** If a shortened link someone pastes into their site happens to carry a private ticket or token in its address, and that link fails to expand, the system writes the whole address — ticket included — into its internal logs rather than just noting that expansion failed.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        Log::warning('routing.shortlink_expand_failed', [
            'url' => $url,
            'error' => $e->getMessage(),
        ]);
        ```

## P3 — Nice to have

- [ ] **#W2-SEC-18** · P3 — Linktree `__NEXT_DATA__` payload is parsed without the sibling liinks.co path's payload-size guard
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/LinkInBioInlinePayloadReader.php:103-113
    - **Affects:** Link-in-bio import parsing of fetched Linktree pages.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Apply the same `MAX_PAYLOAD_BYTES` check used in `objectAfterAssignment()` (liinks.co path) to the `linktree()` method before running the regex capture and `json_decode`.
    - **Technical:** The liinks.co path enforces `MAX_PAYLOAD_BYTES` (4 MB) before its brace-matching walk; the `linktree()` method has no equivalent check before its `preg_match`/`json_decode` of the `__NEXT_DATA__` script block. Actual severity is bounded: `$body` already arrives pre-capped by `SafeUrlFetcher`'s own response byte cap upstream (default 10 MB), so this is an inconsistency between two sibling code paths rather than a genuinely unbounded input.
    - **Plain English:** One of the two similar website-reading paths in this file has a safety limit on how much text it will process at once; the other, nearly identical path doesn't have that same limit, even though it easily could. The overall system already caps how big a page it will download in the first place, so the practical risk is small — it's a leftover inconsistency, not an open door.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        private const MAX_PAYLOAD_BYTES = 4 * 1024 * 1024;
        ```
        ```php
        if (! preg_match('~<script[^>]*id="__NEXT_DATA__"[^>]*>(.*?)</script>~s', $body, $m)) {
            return null;
        }

        $doc = json_decode($m[1], true);
        ```
