    - **Why grouped:** Same root-cause pattern across three files (id-only DB lookup/update where a tenant-scope parameter is already available) — one mechanical `->where('user_id', ...)` addition per site, reviewable together.
    - **Model:** follow the file's Execution policy (Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet).

- **Bundle 2 — Public analytics ingest hardening:** #SEC-8, #SEC-9, #SEC-10
    - **Why grouped:** Same file (`AnalyticsController.php`), same subsystem (public unauthenticated ingest trust boundary).
    - **Model:** follow the file's Execution policy (Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet).

- **Bundle 3 — Site settings Form Request hardening:** #SEC-6, #SEC-7
    - **Why grouped:** Same pattern (unbounded/unclosed JSONB container accepted by a Form Request) on the two sibling site-settings request classes.
    - **Model:** follow the file's Execution policy (Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet).

- **Bundle 4 — Outbound platform-scraper hardening:** #SEC-14, #SEC-15, #SEC-16, #SEC-18
    - **Why grouped:** Same subsystem (`app/Services/Platforms/`), same theme (fetched/third-party content trusted at face value — content-type, URL shape, parser network access, payload size).
    - **Model:** follow the file's Execution policy (Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet).

- **Bundle 5 — Rate-limiter key hygiene:** #SEC-4, #SEC-5
    - **Why grouped:** Same file (`AppServiceProvider::configureRateLimiting()`), same fix shape (adopt an already-proven sibling-limiter pattern).
    - **Model:** follow the file's Execution policy (Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet).

- **Bundle 6 — Log hygiene:** #SEC-17
    - **Why grouped:** Single small fix, no natural pairing; bundle-of-one for scheduling purposes.
    - **Model:** follow the file's Execution policy (Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet).

- **Bundle 7 — Bot-protection boot guard:** #SEC-3
    - **Why grouped:** Single small config/boot fix, no natural pairing.
    - **Model:** follow the file's Execution policy (Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet).

## Standalone — do NOT bundle

- **#SEC-1 — Self-serve unclaimed builds claimable without ownership proof** · P0, auth/account-takeover boundary — requires its own plan, sign-off, and careful interaction with the `ClaimTokenIssuer`/claim-flow spec before implementation.
- **#SEC-2 — Manual video upload missing byte-sniff** · touches the upload trust boundary directly (auth-adjacent content-safety gate) — run with its own plan + review rather than folded into a bundle.

## P1 — Fix before pilot launch

- [x] **#W2-LIFE-1** · P1 — Public payload builder crashes for early-setup accounts with no Site row
    - **Where:** app/Services/PublicSite/IndividualProfilePayloadBuilder.php:653 (`buildPublicConfig()`)
    - **Affects:** Any pre-account / early-setup individual resolved via `IndividualProfileController::show` before their `site.sites` row exists — a cache-miss request for that handle 500s instead of rendering.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Change `$pro->site->shop_link_mode` to `$pro->site?->shop_link_mode ?? Site::DEFAULT_SHOP_LINK_MODE`, matching the null-safe style already used two lines later for `displayGalleryPage`.
        - Add a regression test for `build($pro, null)` / a `$pro` whose `site` relation resolves to null.
    - **Technical:** `IndividualProfileController::show()` explicitly resolves `$site = $resolved['site_id'] ? Site::find($resolved['site_id']) : null;` before calling `$this->builder->build($pro, $site)`, and `build()`'s own `cacheKey()` docblock documents this case: "Falls back to the pro's updated_at for early-setup individuals without a Site row." `buildPublicConfig(User $pro)` is called from `build()` and unconditionally dereferences `$pro->site->shop_link_mode` — the `site` *relation* on `$pro`, not the `$site` parameter — with no null-safe operator. When `$pro->site` resolves to null (a genuinely supported state per the method's own sibling code), this throws a fatal `Error` and the whole payload build fails, taking the cache-miss request down with it. The very next line (`$pro->site?->settings[...]`) already uses `?->`, confirming this line is the one gap in an otherwise null-tolerant method.
    - **Plain English:** Some visitors land on a site that's still being set up — the person hasn't finished building their page yet, so there's no "site" record in the database, just the account itself. One line of code assumes every account already has a finished site and crashes when it doesn't, breaking the page for that visitor. It's like a receptionist handing out room keys for a room that hasn't been built yet — the desk just jams instead of saying "come back soon."
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $config['shopLinkMode'] = $pro->site->shop_link_mode ?? Site::DEFAULT_SHOP_LINK_MODE;
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): STALE — false premise, and it was false at the 9567e3057 scan sha too (the line is byte-identical there). `$pro->site->shop_link_mode ?? Site::DEFAULT_SHOP_LINK_MODE` cannot throw: `??` evaluates its left operand in isset context, so a null property chain yields the default with no warning and no Error (verified by execution). The lazy-load path is safe too — Model::preventLazyLoading is armed outside production, but Builder::hydrate() only arms it per-model when count($items) > 1 (vendor/laravel/framework Eloquent/Builder.php:471-473), and the controller hydrates $pro with a single-row User::find(). IndividualProfileControllerTest passes on the full HTTP path with a persisted user. No fix required. Residual, logged as a nit not a defect: $pro->site is one avoidable lazy query on the hot path when build() already holds $site.

- [x] **#W2-LIFE-2** · P1 — `SourceReconciler::applyIntent()` creates a link connection with a bare read-then-insert; no lock, no `insertOrIgnore`, no typed unique-catch
    - **Where:** app/Routing/SourceReconciler.php:475-507 (`applyIntent()`)
    - **Affects:** Automatic link-routing writes for any user whose scanned links resolve concurrently to the same `(user, surface_key, resource_id)` — e.g. an Instagram bio-scan and a link-in-bio unroll surfacing the same URL in the same sweep, or a paste racing a scheduled scan.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Replace the `first()` / `new IntegrationConnection([...])` / `save()` sequence with a `lockForUpdate` re-read inside the transaction plus a typed `catch (UniqueConstraintViolationException $e)` that re-reads and returns the winner's id, per house doctrine.
        - `reconcile()`'s enclosing `DB::transaction()` already documents "NO catch in here: letting a failure roll back the whole transaction IS the fix" for the intent write — the connection race needs its own resolution *inside* that transaction so a benign duplicate doesn't blow away the whole intent write.
    - **Technical:** `reconcile()` wraps the intent write and `applyIntent()`'s connection creation in one `DB::transaction()` (per the `LIFE-16` comment on that call site), but `applyIntent()` itself still does a plain `IntegrationConnection::query()->where(...)->first()` followed by `new IntegrationConnection([...])->save()` with no lock and no `UniqueConstraintViolationException` catch. Under Postgres READ COMMITTED, two concurrent transactions can both see no matching row and both attempt the insert; the loser raises an unhandled unique violation that propagates out through the whole `DB::transaction()`, rolling back the entire intent write and surfacing as a 500 to whatever request path triggered the reconcile. `SourceProvisioner::sync()` already fixed this exact shape with `insertOrIgnore` (referenced in the codebase as `#LIFE-14`); this call site was not brought in line.
    - **Plain English:** Two different discovery paths (an Instagram bio scan and a link-in-bio import, say) can both notice the same link for the same person at nearly the same moment. Both check "do we already have a card for this?", both see "no," and both try to create one. The database only lets one succeed — but instead of quietly using the winner's card, the loser crashes the whole operation with an error page.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $connection = IntegrationConnection::query()
            ->where('user_id', $user->id)
            ->where('surface_key', $surfaceKey)
            ->where('resource_id', $identifier)
            ->whereNull('deleted_at')
            ->first();

        if ($connection !== null) {
            return (string) $connection->id;
        }

        $connection = new IntegrationConnection([
            'user_id' => $user->id,
            'surface_key' => $surfaceKey,
            ...
        ]);
        $connection->created_by_catalog_digest = CompiledCatalog::digest();
        $connection->save();
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): FIXED — this is the SAME finding as #W1-LIFE-3 (identical file, identical lines, identical evidence, found independently by two lenses). One fix, two ticks. See #W1-LIFE-3 for the reasoning, including why the prescribed catch-inside-the-transaction was rejected in favour of a savepoint.

- [x] **#W2-LIFE-3** · P1 — `SourceReconciler::reconcile()` checks the booking/reservations XOR conflict without taking the shared cross-service lock
    - **Where:** app/Routing/SourceReconciler.php:120-127 (`reconcile()`, the `isExclusiveAuto` branch)
    - **Affects:** Users with more than one automated booking/reservations/ordering surface discovered close together (e.g. a Fresha link and a Square link both surfacing from the same bio scan) — two concurrent reconciles can both see no incumbent and both install a "primary" connection for the same exclusive class.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the `incumbentFor()` check through `applyIntent()`'s connection write in `withBookingXorLock($userId, ...)` / `withReservationsXorLock($userId, ...)` from `BuildsAutoSyncFindings`, the same shared lock every other booking/reservations producer already takes.
        - `AutoBookingConnectDispatcher` was split out specifically because `SourceReconciler` "could not take the trait" without acquiring `write()`/`resolveBookingLink()` alongside it — the fix here is a lock around the XOR *check*, not a re-import of the whole trait.
    - **Technical:** `isExclusiveAuto($routingClass)` covers `['booking', 'reservations', 'ordering']`, and the codebase's own `BuildsAutoSyncFindings::withBookingXorLock()`/`withReservationsXorLock()` exist precisely because "one auto-connection per routing class" cannot be enforced by a per-platform unique index — it spans multiple platforms sharing one slot. Every other producer of a booking/reservations auto-connection (`GoogleBusinessAutoSync::seedBooking`/`seedReservation`, `ReservationsController`) takes that lock before its own check-then-write. `SourceReconciler::reconcile()` — documented in the trait itself as "a third producer... for unclaimed pre-account sites" — calls `incumbentFor()` and then `applyIntent()` with no lock at all. Two concurrent placements for different booking-class surfaces on the same user can both pass the incumbent check before either writes, installing two connections both marked `is_primary`.
    - **Plain English:** The rule is "you can only have one automatic booking link at a time" — if a second one shows up, the system is supposed to ask you which one to keep. But this code checks "is there already one?" without holding the lock every other part of the app uses for that exact question, so two booking links discovered at nearly the same moment can both slip through and both become "the" booking link.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($verdict === Verdict::Place && $this->isExclusiveAuto($routingClass) && ! $context->isDirectRequest()) {
            $incumbent = $this->incumbentFor($user, $routingClass, $placement->surfaceKey, $identifier, $aliasConnectionId);
            if ($incumbent !== null) {
                $verdict = Verdict::Hold;
                $blockReason = 'conflict';
                $conflictId = $incumbent;
            }
        }
        ```
    - Resolution (2026-08-28): FIXED, but the stated impact was WRONG. The audit claims the missing lock lets two connections both be marked is_primary; that cannot happen — idx_platform_connections_primary_per_class is UNIQUE on (user_id, routing_class) WHERE is_primary AND deleted_at IS NULL and has blocked it since 20260727110008. The REAL bug is worse in a different way: the loser's forceFill([is_primary => true])->save() raised an UNCAUGHT 23505 INSIDE the LIFE-16 transaction, so the whole reconcile rolled back into a 500/failed job and the XOR "hold it as a conflict for the inbox" semantics was bypassed entirely — two active booking-class connections, one primary. That index is also absent from the SQLite stand-in, so this arm is only provable in the PG lane. Fixed by taking the exclusive-slot lock around the incumbent check AND the transaction together (lock OUTER, transaction INNER), and savepoint-wrapping the is_primary write so a lost race resolves to "someone else owns the CTA" rather than rethrowing. Note the prescription named withBookingXorLock, which lives on BuildsAutoSyncFindings and SourceReconciler deliberately cannot take; isExclusiveAuto() also covers THREE classes, and there is no orderingXorLock — the ordering family serialises on platformConnectionLock(online-ordering), now exposed as CacheKeyGenerator::orderingFamilyLock() so the reconciler and GoogleBusinessAutoSync::seedOrdering share one source of truth. dispatchFor() stays OUTSIDE the lock closure: under the sync queue it reaches FreshaConnectFetch, which takes bookingXorLock itself, and Cache::lock is not reentrant.

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-4** · P2 — `ProjectionWriter::ensureContentSource()` find-or-create is not atomic; no lock, no `insertOrIgnore`, no typed unique-catch
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php:379-398 (`ensureContentSource()`)
    - **Affects:** `content.sources` identity for a connected connector's ingest lane, when two projection runs for the same `connection_id` overlap (e.g. a manual `ingest:project` invocation racing a scheduled run).
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a `UNIQUE` constraint on `content.sources(connection_id)` if not already present, and replace the read-then-insert with `insertOrIgnore()` + re-read, mirroring `SourceProvisioner::sync()`'s existing fix for the identical shape on `ingest.sources`.
    - **Technical:** `$existing = DB::table('content.sources')->where('connection_id', $connectionId)->value('id'); if ($existing !== null) { return ...; } ... DB::table('content.sources')->insert([...]);` — a plain check-then-insert with no lock and no unique-violation handling. Two overlapping projection runs for the same connection can both see no row and both insert, either minting two `content.sources` rows for one connection (silently splitting `source_items`/facets across two channels if no unique index exists) or throwing an unhandled `QueryException`/`UniqueConstraintViolationException` if one does.
    - **Plain English:** Two workers can both check an empty filing cabinet, both decide a folder is missing, and both create their own folder for the same account's data feed. Papers then get filed in two different places with no way to tell which is the real one.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $existing = DB::table('content.sources')->where('connection_id', $connectionId)->value('id');
        if ($existing !== null) {
            return (string) $existing;
        }

        $id = (string) Str::uuid();
        DB::table('content.sources')->insert([
            'id' => $id,
            'user_id' => $userId,
            'kind' => 'connection',
            'connection_id' => $connectionId,
            ...
        ]);

        return $id;
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-5** · P2 — `writeFacets()`/`refreshItemCaches()` run after the identity advisory lock is released, against item ids a concurrent merge may already have retired
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php:553, 809-816 (`writeManualItem()`'s docblock and the identity-lock method's own "WHAT THIS DOES NOT COVER" section)
    - **Affects:** Owner-authored item writes racing a concurrent merge/resolve for the same user+kind — the facet write can target an item id a later resolve has already retired.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Extend the identity-lock scope through the facet write, or re-validate the resolved item id under the lock immediately before `writeFacets()` runs.
        - This is explicitly flagged by the code's own docblock as one of four identity mutators the advisory lock does not cover — treat as a known, currently-open gap rather than a fresh discovery, and scope the fix alongside `ItemMerger::merge()` (also unreachable/unguarded today) if that ships.
    - **Technical:** The class's own documentation states plainly: "`writeFacets()` and `refreshItemCaches()` run AFTER this commits, against item ids a later resolve may already have merged away (pre-existing, unchanged here)." The identity advisory lock covers the source-item upsert and the resolve, but is released before `writeFacets()` executes. A concurrent merge that hard-deletes the discarded item between the resolve and the facet write leaves `writeFacets()` inserting rows against an item id that no longer exists.
    - **Plain English:** The system locks a filing cabinet while deciding which folder a document belongs to, then unlocks it before actually filing the document. If someone else merges or deletes that folder in the gap, the document gets filed into a folder that's already gone.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // WHAT THIS DOES NOT COVER. An advisory lock only serialises writers that take it, and four
        // identity mutators still do not:
        //   - writeFacets() and refreshItemCaches() run AFTER this commits, against item ids a later
        //     resolve may already have merged away (pre-existing, unchanged here);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-6** · P2 — `buildActions()` swallows a candidate `QueryException`, caching an empty actions list at full TTL without marking the payload degraded
    - **Where:** app/Services/PublicSite/IndividualProfilePayloadBuilder.php:286-291 (`buildActions()`)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage visitors hitting a cache-miss during a transient DB fault — the "actions" section silently goes empty and stays that way for the full cache TTL instead of the short degraded TTL.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Call `$this->resolver->markDegraded()` in the `catch (QueryException $e)` block before falling back to `$candidates = []`, matching the pattern the same method already applies two lines later for `actionRanksForSite()`/`lastReadFailed()`.
    - **Technical:** The candidate-fetch catch logs a warning and sets `$candidates = []`, but — unlike the immediately adjacent `if ($this->popularity->lastReadFailed()) { $this->resolver->markDegraded(); }` block in the very same method — never calls `markDegraded()`. Without that marker the resulting payload is cached at the full primary TTL, so one transient DB blip on a viral page's cache-miss can serve an empty actions section to every visitor for the rest of the TTL window.
    - **Plain English:** If the database hiccups while building the "quick actions" buttons on someone's page, the system quietly serves a page with no buttons and then locks that broken version into the cache for the normal length of time — instead of the special short "try again soon" cache the code uses elsewhere for exactly this kind of hiccup.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        try {
            $candidates = $this->candidates->forSite($pro, $site, $sections, $pools);
        } catch (QueryException $e) {
            Log::warning('sitepage.actions_candidates_failed', ['site_id' => $site->id, 'error' => $e->getMessage()]);
            $candidates = [];
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-7** · P2 — `CommerceProbeJob` catches every `Throwable` around resolution, converting transient failures into a permanent "custom link" with the job's own retry machinery never engaged
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/CommerceProbeJob.php:125-142 (`handle()`)
    - **Affects:** Every scanned/pasted commerce link — a transient HTTP/DB fault during resolution is silently downgraded to a plain link card with no second attempt, even though the job declares `$tries = 2` / `$backoff = [30]`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Narrow the `catch (Throwable $e)` to the specific expected "this link cannot resolve" failure types, and let genuinely unexpected exceptions propagate so `$tries`/`$backoff` actually engage.
    - **Technical:** `handle()`'s resolution `match` is wrapped in `catch (Throwable $e) { report($e); $resolved = false; }`, after which the method returns normally — the job is never marked failed, so its declared `$tries = 2` / `$backoff = [30]` never fires. A transient probe/DB fault and a genuine "nothing here" both end up as the same downgraded custom-link outcome.
    - **Plain English:** If the link-checker has a momentary hiccup — a slow website, a database blip — this job treats it exactly the same as "there's genuinely nothing interesting at this link," files it away as a plain link, and never tries again, even though it was built to retry twice.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        try {
            $resolved = match ($this->category) {
                'event' => $events->seedStandalone($user, (string) $this->platform, $this->url) !== null,
                ...
                default => $this->probe($generic, $brands, $products, $user, $canonicalizer, $observer),
            };
        } catch (Throwable $e) {
            report($e);
            $resolved = false;
        }
        ```

- [x] **#W2-LIFE-8** · P2 — `SuspendUserJob` has no completed-state guard; an at-least-once redelivery can re-suspend a user support already reinstated
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Moderation/SuspendUserJob.php:40-65 (`handle()`)
    - **Affects:** Moderated users suspended/banned and later reinstated by support — a redelivered enforcement job can silently re-apply the suspension.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `if ($entry->status === 'completed') { return; }` immediately after loading `$entry`, matching the guard `NotifyOnCallStaffJob::handle()` already has for the identical `ActionLogEntry` lifecycle.
    - **Technical:** `HasActionLogLifecycle::markDispatched()`/`markCompleted()` provide no idempotency of their own — `NotifyOnCallStaffJob` adds its own explicit `if ($entry->status === 'completed') { return; }` before doing anything, but `SuspendUserJob` (and its two siblings below) do not. Horizon queues are at-least-once; a redelivery after the transaction has already committed and marked the entry `completed` re-reads the case's latest decision and re-writes `status` on `core.users`, which can undo a support reversal that happened in between.
    - **Plain English:** A "suspend this account" instruction can be delivered twice by the queue system. The first delivery suspends the account. If support later reinstates them, the second, late delivery of the same instruction suspends them again — because nothing checks "haven't I already done this?"
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        public function handle(): void
        {
            DB::connection('pgsql')->transaction(function () {
                $case = ModerationCase::query()->findOrFail($this->caseId);
                $entry = ActionLogEntry::query()->findOrFail($this->actionLogId);
                $this->markDispatched($entry);
                if ($case->reportable_owner_user_id !== null) {
                    $decision = $case->decisions()->latest('decided_at')->first();
                    $newStatus = match ($decision?->decision_type) {
                        'ban_user' => 'disabled',
                        default => 'suspended',
                    };
                    User::query()->where('id', $case->reportable_owner_user_id)->update(['status' => $newStatus]);
                }
                $this->markCompleted($entry);
            });
        }
        ```

    - **Resolution (2026-08-30):** Fixed by `4c549e39b` + `9a5c0d621` — a completed-status guard (`if ($entry->status === 'completed') { return; }`) added at the top of `handle()`, re-reading `$entry->status` from fresh DB state inside the transaction, closes the redelivery gap this finding describes. `ShouldBeUnique` was also added but is NOT what closes this: `Bus::chain(...)->dispatch()` (`ModerationActionDispatcher::dispatchFor()`) never constructs a `PendingDispatch`, so `UniqueLock::acquire()` — which lives only in `PendingDispatch::shouldDispatch()`, run from its `__destruct()` — is never invoked for the first link of a chain, and even where it is invoked for a later chain link, a Horizon at-least-once redelivery re-enters `CallQueuedHandler::call()` directly and never touches the lock regardless. The completed-status guard is the entire protection here; do not read the lock as load-bearing.

- [x] **#W2-LIFE-9** · P2 — `SuspendSiteJob` has the same missing completed-state guard as `#LIFE-8`
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Moderation/SuspendSiteJob.php:42-61 (`handle()`)
    - **Affects:** Sites hidden by moderation and later restored — a redelivered job can silently re-hide a reinstated site.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Same fix as `#LIFE-8`: add the `completed` early-return guard before acting.
    - **Technical:** Structurally identical to `#LIFE-8` — `handle()` unconditionally re-derives and writes `moderation_state = 'hidden'` with no check of whether this action log entry was already completed. Same root cause, same fix, same trait (`HasActionLogLifecycle`) as its two siblings — tiered identically per house doctrine on structurally identical findings.
    - **Plain English:** Same problem as the account-suspension job, but for hiding an entire page: a duplicate "hide this page" instruction can re-hide a page staff already restored.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        public function handle(): void
        {
            DB::connection('pgsql')->transaction(function () {
                $case = ModerationCase::query()->findOrFail($this->caseId);
                $entry = ActionLogEntry::query()->findOrFail($this->actionLogId);
                $this->markDispatched($entry);
                $siteId = $this->resolveSiteId($case);
                if ($siteId !== null) {
                    Site::query()->where('id', $siteId)->update(['moderation_state' => 'hidden']);
                }
                $this->markCompleted($entry);
            });
        }
        ```

    - **Resolution (2026-08-30):** Fixed by `4c549e39b` + `9a5c0d621` — a completed-status guard (`if ($entry->status === 'completed') { return; }`) added at the top of `handle()`, re-reading `$entry->status` from fresh DB state inside the transaction, closes the redelivery gap this finding describes. `ShouldBeUnique` was also added but is NOT what closes this: `Bus::chain(...)->dispatch()` (`ModerationActionDispatcher::dispatchFor()`) never constructs a `PendingDispatch`, so `UniqueLock::acquire()` — which lives only in `PendingDispatch::shouldDispatch()`, run from its `__destruct()` — is never invoked for the first link of a chain, and even where it is invoked for a later chain link, a Horizon at-least-once redelivery re-enters `CallQueuedHandler::call()` directly and never touches the lock regardless. The completed-status guard is the entire protection here; do not read the lock as load-bearing.

- [x] **#W2-LIFE-10** · P2 — `QuarantineMediaJob` has the same missing completed-state guard, and never checks whether its raw UPDATE actually touched a row
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Moderation/QuarantineMediaJob.php:35-53 (`handle()`)
    - **Affects:** CSAM-moderation media-quarantine enforcement — a duplicate delivery re-applies the quarantine, and a missing/already-deleted media row is marked "completed" as if enforcement succeeded.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add the same `completed`-status early-return guard as `#LIFE-8`/`#LIFE-9`.
        - Capture the row count from the raw `DB::update()` and log/fail distinctly when it is zero, rather than unconditionally calling `markCompleted()`.
    - **Technical:** Same missing idempotency guard as the two siblings above, plus an additional gap: the raw `DB::update("UPDATE site.site_media SET processing_state = 'quarantined' WHERE id = ?", [$mediaId])` return value (affected-row count) is discarded, and `markCompleted()` runs unconditionally afterward — a stale or already-deleted media id is recorded as a successful enforcement action.
    - **Plain English:** Same duplicate-delivery risk as the other two enforcement jobs, plus one more issue: if the flagged photo has already been deleted or the ID is wrong, this job still stamps "quarantined — done" on the record, even though nothing was actually quarantined.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $mediaId = $entry->action_target['site_media_id'] ?? $case->reportable_id;

        DB::update(
            "UPDATE site.site_media SET processing_state = 'quarantined' WHERE id = ?",
            [$mediaId]
        );

        $this->markCompleted($entry);
        ```

    - **Resolution (2026-08-30):** The completed-state guard half is fixed here — see the shared resolution note on #W2-JOB-3 (same fix, commits `4c549e39b` + `9a5c0d621`). The "never checks whether the UPDATE matched a row" half was already closed by `5d008fb1e` (verified ancestor of the branch base), which added an `$affected === 0` check with a non-throwing, chain-safe `markFailed()` across all three jobs (`QuarantineMediaJob`, `SuspendUserJob`, `SuspendSiteJob`).

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-11** · P2 — `ProcessImageVariantsJob` dispatches an edge-cache purge and logs success on its final READY update without checking whether the write actually matched a row
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/ProcessImageVariantsJob.php:181-209 (`runHandle()`, the final `PROCESSING_STATE_READY` update)
    - **Affects:** Users deleting an image while its variants are still generating — orphaned R2 artifacts persist and an edge purge fires for content that is no longer published.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Capture the affected-row count from the final `processing_state = READY` update and skip the cache purge / success log when it is zero, mirroring the row-count check the job's own `markFailed()` already performs a few lines later in the same class.
    - **Technical:** `SiteMedia::query()->where('id', $this->imageId)->whereNull('deleted_at')->update([...READY...])` discards its result. If the row was soft-deleted mid-processing, the update silently matches zero rows, yet the code proceeds to dispatch `CloudflareCachePurgeJob` (using the in-memory `$siteMedia` loaded before the terminal-state check, which still reflects the pre-delete state) and logs `completed`. The job's own `markFailed()` method — used on the failure path — *does* check `$updated === 0` and logs distinctly; the success path has no equivalent check.
    - **Plain English:** While a photo is being processed into different sizes, the owner deletes it. The processing keeps going, saves the generated files anyway, and tells the website to refresh its cache — even though the photo is already gone. The generated files become orphaned clutter and the cache refresh is wasted.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        SiteMedia::query()
            ->where('id', $this->imageId)
            ->whereNull('deleted_at')
            ->update([
                'processing_state' => SiteMedia::PROCESSING_STATE_READY,
                'processing_error' => null,
            ]);

        try {
            $subdomain = $siteMedia->site?->subdomain;
            if (is_string($subdomain) && $subdomain !== '') {
                CloudflareCachePurgeJob::dispatch($subdomain);
            }
        } catch (Throwable $e) { ... }

        Log::info('ProcessImageVariantsJob: completed.', ['image_id' => $this->imageId]);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-12** · P2 — `GeneratePreAccountSiteJob` has no stuck-`BUILDING` reconcile guard; a worker kill after the generator succeeds re-runs generation on redelivery
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/PreAccount/GeneratePreAccountSiteJob.php:75-92 (`handle()`, the early-return guard and `STATE_BUILDING` transition)
    - **Affects:** Pre-account builds killed (deploy, OOM, supervisor restart) after the source generator has already run but before `build_state` reaches `READY` — a redelivery re-runs the scrape/generation, potentially re-billing a paid connector and duplicating side effects.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add an explicit branch for `build_state === STATE_BUILDING` that treats it as a stale/in-progress build (e.g. checks an elapsed-time threshold and either waits or transitions to a recoverable state) rather than falling through to re-run `generate()`.
    - **Technical:** The early-return guard only checks `$build->claimed_at !== null || $build->build_state === PreAccountBuild::STATE_READY`; a build sitting in `STATE_BUILDING` (set unconditionally before `generate()` runs) falls through and re-executes the generator. `ShouldBeUnique`'s `uniqueFor = 600` only prevents *concurrent* redelivery within that 600s window — a redelivery after a worker kill and the lock's expiry re-enters the method with `build_state === BUILDING` and no other signal that generation already ran, re-dispatching Apify scrapes, `GoogleBusinessEnrichJob`, `BioMentionChainsJob`, etc.
    - **Plain English:** The build tracker marks a job "in progress," then starts generating the site. If the server is switched off at exactly the wrong moment, the tracker stays stuck on "in progress" forever. When the system later retries, it sees "in progress" and — instead of recognizing that work may already be done — starts the whole build over, which can mean paying an outside vendor twice for the same scrape.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $build = PreAccountBuild::find($this->buildId);
        if (! $build || $build->claimed_at !== null || $build->build_state === PreAccountBuild::STATE_READY) {
            return;
        }
        ...
        $build->forceFill(['build_state' => PreAccountBuild::STATE_BUILDING])->save();

        try {
            $registry->for($build->source_type)->generate($user, $site, $build->source_ref, true);
        } catch (SourceGenerationException $e) { ... }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-13** · P2 — `IntegrationConnectionObserver`'s Google Business identity fold runs after commit with no try/catch, unlike every other side effect in the same observer
    - **Where:** app/Observers/Core/IntegrationConnectionObserver.php:118-121, 159-168 (`saved()` → `syncIdentityFromGoogle()`)
    - **Affects:** Dashboard connects and scheduled refreshes for Google Business connections — a malformed payload or transient DB fault inside the identity fold surfaces as if the already-committed connection save itself failed.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the `syncIdentityFromGoogle($connection)` call in `saved()` in a try/catch that reports and logs with connection/user context, matching the `enableContentInstagramAuto()`, `disconnectListingSourcedConnections()`, `clearListingSourcedWorkplaceFields()`, and `syncIngestSource()` methods in the exact same file, all of which are explicitly documented as "best-effort — a failure here must never break the connection save."
    - **Technical:** `$afterCommit = true` on this observer means `saved()` still runs synchronously in the calling request/job after the transaction commits — it is not deferred to a queue. Every other side effect in `saved()`/`deleted()` is wrapped in its own try/catch specifically so a failure there cannot propagate back to the caller of `$connection->save()`. `syncIdentityFromGoogle()` is the one exception: `app(IdentitySync::class)->applyFromGooglePayload($user, $payload->toArray())` runs with no guard, so a fault there throws out through the already-committed save, making a successful write look like a failure to whatever code path triggered it.
    - **Plain English:** Every other cleanup step this file runs after saving a Google Business connection is wrapped so a hiccup in that step can't undo the "success" of the save itself — except this one. If reading Google's business details trips up here, the caller sees an error for a save that actually already went through.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($connection->platform === Platform::GoogleBusiness->value
            && ($connection->wasRecentlyCreated || $connection->wasChanged('payload'))) {
            $this->syncIdentityFromGoogle($connection);
        }
        ```
        ```php
        private function syncIdentityFromGoogle(IntegrationConnection $connection): void
        {
            $payload = GoogleBusinessPayload::fromArray($connection->payload);
            $user = $connection->user;
            if ($user === null || $payload->name() === null) {
                return;
            }
            app(IdentitySync::class)->applyFromGooglePayload($user, $payload->toArray());
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-14** · P2 — `SuggestionApplier::apply()`/`applyDirect()` use the same unlocked read-then-create connection pattern as `#LIFE-2`
    - **Where:** app/Routing/SuggestionApplier.php:35-61 (`applyDirect()`), `apply()` (`~70+`)
    - **Affects:** The suggestions-inbox "Keep/Replace" accept flow and the standing Google-listing OpenTable suggestion — concurrent accepts, or an accept racing an auto-routing write, throws an unhandled unique violation instead of reusing the row.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Apply the same fix as `#LIFE-2`: `insertOrIgnore`/lock + typed `UniqueConstraintViolationException` catch, re-reading and returning the existing row on conflict.
    - **Technical:** Both `applyDirect()` and `apply()` perform `IntegrationConnection::query()->where('user_id', ...)->where('surface_key', ...)->where('resource_id', ...)->first()` then construct-and-save a new row with no lock and no unique-catch — the identical shape confirmed at `#LIFE-2`. `apply()` runs inside its own `DB::transaction()` for the demote/create/settle span, but a unique violation there still aborts that transaction; `applyDirect()` is not even transactional.
    - **Plain English:** The "accept this suggestion" action has the same blind spot as the auto-routing path above: it checks for an existing card, doesn't see one, and creates a new one — but a double-click or a background job landing at the same instant produces a duplicate-key crash instead of just using whichever card won the race.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $connection = IntegrationConnection::query()
            ->where('user_id', $user->id)
            ->where('surface_key', $surfaceKey)
            ->where('resource_id', $identifier)
            ->whereNull('deleted_at')
            ->first();

        if ($connection !== null) {
            return $connection;
        }

        $connection = new IntegrationConnection([...]);
        $connection->user()->associate($user);
        $connection->save();
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-15** · P2 — `InstagramIdentitySync::applyHandle()` allocates a handle with an unlocked check-then-write loop
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/InstagramIdentitySync.php:172-187 (`applyHandle()`)
    - **Affects:** Pre-account and first-time Instagram connects for handle-less users — two concurrent syncs for the same user can compute the same candidate handle and race on the `handle_lc` unique constraint.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the candidate loop in a transaction with `lockForUpdate()` on the user row, or delegate to a shared atomic slug/handle allocator, and catch `UniqueConstraintViolationException` to retry the next candidate.
    - **Technical:** `while (User::query()->where('handle_lc', strtolower($candidate))->exists()) { $candidate = $base.'-'.$attempt; $attempt++; }` is a classic check-then-write race with no lock. Two concurrent Instagram syncs racing to fill the same user's blank handle can both observe the same candidate as free and both attempt to save it; the loser's `$user->save()` throws an unhandled unique violation.
    - **Plain English:** Two background processes both try to give the same new account a username at once. Both check "is `@jane` free?", both hear yes, both try to claim it — and the second one crashes instead of gracefully picking the next option.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $base = Str::slug($username, '-') ?: 'user';
        $candidate = $base;
        $attempt = 1;
        while (User::query()->where('handle_lc', strtolower($candidate))->exists()) {
            $candidate = $base.'-'.$attempt;
            $attempt++;
        }
        $user->handle = $candidate;
        $user->handle_lc = strtolower($candidate);
        $user->save();
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-16** · P2 — `BuildsAutoSyncFindings::runApply()` deletes existing connections before writing the replacement, with no surrounding transaction on the social-platform path
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/Concerns/BuildsAutoSyncFindings.php (`runApply()`)
    - **Affects:** Users applying a "Change to" auto-sync finding on a social platform — if the write step throws after the delete loop, the user is left with no connection at all where they previously had one.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the remove-then-write sequence in `DB::transaction()` so a failed write rolls back the deletions instead of leaving a gap.
    - **Technical:** `runApply()` deletes matching connections via `->get()->each->delete()` and then calls `$this->write(...)`, with only the booking/reservations callers holding the shared XOR lock — social-platform callers are documented as deliberately lock-free, and neither path wraps the delete+write pair in a transaction. Any exception from `write()` (validation, observer failure, DB fault) leaves the old connection tombstoned and the replacement never created.
    - **Plain English:** "Swap my old link for this new one" first deletes the old link, then tries to save the new one. If saving the new one fails partway through, the old link is already gone and the new one never arrived — the user's page has a blank spot where a link used to be.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ((array) ($apply['remove'] ?? []) as $platform) {
            if (! is_string($platform)) { continue; }
            IntegrationConnection::query()
                ->where('user_id', $userId)->where('platform', $platform)
                ->get()->each->delete();
        }
        ...
        if (is_array($apply['write'] ?? null)) {
            $w = $apply['write'];
            $this->write($userId, (string) $w['platform'], (string) $w['resourceId'], (array) $w['payload']);
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-17** · P2 — `MenuScanApplier::resolveMenu()` races on first-menu creation; no lock, no typed unique-catch
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/MenuScanApplier.php:640-655 (`resolveMenu()`)
    - **Affects:** A user's very first menu-scan apply on a new site — a double-click, retry, or concurrent mobile+web request can both survive the `first()` check and both attempt `Menu::create()`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Use `Menu::firstOrCreate(['user_id' => $user->id], [...])` guarded by a `catch (UniqueConstraintViolationException $e)` that re-reads and returns the winning row, rather than a bare `first()`/`create()`.
    - **Technical:** `Menu::query()->where('user_id', $user->id)->first()` returning null falls straight through to `Menu::create([...])` with no lock. Two concurrent scan-apply requests for a user with no existing menu can both see `null` and both attempt to create a row, one of which throws (if `user_id` is uniquely constrained) or silently duplicates the menu (if it is not).
    - **Plain English:** Two requests to add a menu to a brand-new account's page can both check "does this account have a menu yet?", both hear no, and both try to create one — resulting in either a crash or two menus where there should be one.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $menu = Menu::query()->where('user_id', $user->id)->first();
        if ($menu !== null) {
            $menu->forceFill(['last_fetched_at' => now()])->save();
            return $menu;
        }

        return Menu::create([
            'user_id' => $user->id,
            'content_source' => $source,
            'currency' => 'AUD',
            'fetch_status' => 'ok',
            'last_fetched_at' => now(),
        ]);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-18** · P2 — `PoolResolver::reviewsOutsidePersonScope()` has no missing-schema guard, unlike the adjacent `ingestByConnection` read in the same method
    - **Where:** app/Site/Pools/PoolResolver.php:1489-1495 (`reviewsOutsidePersonScope()`)
    - **Affects:** The public review pool and its presence probe for any individual account with `reviews_scoped_to_person` enabled, in any environment where `ingest.sources` is absent or unreachable.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the `ingest.sources` join in `reviewsOutsidePersonScope()` in the same fail-open `try { ... } catch (QueryException $e) { ... }` shape the `$ingestByConnection` read a few hundred lines earlier already uses, with the same explicit "a missing ingest schema is a legitimate shape here" reasoning.
    - **Technical:** The `itemPayloads()` method's `$ingestByConnection` block is explicitly wrapped and documented as tolerating a missing `ingest` schema. `reviewsOutsidePersonScope()`, called from the same class for the same review pool, issues a raw `->join('ingest.sources as ing', 'ing.connection_id', '=', 'cs.connection_id')` with no guard at all — an inconsistency within one file on the exact same schema dependency. This matters beyond the current dev/prod schema gap noted in the codebase's own operational docs (production is currently missing the `content`/`ingest` schemas outright, tracked separately as schema reconciliation work): once schemas converge, any environment where `ingest.sources` is momentarily unreachable turns this into an unhandled 500 on the public review pool, while the sibling code path degrades gracefully.
    - **Plain English:** One part of this file already knows "sometimes this extra data table doesn't exist, and that's fine — just show no badge." A different part of the same file, checking a very similar thing for reviews, doesn't know that and crashes instead of degrading gracefully.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $rows = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.source_items as si')
            ->join('content.sources as cs', 'cs.id', '=', 'si.source_id')
            ->join('ingest.sources as ing', 'ing.connection_id', '=', 'cs.connection_id')
            ->whereIn('si.item_id', $reviewIds)
            ->whereNotNull('ing.selection_ref')
            ->whereNotIn('ing.selection_ref', ['', 'storewide'])
            ->get(['si.item_id']);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-19** · P2 — `MenuScanApplier::apply()` has no surrounding transaction across a multi-item batch, and cache invalidation only runs after the whole loop completes
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/MenuScanApplier.php (`apply()`, the item loop and the post-loop `touchSite()`/`invalidate()` call)
    - **Affects:** Owner-initiated scans applying multiple matched/new dishes — a mid-loop failure leaves already-committed writes in place but skips the cache bust entirely, serving the public sitepage stale content for the failed batch.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Move cache invalidation for successfully-applied items into a `finally` block (or invalidate incrementally per item) so a mid-batch exception still busts the public cache for whatever did land, rather than skipping invalidation entirely.
    - **Technical:** The method's own comment states "NO surrounding transaction: `writeManualItem()` manages its own boundaries," meaning each item commits independently, then `touchSite()`/`invalidate([$siteId])` runs only after the full loop finishes. An exception partway through the batch (a projector fault, a malformed scanned payload) propagates and skips invalidation for every item processed before it — including ones already committed to the database. This lands directly on the doctrine's hottest path: the public sitepage cache.
    - **Plain English:** When applying a batch of scanned menu items and one of them causes an error partway through, every item successfully saved before the error is genuinely on the menu now — but the "please refresh the public page" signal never gets sent, because that signal only fires after the whole batch finishes cleanly. Visitors keep seeing the old menu.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // NO surrounding transaction: writeManualItem() manages its own
        // boundaries and ProjectionWriter's docblock forbids nesting it.
        ...
        foreach ($items as $item) {
            ...
            $itemId = $this->writer->write($userId, $coord, $projection);
            ...
        }
        ...
        if ($updated > 0 || $added > 0) {
            $this->invalidator->touchSite(fn () => $user->site, 'menu-scan-apply', ['user_id' => $userId]);
        }
        ```

- [x] **#W2-LIFE-20** · P2 — `moderation:sla-scan` logs SLA-breach warnings but never alerts Nightwatch
    - **Where:** routes/console.php (`moderation:sla-scan` schedule)
    - **Affects:** Trust & safety operations — moderation cases approaching or breaching their SLA deadline generate no page.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Have the command `report()` (or throw for a batch summary) when cases are approaching/breaching SLA, so Nightwatch actually pages; keep `Log::warning` as supplementary context, not the sole signal.
    - **Technical:** `onFailure()` only fires when the scheduled command itself throws. The schedule's own comment states the command's job is to "log warnings for any approaching their SLA deadline" — a command that scans, logs, and exits 0 produces zero Nightwatch signal, and this is the doctrine's canonical "`Log::warning` used as a breadcrumb when an alert is needed" shape.
    - **Plain English:** This is a smoke detector that writes "smoke detected" in a logbook instead of sounding an alarm. If moderation cases are about to blow past their response-time promise, nobody gets paged to intervene.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // Scan open/triaged/under_review moderation cases and log warnings for any approaching
        // their SLA deadline. Threshold defaults to 120 min...
        Schedule::command('moderation:sla-scan')
            ->everyFifteenMinutes()
            ->onOneServer()
            ->withoutOverlapping(30)
            ->onFailure($reportScheduledFailure('moderation:sla-scan'));
        ```

    - **Resolution (2026-08-30):** WONTFIX-stale in full. Already closed by `37e7e4cb7` + `577ced921` (both verified ancestors of the branch base): `ModerationSlaScanCommand::reportBreachRisk()` (`app/Console/Commands/Moderation/ModerationSlaScanCommand.php:82-94`) already wraps a `ModerationSlaBreachRiskException` in `ThrottledReport::once()`, fails loud on a cache fault, and keys its cooldown on the max severity band — so Nightwatch is in fact paged. This finding's Evidence block showed only the `Schedule::command()` registration in `routes/console.php`, not the command body where the fix actually landed.

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-21** · P2 — Cloudflare KV subdomain routing backstop only runs weekly
    - **Where:** routes/console.php (`partna:backfill-subdomain-kv` schedule)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage edge resolution for any handle whose KV write silently failed (a DB rename or new-handle write whose `SyncSubdomainToKvJob` exhausted retries without paging, or a queue-level loss) — direct-subdomain routing can stay wrong for up to a week.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Run `partna:backfill-subdomain-kv` daily at an off-peak hour rather than weekly, and log a distinguishing count when the run actually repairs a divergence (so the reconcile itself is observable, not just its schedule).
    - **Technical:** `SyncSubdomainToKvJob` is the only normal KV writer for `<handle>.partna.au` routing; this weekly command is the sole reconcile/backstop for entries that diverged from `site.sites`/`core.users` due to a failed or lost write. The doctrine calls for a *daily* reconcile for state that transitions in response to an external write. Given the hottest path in this system is public sitepage resolution via this exact KV, a week of staleness on a rename is a meaningful gap even though the primary write path has its own retries.
    - **Plain English:** This is like changing someone's street address but only telling the post office once a week. If the immediate change gets lost somewhere, their mail can bounce for up to seven days before the weekly catch-up run notices and fixes it.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        Schedule::command('partna:backfill-subdomain-kv', ['--all', '--queue'])
            ->weeklyOn(0, '04:00') // Sunday 04:00 UTC — off-peak for AU/NZ
            ->onOneServer()
            ->withoutOverlapping(120)
            ->description('Weekly resync of Cloudflare KV subdomain routing entries')
            ->onFailure($reportScheduledFailure('backfill-subdomain-kv'));
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-22** · P2 — Welcome-email dispatch failure in `ClaimSiteService::claim()` is only `Log::warning`'d, unlike every other post-commit step in the same method
    - **Where:** app/Services/PreAccount/ClaimSiteService.php:205-214 (`claim()`, the welcome-email block)
    - **Affects:** Newly claimed users who never receive their welcome/activation email if the mail queue or provider is degraded — nobody is paged.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `report($e);` alongside the existing `Log::warning`, matching the `afterClaim()` helper's own pattern two dozen lines above (`Log::warning(...); report($e);`), which every other post-commit step in this same method already routes through.
    - **Technical:** Every other post-commit side effect in `claim()` — cache invalidation, KV sync, Google Business re-enrich, edge purge, cache warm — routes through the `afterClaim()` helper, whose docblock explicitly states "`report()` keeps it visible in Nightwatch rather than silent." The welcome-email block sits just below that same method but is hand-rolled with only `Log::warning`, breaking the file's own established convention for exactly this class of failure.
    - **Plain English:** Every other "after the account is created" step in this file is set up so a hiccup gets reported and someone gets paged. The one exception is the welcome email — if sending it fails, the system just writes a note nobody reads, so a mail-provider outage can silently mean nobody who signs up ever hears from Partna.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        try {
            Mail::to($email)->queue(new WelcomeMail($email, (string) $result['site']->subdomain));
        } catch (\Throwable $e) {
            Log::warning('claim.welcome_mail_failed', ['user_id' => $result['professional']->id, 'error' => $e->getMessage()]);
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-23** · P2 — `BioIntelligence` AI-call failures are only `Log::warning`'d, never reported
    - **Where:** app/Services/Profile/BioIntelligence.php:117-139 (`callModel()`)
    - **Affects:** All pre-account Instagram builds that rely on DeepSeek for enriched identity — a sustained vendor outage degrades every new build to the deterministic parser with no operator visibility.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `report($e);` in the `catch (Throwable $e)` block, and consider `report()` for non-2xx responses whose status indicates a server-side vendor fault.
    - **Technical:** The method deliberately degrades gracefully by design (its own docblock: "When the model is unavailable the deterministic PersonNameParser answer stands unchanged") — that fallback is correct. But the *only* signal of the outage is `Log::warning`, which per doctrine does not reach Nightwatch. A sustained DeepSeek outage would silently reduce every new pre-account profile's quality for as long as it lasts, with no alert path to catch it.
    - **Plain English:** When the AI service that writes cleaned-up bios is down, the system quietly falls back to a simpler, dumber method and writes a note about it that nobody actively watches. The fallback itself is the right call — but if the AI is down for days, nobody finds out unless someone happens to notice weaker-looking new profiles.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        } catch (Throwable $e) {
            Log::warning('bio_intelligence.threw', ['handle' => $handle, 'error' => $e->getMessage()]);
            return null;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-24** · P2 — `SiteProvisioningService::createSiteWithRetry()` conflates a per-user unique-constraint violation with a taken-subdomain violation, wasting up to 30 retries on a misdiagnosed error
    - **Where:** app/Services/User/SiteProvisioningService.php:214-260 (`tryCreateSite()`, called from `createSiteWithRetry()`)
    - **Affects:** The `createSiteWithRetry()` path (used by the bootstrap/client-supplied-handle flow) during a concurrent duplicate-signup attempt for the same user.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Inspect the caught `UniqueConstraintViolationException`'s constraint name and distinguish `sites_user_unique` from the subdomain-lower unique index; on a `sites_user_unique` hit, throw immediately rather than letting the retry loop exhaust 20+10 candidates.
        - Note: the sibling `createSiteForHandle()` method already pre-checks "this user already has a site" explicitly and does *not* have this problem — only `createSiteWithRetry()`'s bare `tryCreateSite()` catch is affected.
    - **Technical:** `tryCreateSite()` catches `UniqueConstraintViolationException` and returns `null` for *any* unique violation on the insert, regardless of which of the table's two unique indexes (`site.sites` on `user_id`, or on `lower(subdomain)`) actually fired. `createSiteForHandle()` avoids this by pre-checking both conditions explicitly before ever calling `tryCreateSite()`; `createSiteWithRetry()` has no such pre-check and relies entirely on the generic catch, so a concurrent duplicate-signup race for the same `user_id` is misdiagnosed as "this subdomain is taken" and burns the full 20+10 candidate retry loop before ultimately throwing a misleading `RuntimeException('Could not allocate a unique subdomain.')`.
    - **Plain English:** If someone accidentally submits a signup twice at the same moment, the second attempt should immediately say "you already have an account." Instead, on this code path, it assumes the *username* was taken and tries 30 different username variations — all of which fail for the same underlying reason — before giving up with a confusing error.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        } catch (UniqueConstraintViolationException $e) {
            // Catch sits OUTSIDE the nested DB::connection('pgsql')->transaction() call:
            // by the time the exception surfaces here, Laravel has already rolled back to
            // (and released) the savepoint...
            // Subdomain candidate taken — caller's retry loop tries the next one.
            return null;
        }
        ```

## P3 — Nice to have

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-25** · P3 — `GoogleBusinessSourceGenerator` paraphrases `PlacesBudgetExhaustedException` instead of preserving vendor detail
    - **Where:** app/Services/PreAccount/Generators/GoogleBusinessSourceGenerator.php:56-63 (`generate()`)
    - **Affects:** Debuggability of Google Places budget exhaustion during pre-account builds.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Pass the caught exception (or its message/context) through to `SourceGenerationException::scrapeFailed(...)` rather than a fixed literal string.
    - **Technical:** `catch (PlacesBudgetExhaustedException) { throw SourceGenerationException::scrapeFailed('places budget exhausted'); }` discards whatever detail the vendor exception carried, replacing it with a fixed string — the doctrine's "verbatim vendor error capture" pattern.
    - **Plain English:** When the budget for looking up business details runs out, the system tells the rest of the app a generic "out of budget" message and throws away any specifics about why.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        } catch (PlacesBudgetExhaustedException) {
            throw SourceGenerationException::scrapeFailed('places budget exhausted');
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-26** · P3 — `GoogleBusinessSourceGenerator` and `InstagramSourceGenerator` both use `updateOrCreate` for a connection marker row with no lock or unique-catch
    - **Where:** app/Services/PreAccount/Generators/GoogleBusinessSourceGenerator.php:89-103; app/Services/PreAccount/Generators/InstagramSourceGenerator.php:83-97 (`generate()` in both)
    - **Affects:** Pre-account build generation if `generate()` is ever invoked concurrently for the same user (e.g. a stuck-`BUILDING` redelivery per `#LIFE-12` running alongside a fresh attempt).
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the `updateOrCreate` in a lock scoped to `(user_id, platform)`, or catch `UniqueConstraintViolationException` and retry the read, in both generators.
    - **Technical:** Both generators call `IntegrationConnection::updateOrCreate(['user_id' => ..., 'platform' => ..., 'resource_id' => ...], [...])`, which performs a SELECT then an UPDATE-or-INSERT with no row lock. This is the same shape flagged elsewhere in this audit; here the practical trigger is narrow — it requires two `generate()` calls for the same user racing, most plausibly via the stuck-`BUILDING` redelivery gap at `#LIFE-12`.
    - **Plain English:** Two attempts to set up the same account's Google or Instagram connection at once can collide — one succeeds, the other's write is rejected and unhandled, failing the whole build attempt.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $connection = IntegrationConnection::updateOrCreate(
            ['user_id' => $user->id, 'platform' => Platform::GoogleBusiness->value, 'resource_id' => Platform::GoogleBusiness->value],
            ['payload' => $payload, 'is_active' => true, 'last_refreshed_at' => now(), ...],
        );
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-27** · P3 — `ConnectFetchJob` writes conditional-request validators from a snapshot loaded before the (unlocked) vendor fetch
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/ConnectFetchJob.php:124-155, 225-241 (`handle()`)
    - **Affects:** A connect/reconnect racing a concurrent scheduled refresh or another writer for the same `(platform, user)` row — narrow, since `ShouldBeUnique` prevents duplicate `ConnectFetchJob`s for the same connection and the write is locked behind the same platform-wide key `ScheduledRefresh` uses.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Re-select the `IntegrationConnection` row inside the lock immediately before the final `update()`, rather than relying on the pre-fetch `$connection` instance's properties.
    - **Technical:** The vendor fetch runs deliberately outside the lock (documented as multi-second, touching nothing shared), mutating `$connection` in memory via `ConditionalContext::applyTo($connection)`. The subsequent locked write persists `payload` plus `$connection->refresh_etag`/`refresh_last_modified` from that same in-memory instance — correct for the common case, but if a different writer updated the row between the fetch starting and the lock being acquired, this write can restore stale validators over a newer one. The practical window is narrow given the job's own `ShouldBeUnique` and shared platform lock with `ScheduledRefresh`.
    - **Plain English:** The job takes a snapshot of a connection, spends a few seconds fetching from the vendor, then writes that snapshot back. If someone else updated the same connection in the meantime, this write can quietly overwrite their change — though several other safeguards in this file make that a rare coincidence rather than a routine occurrence.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        Cache::lock($key, 10)->block(5, function () use ($connection, $next) {
            $connection->update([
                'payload' => $next,
                'last_refreshed_at' => now(),
                ...
                'refresh_etag' => $connection->refresh_etag,
                'refresh_last_modified' => $connection->refresh_last_modified,
            ]);
        });
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-28** · P3 — `CommerceProbeJob`'s "deep page" rule disagrees between the `category === 'shop'` branch and the generic probe branch
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/CommerceProbeJob.php:136, 228-230, 296-300 (`handle()`, `probe()`, `isDeepPage()`)
    - **Affects:** Imported store links pre-classified as `shop` — catalog-shaped URLs (`/shop`, `/store`, `/collections/...`) are treated as questionable "deep pages" on this branch, while the generic probe path explicitly treats the identical shapes as root-equivalent.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Extract the `$catalogShaped` regex check from `probe()` into a shared helper and reuse it in `isDeepPage()`, so both branches apply the same rule.
    - **Technical:** `probe()` computes `$catalogShaped = preg_match('#^(collections(/[^/]+)?|shop|store)/?$#i', $path) === 1; $deepPage = $path !== '' && ! $catalogShaped;`. The `category === 'shop'` branch instead calls `isDeepPage()`, which only checks whether the path is non-empty — it has no catalog-shape exemption at all, so a URL like `example.com/shop` is treated as a "question" (suggest-only) on this branch but as a root-equivalent auto-connect on the generic probe branch for the identical URL shape.
    - **Plain English:** The code has a rule that says "a store's `/shop` or `/collections/all` page is really its front door, not a suspicious deep link." But that rule only applies on one of the two paths that check for it — a link pre-labeled "shop" doesn't get the same benefit of the doubt.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // probe():
        $catalogShaped = preg_match('#^(collections(/[^/]+)?|shop|store)/?$#i', $path) === 1;
        $deepPage = $path !== '' && ! $catalogShaped;
        // category 'shop' branch:
        'shop' => $this->seedStore($brands, $user, $this->url, $this->isDeepPage()),
        private function isDeepPage(): bool
        {
            return $this->acceptedIntentId === null
                && trim((string) parse_url($this->url, PHP_URL_PATH), '/') !== '';
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-29** · P3 — `GoogleBusinessEnrichJob` seeds workplace/booking side effects before the locked compare-and-swap on the connection's `place_id`
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/Platforms/GoogleBusinessEnrichJob.php:246-252, ~454-460 (`handle()`, `persist()`)
    - **Affects:** Users who reconnect a different Google Business place while an enrichment job for the previous place is still in flight — the old place's workplace/booking findings are already written and are not rolled back when the CAS abandons the connection payload write.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Move `seedWorkplaceEarly()`/`seed()` inside the same lock-guarded, re-selected-row scope as `persist()`'s CAS check, or re-verify the connection's `place_id` immediately before seeding and abort early on a mismatch.
    - **Technical:** `persist()`'s own docblock explicitly documents its CAS as "if the user reconnected a DIFFERENT place while this job ran, the re-select comes back null and we abandon rather than write a payload describing a place they no longer have connected" — but `seedWorkplaceEarly()` and `seed()` (which write workplace fields, bookings, reservations, and social findings) run well before that check, and their writes are not reverted when the CAS misses.
    - **Plain English:** While the system is quietly reading details about Place A, the user switches to Place B. At the very end, the system correctly notices the switch and refuses to save Place A's info onto the connection record — but it has already added Place A's hours, menu, and booking details elsewhere on the user's profile, and those leftovers never get cleaned up.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $autoSync->seedWorkplaceEarly($this->userId, $gbp->toArray());
        $findings = $autoSync->seed($this->userId, $enrichment, $gbp->name(), $gbp->toArray(), $this->autoConnectBooking);
        ...
        $saved = $this->persist($connection, 'success', function (IntegrationConnection $fresh) use ($findings) { ... });
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-30** · P3 — `MediaMirror` persists only a reason code on the failing row, not the verbatim vendor/store error
    - **Where:** app/Services/Media/MediaMirror.php:446-477 (`fail()`)
    - **Affects:** Later debugging of mirrored-media failures directly from the database row; the detailed error only exists in the log, which can rotate or be filtered by `LOG_LEVEL`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Persist the verbatim `$error` string (or a truncated version) alongside `mirror_last_reason`, either as a new column or folded into existing JSON state.
    - **Technical:** `fail()` logs the verbatim `$error` via `Log::warning`, but the durable `content.media_assets` row only ever records the fixed reason code (`store_failed`, `fetch_failed`, etc.) via `mirror_last_reason`. The class's own docblock elsewhere argues the row, not the log, is the durable record ("`Log::warning` does not reach Nightwatch... a line says nothing about the assets that were never attempted. The row survives all three") — but that argument currently only covers the reason code, not the specific error text.
    - **Plain English:** The permanent record for a failed media mirror says "storage write failed" but not exactly why — the specific error only lives in the log, which can rotate away or be filtered out in production.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        Log::warning('media_mirror.failed', array_filter([
            'asset_id' => $assetId, 'reason' => $reason,
            'host' => parse_url($sourceUrl, PHP_URL_HOST), 'error' => $error,
        ]));
        ...
        $update->increment('mirror_attempts', 1, [
            'mirror_last_attempt_at' => now(),
            'mirror_last_reason' => $reason,
        ]);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-31** · P3 — `IntegrationConnectionObserver::maybeRunEagerly()` has a documented, accepted double-run race on the eager-run claim
    - **Where:** app/Observers/Core/IntegrationConnectionObserver.php:440-526 (`maybeRunEagerly()`)
    - **Affects:** Paid connectors that run eagerly on first connect — a concurrent duplicate connection save can, in the narrow window described, cause one extra paid vendor run.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Given the codebase's own comment marks this "pre-existing... and out of scope here," treat as a tracked hardening item rather than a fresh discovery: close the claim-release timing gap and the `needs_eager_run` set-after-clear ordering gap the comment describes, or explicitly accept the bounded cost (one extra deferred paid run) in writing.
    - **Technical:** The method's own inline documentation states plainly: "Two gaps this does NOT close, both pre-existing... design and out of scope here: the claim is released once the winner's job finishes, so a loser that reaches `claimOne()` after that point still claims and runs; and `needs_eager_run` is set before the claim and only cleared by a qualifying outcome... so a loser that sets it after the winner already cleared it can still buy one deferred paid run." This is a genuine, still-open race, already known to the team and deliberately deferred — worth carrying forward as a tracked item rather than a new finding, and bounded in impact to at most one extra paid vendor call per race.
    - **Plain English:** Two workers can both notice a brand-new data feed needs its first run and both trigger it — the code already knows about this and has decided it's an acceptable, rare cost (one extra paid API call at worst) rather than something worth a bigger fix right now.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // (Two gaps this does NOT close, both pre-existing #LIFE-5 design and out of scope here:
        // the claim is released once the winner's job finishes, so a loser that reaches
        // claimOne() after that point still claims and runs; and needs_eager_run is set before
        // the claim and only cleared by a qualifying outcome in release(), so a loser that sets
        // it after the winner already cleared it can still buy one deferred paid run.)
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-32** · P3 — `IntegrationConnectionObserver::disconnectListingSourcedConnections()` deletes candidates in an unguarded loop with no per-item retry
    - **Where:** app/Observers/Core/IntegrationConnectionObserver.php:288-317 (`disconnectListingSourcedConnections()`)
    - **Affects:** Users disconnecting a Google Business listing — a mid-loop delete failure leaves some machine-created child connections (socials/booking/ordering) removed and others stale, with no follow-up to finish the cleanup.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Catch per-candidate rather than around the whole method, so one failing delete doesn't abort the remaining candidates; log which ids failed so a retry can target only those.
    - **Technical:** `foreach ($listingSourced as $candidate) { $candidate->delete(); }` sits inside a single try/catch around the whole method body. A throw from any one `delete()` call stops the loop, leaving later candidates un-deleted with no record of which ones were reached, and no reconcile job to finish the sweep later.
    - **Plain English:** When disconnecting a business listing, this cleans up every related card the listing auto-created — but if cleaning up one card fails partway through, the rest are simply left behind with no note of what still needs doing.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($listingSourced as $candidate) {
            $candidate->delete();
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-33** · P3 — `IntegrationConnectionObserver::enableContentInstagramAuto()` performs a raw read-modify-write on `display_settings` with no lock
    - **Where:** app/Observers/Core/IntegrationConnectionObserver.php:178-231 (`enableContentInstagramAuto()`)
    - **Affects:** A newly-created Instagram connection's auto-sync setting — narrow window, single-key strip, self-healing on the next write.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Use `lockForUpdate()` around the read-strip-write, or move the strip to a conditional SQL update scoped to the current JSON value.
    - **Technical:** Reads `$connection->display_settings` into an array, conditionally strips one key, then writes via a raw `DB::table(...)->update()` with no lock between the read and the write. Concurrent writers to the same row's `display_settings` in that window can lose one another's change.
    - **Plain English:** Two updates to the same setting at almost the same time can step on each other — one's change gets silently dropped.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $settings = (array) ($connection->display_settings ?? []);
        if (($settings[AutoSyncSetting::KEY] ?? null) === false) {
            unset($settings[AutoSyncSetting::KEY]);
            DB::connection('pgsql')->table('site.platform_connections')
                ->where('id', $connection->id)
                ->update(['display_settings' => $settings === [] ? null : json_encode($settings), 'updated_at' => now()]);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-34** · P3 — `WorkplaceObserver::mirrorIdentityFields()` performs an unlocked read-modify-write on the user row
    - **Where:** app/Observers/Core/WorkplaceObserver.php:140-182 (`mirrorIdentityFields()`)
    - **Affects:** Business-Partna accounts whose workplace fields mirror onto the public identity — narrow, since each field mirrors independently and rapid concurrent saves are uncommon.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Lock the target `core.users` row (or apply an optimistic `updated_at` check) during the mirror write.
    - **Technical:** Loads `$user`, compares each `IDENTITY_MIRROR` column, conditionally assigns dirty attributes, then calls `$user->save()` — a plain read-modify-write with no lock. Two concurrent workplace saves touching different mirrored fields can each load a stale snapshot of the other's just-written change and overwrite it on save.
    - **Plain English:** Two updates to different parts of a business profile at the same moment can each work from an outdated copy of the record, so the second save can quietly erase the first save's change.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach (self::IDENTITY_MIRROR as $from => $to) {
            if ($workplace->wasRecentlyCreated) {
                if ($workplace->{$from} === null) { continue; }
            } elseif (! $workplace->wasChanged($from)) { continue; }
            if ($user->{$to} !== $workplace->{$from}) {
                $user->{$to} = $workplace->{$from};
                $dirty = true;
            }
        }
        if ($dirty) { $user->save(); }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-35** · P3 — `FreshaConnector::fetchBookingFlow()` conflates HTTP failure, bad JSON, and hash-rotation into the same "re-pin the persisted query" diagnosis
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Connectors/FreshaConnector.php (`fetchBookingFlow()`, `pull()`)
    - **Affects:** Operators diagnosing Fresha booking-flow sync failures — a transient Fresha outage or block page produces the same runbook guidance as an actual hash rotation.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Log distinct discriminators for a non-200 status, a non-JSON body, and the confirmed GraphQL `errors` (hash-rotation) case, and only surface the "re-pin the persisted query" wording for the last one.
    - **Technical:** `fetchBookingFlow()` returns `null` uniformly for a non-200 response, invalid JSON, and a GraphQL `errors` payload — only the `errors` case logs a distinguishing warning. The caller then treats every `null` (after one retry) as "the pinned persisted query was rejected," directing operators toward re-pinning `FRESHA_BOOKING_INIT_HASH`/`FRESHA_CLIENT_VERSION` even during an ordinary Fresha-side outage.
    - **Plain English:** If Fresha's server is briefly down, this code gives the exact same diagnosis as "your access key has expired" — like a car dashboard showing "check your oil" for both low oil and a dead battery.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($response['status'] !== 200) {
            return null;
        }
        ```
