        ```php
        $decoded = json_decode($response['body'], true);
        if (is_array($decoded) && isset($decoded['errors'])) {
            Log::warning('ingest.fresha.booking_flow_graphql_rejected', [...]);
            return null;
        }
        if (! is_array($decoded)) {
            return null;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-36** · P3 — `ResidentAdvisorConnector::pull()` discards the GraphQL error body, leaving no vendor signal for diagnosing a rejected query
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Connectors/ResidentAdvisorConnector.php (`pull()`)
    - **Affects:** Resident Advisor event-sync diagnostics — a genuine schema rotation and a transient vendor error are indistinguishable in logs.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Log the bounded contents of `$decoded['errors']` (with source/identifier context) before falling back to the generic `Unavailable` reason.
    - **Technical:** `if (! is_array($decoded) || isset($decoded['errors'])) { yield new Unavailable('ra.co graphql rejected the query — the schema may have rotated', $response['status']); }` — the actual GraphQL error payload is never logged or otherwise recorded, so a typo'd provisioned slug and a genuine schema rotation both surface identically.
    - **Plain English:** When Resident Advisor explains exactly why a request failed, this code throws that explanation away and just writes "something went wrong, maybe their format changed."
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $decoded = json_decode($response['body'], true);
        if (! is_array($decoded) || isset($decoded['errors'])) {
            yield new Unavailable('ra.co graphql rejected the query — the schema may have rotated', $response['status']);
            return;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-37** · P3 — `DeezerTracksConnector::pull()` drops the vendor's structured error body from its `Unavailable` reason
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Connectors/DeezerTracksConnector.php (`pull()`)
    - **Affects:** Deezer track-sync diagnostics — missing-artist, rate-limit, and internal-fault responses all record the same generic reason.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Extract `error.message`/`error.code` from the decoded body and include it (bounded) in the `Unavailable` reason and/or an accompanying log line.
    - **Technical:** The connector correctly detects Deezer's 200-with-`{error:{...}}` shape but discards the error object, yielding only `Unavailable('deezer api answered an error body', $response['status'])`.
    - **Plain English:** Deezer sends back a specific reason ("artist not found," "rate limited," etc.), but this code only records "Deezer said something went wrong" — the actual reason is thrown away.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $decoded = json_decode($response['body'], true);
        if (! is_array($decoded) || isset($decoded['error'])) {
            yield new Unavailable('deezer api answered an error body', $response['status']);
            return;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-38** · P3 — `InstagramIdentitySync`'s `applyDisplayName()`/`applyBioIntelligence()`/`applyContactFields()` are unlocked reads-then-writes on user/workplace fields
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/InstagramIdentitySync.php (`applyDisplayName`, `applyBioIntelligence`, `applyContactFields`)
    - **Affects:** A user editing their own profile while a background Instagram refresh runs concurrently — low real-world harm since every write here is only-fill-when-blank and idempotent (self-healing on the next sync).
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Apply the same lock already used by `applySector()`/`IdentitySync::applyUserIdentityFields()` (`lockForUpdate` on the user row for user fields, on the workplace row for contact fields) to these three methods for consistency.
    - **Technical:** These three methods each read a blank field, conditionally set it, and `save()` with no lock, unlike `applySector()` in the same class, which already takes `lockForUpdate`. Because every field here is only ever filled when currently blank, the worst outcome of a lost race is a field that stays unfilled until the next sync — not data loss of an owner-authored value.
    - **Plain English:** A few identity-filling steps in this file don't use the same "lock the row while I check and write" safety the sector-setting step next to them already uses. In practice this is low-risk because these steps only ever fill in blanks, never overwrite something the owner typed.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        private function applyDisplayName(User $user, ?string $fullName): void
        {
            if ($fullName === null || ! $this->isBlank($user->display_name)) { return; }
            $user->display_name = $fullName;
            $user->save();
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-39** · P3 — `InstagramConnectionSeeder::seed()` deletes stale media files outside the platform-connection lock
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/InstagramConnectionSeeder.php (`seed()`, between the media-mirroring block and the `Cache::lock` block)
    - **Affects:** Instagram profile media artifacts on the rare occasion a manual refresh runs concurrently with a scheduled refresh for the same connection.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Move the stale-file deletion inside the same `platformConnectionLock` critical section that protects the subsequent row write.
    - **Technical:** `$stale` files are computed and deleted before `Cache::lock(CacheKeyGenerator::platformConnectionLock(...), 10)` is acquired, based on this call's own view of `$written`. Two overlapping refreshes for the same connection can each compute a different "stale" set and delete a file the other refresh just wrote.
    - **Plain English:** Two cleaners tidying the same room at once can each throw away a towel the other just put down, because only the final "update the record" step — not the "throw away old towels" step — is protected by a lock.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $written = array_filter([...]);
        $stale = array_values(array_diff([...], $written));
        if ($stale) {
            try { $this->mediaDisk()->delete($stale); } catch (Throwable $e) { report($e); }
        }
        $key = CacheKeyGenerator::platformConnectionLock($connection->platform, (string) $connection->user_id);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-40** · P3 — `FreshaWorkplaceLinker::attempt()` splits the connection create and the `place_id`/`apify_status` write into two non-transactional saves
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/FreshaWorkplaceLinker.php (`attempt()`)
    - **Affects:** Fresha auto-connect flows — if the second save fails, the connection row exists without a `place_id` and the enrichment job never dispatches.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap `updateOrCreate()` and the subsequent `forceFill()->saveQuietly()` in one `DB::transaction()`, or fold `place_id`/`apify_status` into the initial `updateOrCreate()` values.
    - **Technical:** `IntegrationConnection::updateOrCreate([...], [...])` commits first; a separate `$connection->forceFill(['place_id' => ..., 'apify_status' => ...])->saveQuietly()` follows as a second statement. A failure in the second save leaves a committed connection row with no `place_id`, and the subsequent `GoogleBusinessEnrichJob::dispatch()` never fires.
    - **Plain English:** The system writes a membership card, then tries to stamp a table number on it in a second step. If the stamping step fails, the member is checked in but the kitchen never gets their order.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $connection = IntegrationConnection::updateOrCreate([...], [...]);
        $connection->forceFill(['place_id' => $match['id'], 'apify_status' => $enrich ? 'pending' : null])->saveQuietly();
        if ($enrich) { GoogleBusinessEnrichJob::dispatch((string) $user->id, $match['id']); }
        ```

    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/FreshaScraper.php (`resolveCurrentSlug()`)

    - **Technical:** `try { $response = $this->fetcher->fetch($probe, [...]); } catch (Throwable) { return null; }` swallows every exception silently; the caller's subsequent `abort(502, ...)` gives no hint that the failure was a probe-fetch fault specifically.
    - **Plain English:** When trying to find a salon's new web address after it moves, if the attempt itself fails (not "no new address found," but "couldn't even check"), the system just shrugs and reports a generic error with no record of what actually happened.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        try {
            $response = $this->fetcher->fetch($probe, ['User-Agent' => self::SCRAPE_USER_AGENT]);
        } catch (Throwable) {
            return null;

        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-42** · P3 — `MenuScanApplier::apply()` computes new-category positions from an in-memory read with no lock
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/MenuScanApplier.php (`categoryEntry()`/the pre-read of `nextCategoryPosition`)
    - **Affects:** Concurrent scan applies for the same user that both create a new category — narrow window, cosmetic ordering consequence rather than data loss.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Derive new-category positions under a row lock scoped to the user's menu, or rely on a DB-side unique constraint on position-within-scope with a retry on conflict.
    - **Technical:** Existing categories are pre-read into `$positionByRef`/`$nextCategoryPosition` in memory; `categoryEntry()` assigns `$positionByRef[$ref] ??= ... $nextPosition++` from that snapshot. Two concurrent applies creating different new categories can both compute the same next position.
    - **Plain English:** Two people numbering new sections of a menu at the same time, without checking each other's work, can both claim the same slot number.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($this->items->categories($userId) as $category) {
            $positionByRef[(string) $category->external_ref] = (int) $category->position;
            $nextCategoryPosition = max($nextCategoryPosition, ((int) $category->position) + 1);
        }
        ...
        $positionByRef[$ref] ??= $label === self::DEFAULT_CATEGORY_NAME ? 32000 : $nextPosition++;
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-43** · P3 — `BuildState::bump()` dispatches the rebuild job outside `DB::afterCommit`
    - **Where:** app/Site/Documents/BuildState.php (`bump()`)
    - **Affects:** Any content write that calls `bump()` from inside an open transaction that later rolls back — mitigated substantially by the built-in 15s dispatch delay and the 5-minute stale sweeper as a net.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the `BuildSiteDocumentJob::dispatch($siteId)->delay(...)` call in `DB::afterCommit(fn () => ...)`.
    - **Technical:** The `site_build_state` upsert runs inside whatever transaction the caller is in, but the queued job dispatch is immediate and unconditional. A caller that rolls back after calling `bump()` still leaves a dispatched (delayed) build job in the queue, which — while the 15s delay and 5-minute sweeper reduce the practical blast radius — is not the `DB::afterCommit` pattern the doctrine calls for.
    - **Plain English:** This "please rebuild the page" signal is sent immediately, before the underlying database change is guaranteed to have actually stuck. If that change later gets cancelled, the rebuild still happens off a change that never really landed — though a short built-in delay and a periodic safety-net job limit how often this actually matters.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        DB::table('site.site_build_state')->upsert([...], ['site_id'], [...]);
        BuildSiteDocumentJob::dispatch($siteId)->delay(self::EVENT_BUILD_DELAY_SECONDS);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-44** · P3 — `YoutubeScraper::fetchUploadsFeed()` runs a synchronous, spaced retry loop on the interactive (non-deferred) connect path
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/YoutubeScraper.php (`fetchUploadsFeed()`)
    - **Affects:** Users connecting/refreshing a YouTube channel during vendor flakiness — up to ~5s of added request latency.
    - **Technical:** The code's own comment acknowledges "the interactive connect path — youtube isn't in `PARTNA_CONNECT_DEFERRED`, so this can run in-request" — and then retries with `usleep()` waits of 0.5s/1s/1.5s/2s on consecutive failures, all inside the request cycle.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `youtube` to the deferred-connect set, or cap the in-request retry to a single short attempt and move the remainder to a queued follow-up.
    - **Plain English:** When YouTube is briefly flaky, this makes the person connecting their channel sit and wait up to five extra seconds while the backend quietly retries in the background of their own request.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ([500_000, 1_000_000, 1_500_000, 2_000_000] as $delay) {
            if (! is_array($rss) || in_array($rss['status'], [200, 304], true)) { break; }
            usleep($delay);
            $rss = $this->fetcher->tryFetch($feedUrl, $headers) ?? $rss;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-45** · P3 — `YoutubeScraper` feed/channel-page fetch failures are only `Log::warning`'d, with no alert path for a sustained outage
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/YoutubeScraper.php (`fetchUploadsFeed()`, `fetchChannelPage()`)
    - **Affects:** YouTube-connected channels during a sustained vendor outage — the last-known video list/channel data stays live indefinitely with no operator signal.
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Track consecutive failures and escalate (`report()`/fail visibly) once a sustained-outage threshold is crossed, rather than relying solely on `Log::warning` breadcrumbs.
    - **Technical:** Both methods log a warning and return `null` on transport failure or a non-200/304 status; nothing distinguishes a one-off blip from a sustained vendor outage, and neither path ever escalates to Nightwatch.
    - **Plain English:** If YouTube's feed goes down for an extended period, this code just quietly keeps showing whatever was last fetched and writes an unread note each time — nobody gets told the connection has been broken for days.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($rss['status'] !== 200) {
            Log::warning('youtube.uploads_feed_failed', ['channelId' => $channelId, 'reason' => 'non_200:'.$rss['status']]);
            return null;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-48** · P3 — `WebsiteLinkHarvester` silently swallows both a missing compiled catalog and an unreachable/oversized website with no distinguishing log
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/WebsiteLinkHarvester.php (`classifyFromCatalog()`, `harvest()`)
    - **Affects:** GoogleBusiness auto-sync and link-in-bio enrichment quality — a genuinely unreachable business website and a genuinely link-free one produce the identical empty result, with zero trace of which occurred.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Log a distinct warning when `CatalogNotCompiled` is caught, and separately when `harvest()` returns empty because the fetch failed/was oversized versus genuinely finding no links.
    - **Technical:** `classifyFromCatalog()` catches `CatalogNotCompiled` and returns `null` with no log ("Never fatal on a scrape path" — correct, but unobserved). `harvest()` returns `[]` uniformly whether the fetch failed, the response wasn't 200, or the body exceeded 3MB, with no logging on any branch.
    - **Plain English:** If a business's own website is unreachable during enrichment, or if the platform's classification data is temporarily missing, this code quietly produces the same "found nothing" result it would give for a website that genuinely has no useful links — with no record anywhere of which actually happened.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $response = $this->fetcher->tryFetch($websiteUrl);
        $html = is_array($response) && ($response['status'] ?? 0) === 200 ? (string) ($response['body'] ?? '') : '';
        if ($html === '' || strlen($html) > 3_000_000) {
            return [];
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-49** · P3 — `keep-alive-ping` fully swallows every failure with no alerting path
    - **Where:** routes/console.php (`keep-alive-ping` scheduled closure)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage cold-start behavior — if this scheduled ping silently stops working (e.g. `app.url` misconfiguration), the cold-start behavior it exists to prevent can return with no operator signal.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Track a rolling failure count and `report()`/alert once it crosses a threshold, rather than swallowing unconditionally.
    - **Technical:** The closure's own comment states "Silent — keep-alive failures aren't actionable," a deliberate design decision. It is nonetheless worth a lightweight sustained-failure escalation: if `app.url` regresses or network egress breaks, every ping fails silently and the exact cold-start problem this job was built to prevent can quietly return.
    - **Plain English:** This job pings the app every minute to keep it warm so visitors don't hit slow cold-starts. If the ping itself starts failing — say, from a bad configuration — nothing alerts anyone, and the cold-start problem it was built to prevent can quietly come back.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        try {
            $url = rtrim((string) config('app.url'), '/').'/up';
            Http::timeout(3)->retry(1, 200)->get($url);
        } catch (Throwable $e) {
            // Silent — keep-alive failures aren't actionable.
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-LIFE-50** · P3 — `slugs:prune-retired` and `gdpr:sweep-stale-exports` are both scheduled at 03:35, contradicting the file's own "03:35 is free" comment
    - **Where:** routes/console.php (both schedule entries)
    - **Affects:** Daily maintenance-band contention on the single Postgres primary — minor, avoidable overlap.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Move one of the two commands to a genuinely free slot in the documented maintenance band and correct the stale "03:35 is free" comment.
    - **Technical:** The comment above `slugs:prune-retired` reads "03:35 -- the daily maintenance band is 03:00/03:15/03:20/03:25/03:55; 03:35 is free," but `gdpr:sweep-stale-exports` a few dozen lines later is also scheduled `dailyAt('03:35')`. Both use `withoutOverlapping` locks and `onOneServer`, so this is a self-contradiction in the file's own scheduling bookkeeping rather than a correctness bug, but a real, easily-fixed inconsistency.
    - **Plain English:** Two overnight cleanup jobs were both told to start at exactly the same minute, even though a comment right next to one of them claims that time slot is free.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // 03:35 -- the daily maintenance band is 03:00/03:15/03:20/03:25/03:55; 03:35 is free.
        Schedule::command('slugs:prune-retired')->dailyAt('03:35')->onOneServer()->withoutOverlapping(120)...
        ...
        Schedule::command('gdpr:sweep-stale-exports')->dailyAt('03:35')->onOneServer()->withoutOverlapping(60)...
        ```

# Scaling Antipatterns Audit — 2026-08-28

**Branch:** development
**Lens:** Scaling antipatterns: write amplification, rebuild-on-write, weak caching (bundle: write-paths, read-surface, ingest-fanout chunks)

**Source files audited:**
- `app/Jobs/Cloudflare/CloudflareCachePurgeJob.php`
- `app/Observers/Core/IntegrationConnectionObserver.php`
- `app/Observers/Core/WorkplaceObserver.php`
- `app/Services/Analytics/ActionScorer.php`
- `app/Services/Analytics/ContentFreshness.php`
- `app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Analytics/DevInsightsController.php`
- `app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php`

## Progress

- P0 Blockers: 0 of 0 complete
- P1 High: 0 of 0 complete

---

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W2-CACHE-1** · P2 — Dev-insights page scores run unbounded all-time aggregates instead of the endpoint's own windowed convention
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Analytics/DevInsightsController.php:140-156 (`pageScores`)
    - **Affects:** Any authenticated professional loading `GET /api/professional/dev-insights` for their own site; `analytics.section_views` and `analytics.link_clicks`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `->where('occurred_at', '>=', $since)` to both the impressions/dwell and clicks queries in `pageScores()`, using the same `partna.analytics.dev_insights_series_days` window `pageDailySeries()`/`itemDailySeries()` already apply.
        - Thread `$since` (or the raw `$seriesDays`) into `pageScores()`/`itemScores()` from `index()`, which already computes it.
    - **Technical:** Category (4). `pageScores()` groups `analytics.section_views` and `analytics.link_clicks` by `section_key` with only a `site_id` predicate — no `occurred_at` bound — while the sibling methods `pageDailySeries()`/`itemDailySeries()` in the same controller correctly scope to `Carbon::now()->subDays($seriesDays)`. The inconsistency means every load of this endpoint rescans a site's entire event history instead of the same 30-day window the rest of the page displays. This is a per-request cost that scales with the site's own lifetime traffic, not with the request itself — for a page that has gone viral, "why is this first" turns into a full-history GROUP BY on every open. Canonical replacement: bound the query to the same window as its sibling methods (a live query is fine at that bounded scale per the schema's `user_id`+`occurred_at` indexing).
    - **Plain English:** Every time a professional opens the diagnostics page that explains their site's ranking, one part of that page re-counts every view and click the site has *ever* received instead of just the last 30 days like the rest of the page does. For a site that suddenly gets a lot of traffic, that's an ever-growing pile of history to sift through on every single page load — it's like reading a whole ledger book cover-to-cover instead of just this month's page.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $impressions = $this->foldToPage(
            DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.section_views')
                ->where('site_id', $siteId)->whereNotNull('section_key')
                ->selectRaw('section_key, COUNT(*) as n')->groupBy('section_key')->pluck('n', 'section_key')
        );
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-CACHE-2** · P2 — Dev-insights item scores run unbounded all-time aggregates over `item_views` and `link_clicks`
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Analytics/DevInsightsController.php:207-230 (`itemScores`)
    - **Affects:** Any authenticated professional loading `GET /api/professional/dev-insights` for their own site; `analytics.item_views` and `analytics.link_clicks`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Same fix as CACHE-1, applied to both grouped queries in `itemScores()`.
    - **Technical:** Category (4) — same root cause as CACHE-1, same file, same missing bound; tiered identically. `itemScores()` groups `analytics.item_views` by `(item_type, item_id)` and `analytics.link_clicks` by `(product_id, section_key)` with no `occurred_at` predicate, in contrast to `itemDailySeries()` two methods below it, which correctly bounds to `$since`. Canonical replacement: bound both queries to the same window.
    - **Plain English:** Same problem as the page-scores issue above, but for individual products, tracks or listings — the system re-sorts every view and click an item has ever had instead of the recent window the chart next to it already shows.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach (DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.item_views')
            ->where('site_id', $siteId)
            ->selectRaw('item_type, item_id, MAX(item_title) as title, COUNT(*) as n')
            ->groupBy('item_type', 'item_id')->get() as $r) {
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-CACHE-3** · P2 — `dispatchMirrors()` enqueues one job per media asset with no batch/chain grouping
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php:2861-2900 (`dispatchMirrors`)
    - **Affects:** The `images` Horizon queue; a first sync/backfill or reconnect that surfaces many owned media assets at once (Facebook/TikTok/Instagram photo or video catalogues).
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Group qualifying asset IDs into a `Bus::batch()` per projection run (or per chunk) instead of one `MirrorMediaAssetJob::dispatch()` call per asset, so a large first-sync backfill produces one trackable unit of work instead of thousands of independent Redis dispatches inside a single PHP loop.
        - Preserve the existing per-asset `ShouldBeUnique` semantics inside the batched job so retries still dedupe correctly.
    - **Technical:** Category (2)/(5). `array_chunk($candidates, $chunk, true)` bounds only the `content.media_assets` SELECT; inside that chunked loop, `MirrorMediaAssetJob::dispatch($userId, $assetId, ...)` still fires once per asset that needs mirroring. A first sync of a large owned-media catalogue (thousands of Facebook/TikTok photos or video frames) dispatches thousands of individual jobs — and Redis round-trip work — from inside one synchronous projection-job loop, so the job's own runtime and the `images` queue's depth both scale with catalogue cardinality rather than with the size of the triggering sync event. CLAUDE.md already documents that `images` was split off from other media work specifically because of prior queue-starvation pressure from this class of fan-out — that isolation protects OTHER queues but does not remove the per-asset dispatch cost itself. Canonical replacement: chunked/batched fan-out (`Bus::batch`).
    - **Plain English:** When someone connects a platform with a large photo or video library for the first time, the system doesn't send one "go fetch these" instruction — it sends one instruction per photo, all at once. For a library of a few thousand images, that's a few thousand separate instructions fired off in one go, which slows down the sync job itself and floods the image-processing queue.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach (array_chunk($candidates, $chunk, true) as $slice) {
            $rows = DB::table('content.media_assets')
                ->whereIn('id', array_keys($slice))
                ->get(['id', 'storage_path', 'site_media_id', 'source_url', 'mirror_attempts']);

            foreach ($rows as $row) {
                ...
                MirrorMediaAssetJob::dispatch($userId, $assetId, $slice[$assetId]);
                $dispatched++;
            }
        }
        ```

## P3 — Nice to have

- [ ] **#W2-CACHE-4** · P3 — Google Business listing disconnect deletes every machine-sourced connection in a `foreach`, each firing its own cleanup cascade
    - **Where:** app/Observers/Core/IntegrationConnectionObserver.php:300-313 (`disconnectListingSourcedConnections`)
    - **Affects:** Business accounts disconnecting a Google Business listing that previously seeded other machine-created platform connections (fresha, facebook, instagram, doordash, uber_eats, etc.).
    - **Effort:** M (~2–4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Keep the per-model `->delete()` loop — it is deliberate (the code comment explains each child's own `deleted()` hooks for R2 media reclaim, ingest-source sync, and cache purge must run, and none of those hooks recurses into a listing delete).
        - If this becomes a measured problem, defer the cleanup side-effect jobs (media reclaim, ingest sync) behind a single batched job that iterates the already-resolved `$listingSourced` collection, rather than avoiding the per-row Eloquent delete itself.
    - **Technical:** Category (2) — write amplification, but bounded. One `google_business.listing` disconnect loads every other connection for the user and deletes each machine-sourced candidate individually, and each `$candidate->delete()` re-enters `IntegrationConnectionObserver::deleted()`'s own cascade (R2 media reclaim, ingest-source sync, `CloudflareCachePurgeJob` dispatch). This is real N-deletes-per-event fan-out, but N is bounded by the number of distinct platforms one business can plausibly have connected (typically single digits to low tens), not by an unbounded or attacker-influenced cardinality, and the code explicitly requires per-model deletion for correctness (bulk deletion would skip the required cleanup). The downstream cache-purge jobs already coalesce via `CloudflareCachePurgeJob`'s `ShouldBeUnique` + 35s window, so the primary remaining cost is the ingest-source/media-reclaim job count, not edge-purge API pressure. Given the small realistic N and the existing purge coalescing, this is hardening rather than an urgent fix.
    - **Plain English:** When a business disconnects their Google listing, the system also removes every other platform connection that listing had auto-created — but it does so one at a time, each with its own full cleanup routine (freeing storage, updating sync records, refreshing the public page). For a business with a handful of auto-connected platforms this is a small, deliberate list, but it's still worth batching the cleanup work into one job instead of firing a separate one per connection.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $listingSourced = IntegrationConnection::query()
            ->where('user_id', $connection->user_id)
            ->whereKeyNot($connection->getKey())
            ->get()
            ->filter(function (IntegrationConnection $candidate) use ($websiteWasMachine): bool {
                $source = CardPayload::fromArray((array) $candidate->payload)->source();

                return $source === 'google-business'
                    || ($websiteWasMachine && $source === 'website_import');
            });

        foreach ($listingSourced as $candidate) {
            $candidate->delete();
        }
        ```

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-1** · P2 — `supervisor-videos` runs one worker, so a long encode serialises every other user's video job
    - **Where:** config/horizon.php:323-334 (`defaults.supervisor-videos`)
    - **Affects:** All users uploading video — one long transcode blocks every other queued video job platform-wide.
    - **Effort:** L (~1-2d, requires box resize)
    - **What to do:**
        - Track actual `videos` queue wait times against the existing `redis_video:videos => 300` Horizon alert and confirm it's firing appropriately.
        - Do not raise `maxProcesses` until the box resize the file's own "KNOWN OVER-COMMIT" comment calls out lands — the permitted worker-memory ceiling is already ~194 MiB over the box.
    - **Technical:** `supervisor-videos` uses `balance => false`, `maxProcesses => 1` in every environment (`config/horizon.php:327`, unioned into all envs per the file's "Worker Lanes" docblock). `ProcessVideoVariantsJob` can run up to 3600s. The file's own "KNOWN OVER-COMMIT" note explains this ceiling is deliberate and cannot be raised without a box resize (an owner cost decision), so this is a documented, already-understood constraint rather than an oversight — but it remains a live noisy-neighbour risk today.
    - **Plain English:** There's one editing desk for every video. If one customer drops off a two-hour project, every other customer's ten-second clip waits behind it. The team already knows this and is waiting on a decision to buy a bigger desk.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        'supervisor-videos' => [
            'connection' => 'redis_video',
            'queue' => ['videos'],
            'balance' => false,
            'maxProcesses' => 1,
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-2** · P2 — `ContentFreshness::boostsForSite` loads every live content item for a user with no bound
    - **Where:** app/Services/Analytics/ContentFreshness.php:59-70 (`boostsForSite`)
    - **Affects:** Popularity-scoring runs (every 15 minutes) for content-heavy creators (large Instagram/TikTok/SoundCloud catalogs).
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Chunk the read with `chunkById()` keyed on `content.items.id`, or push the `MIN(published_from)` grouping into a keyset-paginated query.
        - Verify chunking doesn't break the per-item grouping already computed in SQL.
    - **Technical:** The query joins `content.items` to `content.f_published` and calls `->get([...])` with no limit for every user whose site is scored this run. For a prolific creator with a large connector-sourced catalog this materialises the full joined result in PHP on every 15-minute scoring tick.
    - **Plain English:** For one creator with a huge content library, this reads their entire catalog into memory every 15 minutes just to check what's new. It should page through a few hundred rows at a time instead of pulling the whole shelf onto the desk.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $rows = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('content.items as i')
            ->leftJoin('content.f_published as fp', 'fp.item_id', '=', 'i.id')
            ->where('i.user_id', $site->user_id)
            ->whereIn('i.kind', array_keys(ItemFamily::KIND_TO_FAMILY))
            ->whereNull('i.removed_at')
            ->groupBy('i.id', 'i.kind', 'i.first_seen_at')
            ->get([...]);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-3** · P2 — Auto-sync link resolution issues a fresh `IntegrationConnection` query per classified link
    - **Where:** app/Services/Platforms/Concerns/BuildsAutoSyncFindings.php:677-679, 796-799 (`resolveBookingLink`, `resolveSocialLink`)
    - **Affects:** Instagram bio-link auto-sync and Google Business discovered-link auto-sync — every link in a bio triggers its own DB round trip.
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Preload all live `IntegrationConnection` rows for the user once per sync call, keyed by `platform`/`routing_class`, and pass the map into both resolver methods.
        - Remove the per-link `->first()` calls.
    - **Technical:** `resolveSocialLink()` and `resolveBookingLink()` each run at least one `IntegrationConnection::query()->where('user_id', ...)->first()` per classified link, inside the auto-sync loop that processes every link found in a bio. A bio with N links produces N+ independent round trips on a write path that also performs outbound fetches per link.
    - **Plain English:** For every link found in someone's bio, the system separately asks the database "do we already have this one?" one at a time. Asking once for the whole list would be faster and put less strain on the database during a sign-up rush.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $existing = IntegrationConnection::query()
            ->where('user_id', $userId)->where('platform', $platform)
            ->first();
        ```

    - **What to do:**
        - Apply the same `isBotUserAgent()` guard `click`/`sectionSeen`/`itemSeen`/`actionSeen` already use, ahead of `buildEvent()`.
        - If the metrics team wants raw bot pageviews for a specific reason, gate that behind a config flag rather than leaving the endpoint permanently open.
    - **Technical:** The code's own comment states pageview "intentionally has NO bot filter and NO dedup (preserved)." Every other event type in this controller (`click`, `sectionSeen`, `sectionDwell`, `itemSeen`, `actionSeen`, `actionTap`, `ping`) checks `isBotUserAgent()` before ingesting. The pageview exemption reads as a deliberate metrics decision, not a scale-hardening one — a scripted caller that replays the pageview beacon (not a passive search-engine crawl of the rendered page, since this is a backend API a crawler wouldn't call directly) can mint unlimited rows with no bot gate and no dedup.
    - **Plain English:** Every kind of visit-tracking beacon on the site checks "is this a robot?" before counting it — except the most basic one, pageviews. If someone scripts a flood of fake page-visit calls, nothing currently filters them out before they reach the database.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // NOTE: pageview intentionally has NO bot filter and NO dedup (preserved). A bot
        // UA still records a pageview today; changing that is a separate metrics decision.
        $event = $this->buildEvent(...);
        $this->ingestor->ingest($event);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-6** · P2 — `ProcessImageVariantsJob` reads the entire original headshot into a PHP string before resizing
    - **Where:** app/Jobs/ProcessImageVariantsJob.php:255-256 (`storeHeadshotIcon`)
    - **Affects:** Headshot uploads on the `images` queue — a large original photo spikes worker memory during an otherwise-streamed pipeline.
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Replace `file_get_contents()` + `imagecreatefromstring()` with a format-specific `imagecreatefromjpeg`/`imagecreatefromwebp`/`imagecreatefrompng` call directly against the temp file path.
        - Keep everything else in the job's streaming pattern (it already avoids buffering the original everywhere else).
    - **Technical:** The rest of `runHandle()` deliberately streams the original to a local temp file via `readStream()`/`stream_copy_to_stream`. `storeHeadshotIcon()` breaks that discipline: `$raw = @file_get_contents($originalTmpPath); $src = @imagecreatefromstring($raw);` materialises the whole original as a string plus a decoded GD image simultaneously, just to produce a 192px icon. `supervisor-1`'s worker memory threshold is 256MB; a large phone photo pushes this job disproportionately close to that ceiling relative to the rest of the pipeline.
    - **Plain English:** The app is careful everywhere else to avoid loading a big photo fully into memory — except this one spot, where it reads the whole original just to make a tiny icon. A large photo here uses much more memory than the rest of the pipeline needs.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $raw = @file_get_contents($originalTmpPath);
        $src = $raw === false ? false : @imagecreatefromstring($raw);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-7** · P2 — Content-popularity rank hysteresis is an O(n²) bubble sort over every scored key
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/ComputeContentPopularityScores.php:887-899 (`rankWithHysteresis`)
    - **Affects:** The scheduled `analytics:compute-popularity` sweep (every 15 minutes) — a single site with an unusually large per-type item count degrades that site's own scoring pass.
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Replace the bubble traversal with a single stable sort that encodes the >10% swap-gate as a comparator, or bound the loop with an iteration cap per content type.
    - **Technical:** `rankWithHysteresis()` runs a `do { ... } while ($swapped)` bubble pass with an O(n²) worst case in the number of scored keys for one `(site, content_type)` pair. For a typical individual sitepage's per-type item count (tens to low hundreds), this is trivial; it only becomes a real cost for an outlier account with very large per-type catalogs (e.g. thousands of `listen_item`/`watch_item` rows from a music/video-heavy connector sync). `ActionScorer::rankWithHysteresis` (app/Services/Analytics/ActionScorer.php) uses the structurally identical pattern and should be fixed alongside this one.
    - **Plain English:** Sorting a leaderboard by repeatedly scanning the whole list every time two entries are out of order works fine for a normal-sized list, but for an outlier account with an unusually large catalog it can start to noticeably slow down that one site's scoring pass.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $n = count($keys);
        do {
            $swapped = false;
            for ($i = 0; $i < $n - 1; $i++) {
                $upper = $keys[$i];
                $lower = $keys[$i + 1];
                if ($blended[$lower] > $blended[$upper] * (1 + self::RANK_SWAP_THRESHOLD)) {
                    [$keys[$i], $keys[$i + 1]] = [$keys[$i + 1], $keys[$i]];
                    $swapped = true;
                }
            }
        } while ($swapped);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-8** · P2 — Per-site scoring re-queries `content_popularity_scores` once per content type instead of once per site
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/ComputeContentPopularityScores.php:796-799 (`blendAndRank`, called once per `content_type` from `computeForSite`)
    - **Affects:** The scheduled 15-minute popularity sweep, `chunkById(200)` sites per pass.
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Fetch all previous score rows for the site once (grouped by `content_type` in PHP), then pass the relevant subset into `blendAndRank` instead of querying per type.
    - **Technical:** `computeForSite()` iterates every scored content type for a site and calls `scoreAndRank`/`blendAndRank`, each of which independently queries `analytics.content_popularity_scores` filtered by `site_id` + `content_type`. A site with ~15 distinct content types (page/action/shop_product/menu_item/service/gallery_item/listen_item/watch_item/link_item/event_item/categories) issues ~15 queries per site per run; across a 200-site chunk that's ~3,000 queries before any other per-site work.
    - **Plain English:** For every customer's site, the scoring pass asks the database for last run's leaderboard one category at a time — 15 separate questions instead of one. Asking for the whole drawer at once would be far more efficient.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $previous = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.content_popularity_scores')
            ->where('site_id', $site->id)
            ->where('content_type', $contentType)
            ->get(['content_key', 'score', 'rank', 'computed_at']);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-9** · P2 — Cold (first-ever) connector projection scales linearly with catalog size and can approach its own documented lock-timeout bound
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php:96-124, 1153-1162 (`IDENTITY_LOCK_TIMEOUT_MS` docblock, `resolveItemsLocked`'s closing per-target update loop)
    - **Affects:** A user's very first connector sync (e.g. a musician connecting Spotify/SoundCloud with a large back catalog) — held under the `identity:{user_id}:{kind}` advisory lock for the whole resolve+write.
    - **Effort:** L (~1-2d) — the team's own docblock names the exact fix.
    - **What to do:**
        - When this becomes an observed problem (per the code's own stated trigger), batch `createItem()`/the anchor insert and the closing `content.source_items` update across groups instead of one `UPDATE ... WHERE id IN (...)` per resolved target.
        - Until then, no action needed — this is a documented, deliberately-deferred trade-off, not an oversight.
    - **Technical:** The class's own docblock states this was MEASURED on 2026-08-19: 120ms steady-state vs. 2,267ms for a cold pass minting 2,000 items, "roughly linear in unbound coords, so a first-ever projection of a ~4,500-item catalogue would sit on this [5000ms] bound." The closing write in `resolveItemsLocked()` still issues one `UPDATE content.source_items` per distinct resolved target (`foreach ($idsByTarget as $target => $ids) { ...->update(...); }`), and the earlier reads (`content.source_items`, `content.identity_keys` for the user+kind) are similarly unbounded on a cold pass — the #CACHE-2/#CACHE-4 narrowing that already shipped only reduces cost for *incremental* (steady-state) runs, not the first sync. The team explicitly names the timeout-then-retry as "the intended failure" today and defers batching until it becomes a real complaint — but on the *connector* lane (not the interactive dashboard lane) a timeout here throws inside `projectStream()`, which `RunExecutor` converts into a `severity='critical'` `ingest.anomalies` row that pages on-call once per failure, not a quiet retry. At pilot launch, an early user with a large existing catalog is a plausible way to hit this for the first time.
    - **Plain English:** Importing someone's very large back-catalogue for the first time can take long enough to trip the system's own 5-second safety timer, because it's still doing one small database update per new item rather than one big batch update. The team already measured this and decided not to fix it until it actually happens to a real user — this flags that a real user with a large catalog is exactly the kind of account expected during pilot onboarding.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // MEASURED against a real Postgres on 2026-08-19, 2,000 live source items of one kind for
        // one user: 120ms for a steady-state pass ... and 2,267ms for the cold pass that mints an item and
        // an anchor for all 2,000 at once. The cold figure is the one to watch: it is roughly linear
        // in unbound coords, so a first-ever projection of a ~4,500-item catalogue would sit on this
        // bound
        ```
        ```php
        foreach ($idsByTarget as $target => $ids) {
            DB::table('content.source_items')->whereIn('id', $ids)->update(['item_id' => $target]);
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-10** · P2 — Cold catalog cache-refresh mints one public URL slug per item instead of batching, compounding the same cold-sync cost
    - **Where:** app/Ingest/Projection/ProjectionWriter.php:3147-3155 (`refreshItemCaches`)
    - **Affects:** The same first-ever connector sync as #SCALE-9 — a large new catalog issues one `ensureCurrent()` call (slug lookup + possible insert) per new slugged item.
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Batch slug minting for newly-slugged headlines within the batch loop rather than calling `ensureCurrent()` per item, or move first-sync slug minting to a follow-up queued step.
        - Preserve the collision-suffix/rename-back semantics `ensureCurrent()` owns — the method's own docblock warns against re-implementing that logic inline.
    - **Technical:** `refreshItemCaches()` was already rewritten to batch 18 of its 19 read/write operations across chunks (its own docblock: "this used to be 20 queries PER ITEM ... Rewritten to batch"). The one operation left unbatched is `ensureCurrent()` inside the per-item `foreach ($batch as $itemId)` loop — every new or headline-changed slugged item still triggers its own slug-table round trip. On a cold sync where every item is new, this is N slug operations for N items, in the same code path as #SCALE-9.
    - **Plain English:** After a big import, each new item needs a public web address. The system now batches almost everything about that process — except minting the actual address, which still happens one item at a time.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if ($headline !== null && $headline !== ''
            && in_array((string) $row->kind, ContentItemSlugAllocator::SLUGGED_KINDS, true)
            && ($liveSlugs[$itemId] ?? null) !== $this->slugs->baseSlug((string) $headline, (string) $itemId)) {
            try {
                $this->slugs->ensureCurrent((string) $row->user_id, (string) $itemId, (string) $headline);
            } catch (\Throwable $e) {
                report($e);
            }
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-13** · P3 — `ActionScorer`'s per-site aggregate reads are bounded by time window but not by the current candidate set
    - **Where:** app/Services/Analytics/ActionScorer.php:232-247 (`aggregate`), :251-263 (`previousRows`)
    - **Affects:** Per-site "smart action" scoring (every 15 minutes) for sites with a large historical action-id corpus.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5-1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Once `ActionCandidates::forSite()` has resolved the live candidate set, add `whereIn('action_id'/'content_key', $candidateIds)` to both queries.
    - **Technical:** Both queries are already bounded (`aggregate()` by `ScoringWindow::since()` — 120 days — per the in-code `SCALE-3` fix comment; `previousRows()` by `site_id` + `content_type`), but neither is narrowed to the action IDs currently being ranked, so a site with a large historical action corpus (config/priors changed over time, retired actions never purged) inflates both reads with rows the ranking pass doesn't use.
    - **Plain English:** These two queries already avoid scanning *all* history, but they still pull every historical action for the site rather than just the ones currently on the menu.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        DB::connection('pgsql')->table('analytics.action_events')
            ->where('site_id', $site->id)
            ->where('occurred_at', '>=', ScoringWindow::since())
            ->selectRaw("action_id, event, {$day} as day, COUNT(DISTINCT COALESCE(session_id, visitor_id, id)) as sessions")
            ->groupByRaw("action_id, event, {$day}")
            ->get()
        ```

    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/BackfillStorefrontFaviconsCommand.php:43-60 (`handle`)

    - **Effort:** S (~0.5-1h)
    - **What to do:**

This is a manually-invoked, operator-paced maintenance command against a currently small table (per the file's own comment, 21-33 candidate rows on dev), so the practical impact today is minimal.
    - **Plain English:** The tool has a "stop after N" switch, but it still pulls every candidate into memory first before honouring it — like asking a librarian for the first ten pages but they wheel over the whole shelf anyway.

        ```php
            ->join('content.collections as c', 'c.id', '=', 's.collection_id')
            ->where('c.kind', 'storefront')
            ->whereNull('s.favicon_url')->whereNull('s.logo_url')->where('s.is_individual', false)
            ->select('s.collection_id', 'c.user_id')->orderBy('s.collection_id')->get();

        if ($limit > 0) {
            $candidates = $candidates->take($limit);
        }
        ```

        - Batch-load handles with one `whereIn('id', $userIds)` before mapping the report rows.
    - **Technical:** For every build row in the report window, the command does `User::query()->whereKey($b->user_id)->value('handle_lc')`, one query per row.
    - **Plain English:** For every build listed in the final report, the tool makes a separate database trip to look up that person's handle, instead of asking for the whole list at once.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $rows = PreAccountBuild::query()
            ->where('created_at', '>=', $since)
            ->orderBy('created_at')
            ->get()
            ->map(fn (PreAccountBuild $b) => [
                User::query()->whereKey($b->user_id)->value('handle_lc') ?? '(gone)',
                ...
            ])
            ->all();
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-23** · P3 — `builds:await`'s final report loads the full `--since` window into memory with no cap
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/BuildsAwaitCommand.php (final report query)
    - **Affects:** CLI tooling memory usage for a wide manual `--since` range.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5-1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a sane upper bound or chunk the report query when `--since` spans a large window.
    - **Technical:** The default window is 15 minutes, but `--since` accepts any date with no validation or cap, so an operator passing a wide range loads every matching row into memory before formatting the report.
    - **Plain English:** The report tool can be told to look back over any date range, and it always pulls the whole thing into memory first rather than paging through it.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $rows = PreAccountBuild::query()
            ->where('created_at', '>=', $since)
            ->orderBy('created_at')
            ->get()
            ->map(...)
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-24** · P3 — `fleet:rebuild` issues multiple separate queries per handle instead of batching
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/FleetRebuildCommand.php (per-handle loop)
    - **Affects:** CLI rebuild tooling — slower runs when passed many handles.
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Batch-load `User`/`PreAccountBuild`/`Site`/`Workplace` via `whereIn('handle_lc', $handles)` with eager loads, then iterate in memory.
    - **Technical:** For each supplied handle the command runs separate `User`, `PreAccountBuild`, `Site`, and `Workplace` queries — four round trips per handle.
    - **Plain English:** Asking this tool to rebuild 200 accounts means looking each one up in four different filing cabinets individually, rather than pulling four big lists once.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        foreach ($this->argument('handles') as $handle) {
            $user = User::query()->where('handle_lc', $handle)->first();
            $build = PreAccountBuild::query()->where('user_id', $user->id)->orderByDesc('created_at')->first();
            $siteId = Site::query()->where('user_id', $user->id)->value('id');
            $fullName = $siteId ? Workplace::query()->where('site_id', $siteId)->value('name') : null;
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-25** · P3 — `fleet:verify` issues multiple separate queries per handle, and its `--http` mode has no throttle
    - **Where:** app/Console/Commands/FleetVerifyCommand.php (per-handle loop; `--http` branch)
    - **Affects:** CLI verification tooling — slower runs over many handles; unthrottled requests to `*.partna.au` under `--http`.
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Batch-load `User`/`PreAccountBuild`/`Site`/`SiteMedia`/`Workplace`/`Block` by `whereIn` across the supplied handles.
        - Add a small delay between requests when `--http` is passed.
    - **Technical:** Same per-handle query fan-out pattern as #SCALE-24 (`FleetRebuildCommand`), plus an opt-in `--http` mode that does a synchronous `Http::timeout(10)->get("https://{$handle}.partna.au")` per handle with no delay or concurrency cap. `--http` is opt-in, so this only fires when an operator explicitly requests it.
    - **Plain English:** This verification tool visits each account's database records — and, if asked, each account's live web page — one at a time rather than in efficient batches.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        $user = User::query()->where('handle_lc', $handle)->first();
        $build = PreAccountBuild::query()->where('user_id', $user->id)->orderByDesc('created_at')->first();
        $site = Site::query()->where('user_id', $user->id)->first();
        ```
        ```php
        if ($this->option('http')) {
            try {
                $http = (string) Http::timeout(10)->get("https://{$handle}.partna.au")->status();
            } catch (\Throwable) {
                $http = 'ERR';
            }
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-26** · P3 — Analytics ingest re-reads `core.users` on every request to an unpublished site
    - **Where:** app/Http/Controllers/Api/PublicSite/AnalyticsController.php:404-427 (`resolvePublishedSite`)
    - **Affects:** Analytics ingest for unclaimed pre-account demo sites only — published sites (the common case) never hit this branch.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5-1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Fold the owner's `status` into the same site-resolution query (a join or subselect) rather than a second round trip.
    - **Technical:** This check was added 2026-08-27 (dated one day before this audit) to correctly allow analytics ingest for renderable-but-unpublished pre-account demo sites. It only runs when `$site->is_published` is false, so it does not affect the common case (published sites, which is what a viral page typically is) — the exposure is narrower than "every request," but a pre-account demo site going viral pre-claim is an explicitly supported product scenario per the platform's own pre-account doctrine.
    - **Plain English:** For one specific kind of visitor — someone viewing a not-yet-claimed demo site — the app checks the database twice instead of once before recording their visit. Regular published sites aren't affected.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        if (! $site->is_published
            && User::query()->whereKey($site->user_id)->value('status') !== 'unclaimed') {
            $error = $this->error('Site not found', 404);
            return null;
        }
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-27** · P3 — `PoolResolver`'s curation reads grow unbounded with a site's lifetime, already flagged once and still unfixed
    - **Where:** app/Site/Pools/PoolResolver.php:118-133, 238-247, 318-333 (`hasSelection`, `plan`, `preloadCuration`)
    - **Affects:** Public sitepage payload builds, presence probes, and the dashboard pool page — worse for long-lived sites with a large excluded-item history.
    - **Effort:** M (~2-4h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add a bounded/streaming read or an archival path for excluded curation rows, or answer `hasSelection()` via an indexed `EXISTS` query instead of materialising the full row set.
    - **Technical:** The code's own in-place comment (`SCALE-13`) already documents this exact gap — "excluded rows have no pruning path, so a full-row select grows unbounded with a site's lifetime for no benefit" — but it has not been remediated; all three methods still run unbounded `->get(['section_id', 'item_id', 'state', 'sort_key'])` reads against `site.section_items`.
    - **Plain English:** A site's exclusion list is kept forever and re-read in full on every relevant page build, even though old excluded items serve no purpose once they're excluded. The team already flagged this once — it's still true.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        // SCALE-13: `id`/`created_at` are read nowhere downstream, and excluded
        // rows have no pruning path, so a full-row select grows unbounded with
        // a site's lifetime for no benefit.
        $curation = DB::connection('pgsql')->table('site.section_items')
            ->where('section_id', $section->id)
            ->get(['section_id', 'item_id', 'state', 'sort_key']);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-28** · P3 — `BuildSiteDocumentJob` is dispatched to the unnamed `default` queue instead of a domain queue
    - **Where:** app/Site/Documents/BuildState.php:55-56 (`bump`)
    - **Affects:** Naming/observability consistency — every other queue in this system is domain-named.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5-1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `->onQueue('cache-warm')` (the queue this system already names for post-write cache/document rebuilds) for consistency with the rest of the codebase's explicit queue routing.
    - **Technical:** `BuildSiteDocumentJob` has no `$queue` property or `onQueue()` call, so it lands on `default`, which is unusual for this codebase — every other job in the system uses an explicit domain queue name. Note this is **not** currently a priority regression: `default` sits second in `supervisor-1`'s strict-priority order (`[ModerationQueue::HIGH, 'default', 'cloudflare', 'cache-warm', ...]`), ahead of `cache-warm` — so moving it to `cache-warm` would technically *lower* its effective priority. Given the job already has `ShouldBeUnique` coalescing, a short dispatch delay, and a 5-minute sweeper safety net, this is a naming/observability cleanup rather than an active throughput problem.
    - **Plain English:** This job is one of the very few in the codebase that doesn't get its own named lane — it rides on the generic "default" line. It's not currently causing a slowdown (that lane is actually fast), but it makes the system harder to reason about at a glance.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        BuildSiteDocumentJob::dispatch($siteId)
            ->delay(self::EVENT_BUILD_DELAY_SECONDS);
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCALE-29** · P3 — Migration combined an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`-holding `ALTER ... SET DEFAULT` with a full-table `UPDATE` in one transaction
    - **Where:** supabase/migrations/20260827120000_sites_architecture_default_scroll.sql:23-33
    - **Affects:** Historical only — this migration already ran (2026-08-27) against a table the project's own status notes describe as carrying zero real customer rows at that time. Documented here as a pattern to avoid in future migrations against `site.sites` once it holds real traffic.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5-1h, for the *pattern*, not this file)
    - **What to do:**
        - For any *future* `ALTER TABLE ... SET DEFAULT` + backfill against a hot table, split the metadata-only `ALTER` into its own committed transaction before running the row backfill, so the `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock is released before the (potentially slower) `UPDATE` runs.
        - No action needed on this specific file — it already ran.
    - **Technical:** `ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN SET DEFAULT` acquires `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, held until `COMMIT`. Because the `UPDATE` runs in the same transaction, every reader of `site.sites` (the platform's hottest table) would have blocked for the full duration of the `UPDATE`. The migration's own comment states the table was "small at this stage" — consistent with `core.users = 0` in production at the time — so the real-world impact was negligible; this is a pattern note, not an active incident.
    - **Plain English:** This particular database change already ran safely, back when the table was still nearly empty. It's flagged here only so the same shortcut isn't taken later once that table has real customer data in it — the site page would then briefly stop responding to anyone during a change like this.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```sql
        BEGIN;
        SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s';
        SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '30s';
        ALTER TABLE "site"."sites" ALTER COLUMN architecture_id SET DEFAULT 'scroll';
        UPDATE "site"."sites" SET architecture_id = 'scroll'
         WHERE architecture_id IS DISTINCT FROM 'scroll';
        COMMIT;
        ```

    - **Why grouped:** All in `app/Services/Analytics/*` + `ComputeContentPopularityScores` — the same scoring-pipeline subsystem, same "unbounded/O(n²) at outlier scale" root cause.
    - **Model:** Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

    - **Why grouped:** Both in `ProjectionWriter.php`'s cold-projection path; same root cause (first-sync cost scales linearly with catalog size), same file, same fix pattern (batch the per-item writes).
    - **Model:** Plan: Opus (touches the identity/anchor resolver — genuinely gnarly) · Implement: Opus · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 3 — Platform auto-sync N+1 preloads:** #SCALE-3, #SCALE-4

- **Bundle 4 — Ops CLI tooling batching:** #SCALE-11, #SCALE-17, #SCALE-22, #SCALE-23, #SCALE-24, #SCALE-25
    - **Why grouped:** All internal maintenance/verification commands with the same "batch instead of per-row query" fix; none touch the public wire or auth.
    - **Model:** Plan: Opus · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 5 — Dev-insights & content-library bounding:** #SCALE-19, #SCALE-20, #SCALE-21
    - **Why grouped:** Same subsystem class (per-user dashboard endpoints), same fix pattern (pagination/time-bound).
    - **Model:** Plan: Sonnet · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 6 — ProcessImageVariantsJob cleanup:** #SCALE-6, #SCALE-12
    - **Why grouped:** Same file, same job, unrelated-but-adjacent fixes that touch the same `runHandle()` method.
    - **Model:** Plan: Sonnet · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

- **Bundle 7 — Menu/link scan bounding:** #SCALE-14, #SCALE-15
    - **Why grouped:** Same "unbounded per-account read/loop" pattern in the platform content-import lane.
    - **Model:** Plan: Sonnet · Implement: Sonnet · Review: Sonnet.

## Standalone — do NOT bundle

- **#SCALE-1 — `supervisor-videos` single-worker noisy-neighbour risk** · standalone: touches `config/horizon.php`, a shared Horizon topology file where the codebase's own docblocks warn against uncoordinated changes (the 2026-07-22 OOM-loop history); also gated on an infra/cost decision, not a code fix.
- **#SCALE-5 — Pageview ingest has no bot filter** · standalone: touches the public analytics ingest security/abuse surface directly.
- **#SCALE-26 — Extra `core.users` read on unpublished-site analytics** · standalone: touches the same public analytics resolution path added 2026-08-27 for the pre-account visibility doctrine — any change here needs to be checked against `tests/Feature/PreAccount/UnclaimedGatingTest.php`.
- **#SCALE-27 — `PoolResolver` unbounded curation reads** · standalone: touches the public sitepage's hottest resolution path (`SitepageDataResolverService` presence probe) — any change needs its own review against public-payload correctness, not bundled with unrelated work.
- **#SCALE-28 — `BuildSiteDocumentJob` queue naming** · standalone: a one-line change with outsized blast-radius risk (queue placement interacts with Horizon's documented `balance => false` priority ordering and the box's memory-overcommit ceiling) — verify against `HorizonQueueCoverageTest` before merging.
- **#SCALE-29 — Migration lock-scoping pattern note** · standalone: any `supabase/migrations/*.sql` change always requires its own plan + sign-off per the repo's migration doctrine, even though this specific file is historical/informational only.

# Schema / RLS / search_path Audit — 2026-08-28

**Branch:** development
**Lens:** Schema / RLS / search_path — database-side correctness, constraint coverage, migration safety
**Pipeline:** scan-tier draft by `deepseek-v4-pro`, adjudicated by `claude-opus-4.6`
**Source files audited:**
- supabase/migrations/20260827072000_event_item_family.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260827120000_sites_architecture_default_scroll.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260828010000_pre_account_builds_published_by_claim.sql
- supabase/migrations/20260828030000_f_review_staff_name.sql
- app/Models/BaseModel.php
- app/Models/Core/Site/Site.php
- app/Models/Core/Site/SiteMedia.php
- app/Models/Core/User/PreAccountBuild.php

## Progress

- P0 Blockers: 0 of 0 complete
- P1 High: 0 of 0 complete
- P2 Medium: 0 of 1 complete
- P3 Low: 0 of 3 complete

---

## P2 — Should fix

- [ ] **#W2-SCHEMA-1** · P2 — `site.sites.settings` JSONB has no DB-level guard against reintroducing the stripped `design` key
    - **Where:** app/Models/Core/Site/Site.php:25
    - **Affects:** Site design storage; a raw `DB::table()` write, an unmigrated legacy queued job, or a manual data fix could reintroduce `settings.design.*` after the architecture-system cleanup moved design vars to `site.design_kits`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `ALTER TABLE site.sites ADD CONSTRAINT sites_settings_no_design_key_check CHECK (NOT (settings ? 'design')) NOT VALID;` in a new migration, following the `NOT VALID` + separate-transaction `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` split (`site.sites` is a `HOT_TABLES` entry — wrap both windows in `SET LOCAL lock_timeout`/`statement_timeout`, per `docs/migration-guidelines.md` §Lock and statement timeouts).
        - Confirm no live row currently has a `design` key before validating (should be zero post-cleanup, but verify against dev before running `VALIDATE`).
        - Add a `tests/Schema` regression guard alongside the existing architecture/design-kit schema tests asserting the CHECK exists and rejects a `design` key.
    - **Technical:** (Category 10 — JSONB design) `UpdateSiteRequest` already rejects `settings.design.*` at the Form Request layer (`'settings.design' => ['prohibited']`), but that is an application-layer opt-out only — the JSONB column itself has no shape enforcement. Per the lens's own explicit callout for this column ("Any code path that writes `settings.design.*` back into the column is a finding. Flag JSONB shapes that could reintroduce stripped keys."), a raw write or an un-migrated background job bypassing the Form Request can silently reintroduce the retired shape. The canonical fix is a JSONB-existence `CHECK` added with the `NOT VALID + VALIDATE` split, matching the pattern already established for `sites_architecture_id_check` and `sites_shop_link_mode_check` on this same table.
    - **Plain English:** The team moved design blueprints out of a shared filing folder into their own dedicated drawer, and put a note on the shared folder saying "don't file design blueprints here anymore." The note only works for people who read it — the folder itself will still physically accept those papers if someone (or some old automated process) files them directly. Adding a rule at the storage level means the folder itself refuses those papers, so an old script or a manual database fix can't quietly bring back the retired system.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        * @property array<string, mixed> $settings Free-form bag; the 5 FOUND-16 keys (show_branding, charlie_enabled, services_auto_sync_enabled, booking_mode, manual_booking_url) were promoted to real columns and are re-merged at read time (SiteResource) rather than read from here. Known remaining keys: privacy, smart_page_order, manual_page_order, actions, pool_order, pool_locks, display_gallery_page. settings.design.* is REJECTED on write (site.design_kits is the design-var store).
        ```

## P3 — Nice to have

- [ ] **#W2-SCHEMA-2** · P3 — `core.pre_account_builds.failure_code` is an enum-like TEXT column without a DB CHECK
    - **Where:** app/Models/Core/User/PreAccountBuild.php:23,61
    - **Affects:** Pre-account build failure triage/dashboards; a raw write could persist a failure code outside the documented `FAILURE_*` vocabulary.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Add `CHECK (failure_code IS NULL OR failure_code IN ('source_not_found', 'scrape_failed', 'stuck_timeout'))`, `NOT VALID` first, `VALIDATE` in a following transaction.
        - Add a schema regression guard mirroring the house `sites_architecture_id_check` style test.
    - **Technical:** (Category 3 — constraint coverage) `source_type`, `built_via`, and `build_state` on this same table carry real DB CHECKs, but `failure_code` is explicitly documented twice in the model as "not DB-CHECK-enforced, app-level vocabulary only" — a deliberate, low-stakes design choice (the column is diagnostic, populated only when `build_state = 'failed'`, and read by staff triage tooling, not by any security- or money-relevant path). Tiered P3 rather than P2 because the blast radius is a mis-labeled triage dashboard entry, not a functional or security defect — consistent with how this codebase has previously dispositioned structurally identical "documented no-DB-CHECK" vocabulary columns (e.g. `analytics.action_events.action_id`) as low-priority hardening rather than urgent gaps.
    - **Plain English:** The system has an official list of reasons a pre-account build can fail, written down in the code, but the database itself doesn't enforce that list. A typo written directly into the database would be silently accepted and could confuse a support dashboard later — low-stakes, but worth tidying up the same way the platform already enforces its other status lists.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```php
        @property string|null $failure_code One of FAILURE_* (e.g. FAILURE_SOURCE_NOT_FOUND, FAILURE_SCRAPE_FAILED) when build_state is 'failed' — not DB-CHECK-enforced, app-level vocabulary only.
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCHEMA-3** · P3 — `core.pre_account_builds` DDL omits the standard lock/statement timeout guard used by sibling migrations
    - **Where:** supabase/migrations/20260828010000_pre_account_builds_published_by_claim.sql:16-21
    - **Affects:** Deploy safety for the claim/release flow if this `ALTER TABLE` ever contends with a long-running transaction on `core.pre_account_builds`.
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the `ALTER TABLE` with `SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s'; SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '10s';` inside the existing `BEGIN`/`COMMIT`, matching `20260827072000_event_item_family.sql` and `20260827120000_sites_architecture_default_scroll.sql` in the same date range.
        - Consider whether `core.pre_account_builds` should be added to `HOT_TABLES` in `scripts/guard-no-unsafe-migrations.php` given it is read/written on the claim/release path.
    - **Technical:** (Category 6 — migration safety under load) `core.pre_account_builds` is not in the CI-enforced `HOT_TABLES` list (`scripts/guard-no-unsafe-migrations.php`: `['site.design_kits', 'site.sites', 'site.blocks', 'core.users']`), so `guard:no-unsafe-migrations` Check 5 does not fire here — this is not a CI failure. Practical risk is low: `ADD COLUMN ... NOT NULL DEFAULT false` with a constant default is a metadata-only operation on Postgres 11+, no full-table rewrite. The finding is a consistency/hardening gap, not a correctness defect: two sibling migrations landing the same day in the same table family (`20260827072000`, `20260827120000`) both apply the timeout guard as a matter of course, and this file (touching a table that is genuinely live during the claim/release flow per T28's own commit message) does not.
    - **Plain English:** Two other recent database changes in this same batch of work included a safety timer so a stuck update wouldn't accidentally freeze the app during deploy. This one skipped that timer. The actual job here is small and low-risk, but for consistency it should carry the same two-second safety timer as its neighbors.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```sql
        BEGIN;

        ALTER TABLE core.pre_account_builds
            ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS published_by_claim boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false;

        COMMIT;
        ```

- [ ] **#W2-SCHEMA-4** · P3 — `content.f_review` DDL omits the standard lock/statement timeout guard, and the table is not in the CI hot-table allowlist despite being read on the public sitepage path
    - **Where:** supabase/migrations/20260828030000_f_review_staff_name.sql:9
    - **Affects:** Deploy safety for the reviews pool on public sitepage resolution, which reads `content.f_review` per `PoolResolver::` person-scoping (`app/Site/Pools/PoolResolver.php`).
    - **Effort:** S (~0.5–1h)
    - **What to do:**
        - Wrap the `ALTER TABLE` in `BEGIN; SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s'; SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '10s'; ...; COMMIT;`, matching the pattern already used elsewhere in this migration batch.
        - Evaluate adding `content.f_review` to `HOT_TABLES` in `scripts/guard-no-unsafe-migrations.php` — it is queried by `PoolResolver` on the public sitepage's reviews pool, which `CLAUDE.md` names as the hottest path on the platform, but the guard's current four-table allowlist predates the content-pool convergence and doesn't cover it.
    - **Technical:** (Category 6 — migration safety under load) The `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS staff_name text NULL` is itself metadata-only (nullable column, no rewrite), so the immediate risk from this specific migration is minimal. The more durable gap is that `content.f_review` sits outside the CI-enforced `HOT_TABLES` list even though it is read on the public sitepage resolution path (`app/Site/Pools/PoolResolver.php:1502`, person-scoped reviews, landed the same day as this migration per commit `90a97734f`) — a future DDL against this table (e.g. an index change) would not be caught by `guard:no-unsafe-migrations` Check 5 the way `site.sites` or `site.blocks` changes are.
    - **Plain English:** This column addition itself is a quick, low-risk job. But the bigger point is that this table is actually read every time a visitor views someone's public review section — and it isn't on the platform's list of "handle with extra care" tables the way the site's core tables are. That list should probably grow to match how the product has grown.
    - **Evidence:**
        ```sql
        ALTER TABLE "content"."f_review" ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS "staff_name" text NULL;
        ```

## Suggested Bundled Sessions

None — every surviving finding requires a `supabase/migrations/` change (a new CHECK constraint or a DDL rewrite), which the fix-flow blocker gate always runs standalone.

## Standalone — do NOT bundle

- **#SCHEMA-1 — `site.sites.settings` JSONB design-key guard** · DB migration (new CHECK constraint on a `HOT_TABLES` table).
- **#SCHEMA-2 — `failure_code` CHECK constraint** · DB migration (new CHECK constraint).

# Caching: Gold-Standard Adherence Audit — 2026-08-28

**Branch:** development
**Lens:** Caching: gold-standard adherence audit — hunts every cache read/write against the `CacheLockService`/`SiteCacheService`/`UserCacheService` gold standard (single-flight lock, TTL jitter, SWR, push-invalidation, version tokens, lock/connection hygiene, bounded TTLs, centralised key generation, cache-vs-DB race safety, no error-swallowing read-throughs).
**Pipeline:** scan-tier draft by `deepseek-v4-pro`, adjudicated by `claude-opus-4.6`
**Source files audited:**
- app/Routing/ConnectionPayload.php
- app/Routing/ShortLinkExpander.php
- app/Routing/SourceReconciler.php
- app/Routing/SuggestionApplier.php
- app/Services/Accounts/AccountCapabilities.php
- app/Services/Accounts/AccountCapabilitySet.php
- app/Services/PublicSite/IndividualProfilePayloadBuilder.php
- app/Services/Site/SectionBlockProvisioner.php
- app/Services/User/SiteProvisioningService.php
- app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Analytics/DevInsightsController.php
- app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/Content/ContentController.php
- app/Http/Controllers/Api/User/SiteManagement/UserSectionBlockController.php
